## INTRODUCTION

In response to your desire to constantly improve your formation and your eagerness to learn, the Work offers you, in a way and manner required by each one’s personal circumstances, accurate knowledge of the Church’s dogmatic and moral teaching, of Sacred Scripture and liturgy, Church law and history. Thus you can more easily raise human areas of knowledge to the supernatural order and convert them into apostolic instruments.1 All the formation we receive in the Work, and pass on to others through our apostolic activity, has a very clear aim: to sanctify us and make us God’s instruments, as good stewards of God’s varied grace.2 Thus we must go beyond simply knowing doctrine, or assenting intellectually to the truths of faith that are the foundation of our Christian life. Christ’s gospel “must not just be known; it must also be accepted with our whole heart and mind, since it is Christ himself who is speaking. As St Peter exclaimed after the mysterious discourse in Capharnaum, only Jesus has words of eternal life3.”4 Along with accepting divine truth, faith in Christ involves practical consequences, a new way of living. For what does it profit, my brethren, if a man says he has faith but has not works? ... But someone will say: “You have faith and I have works.” Show me your faith apart from

1 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 49. 2 1 Pet 4:10. 3 John 6:68. 4 Paul VI, Address, 15 March 1967.

8 On Christian life

your works, and I by my works will show you my faith.5 It is an error to separate Faith from life, as if neither sphere concerned the other except by accident. Being a Christian means believing in Christ, imitating Christ, living with Christ. Sanctity is a personal encounter of each soul with God, and each on his part must do all he can with deeds, with personal effort. I’ll tell you, along with James in his epistle: sic et fides, si non habeat opera, mortua est in semetipsa (Jas 2:17). Faith, then, if it has no works, is dead. It’s useless! Our Lord points out the close relationship between doctrine and life when he says: If you know these things, blessed are you if you do them.6 Authentic faith is not mere external observance, but the complete and total assent, in body and soul, to what God reveals. Faith must lead to “Christian life that is full, strong, firm and deep. This life is nourished by Christ’s doctrine and sacraments, sustained by prayer and meditation, and kept alive by a generous struggle against anything that could stifle, harm or weaken it.”7 So it is not surprising that a crisis in the truths of faith produces erroneous conclusions in moral theology and aberrations in everyday life, or that mistaken conduct stemming from yielding to disordered passions leads to rationalizing this way of living that a well-formed conscience would reject. Sadly enough, this is the behaviour we see in many people, who have lost the Christian meaning of their lives. Our Father insistently put us on guard: One sees confusion, lack of faith, people going astray in the ranks of God’s army on earth. It is sad to see all that one sees, all the moral laxity; it is sad to hear all that one hears from the lips of not a few priests and religious, all that is written by so many false theologians. And the weakness of those entrusted

5 Jas 2:14-18. 6 John 13:17. 7 Pius XII, Address, 12 April 1941.

Introduction 9

with nourishing the sheep of the flock of Christ, who remain silent, in spite of seeing the Church so painfully riddled with errors, aberrations and heresies.8 It is well known that in many places the sense of sin has been lost. People then talk about “liberation”, but they forget that Christ came with the truth to free us from sin and the chains of the devil: veritas liberabit vos (John 8:32). People want freedom from poverty, suffering, sickness, old age, servitude – from everything, but not from sin. Then they give free rein to their passions and end up living like animals. They seek only to satisfy their desire for riches, their pride, their quest for power, their sensuality; they want to impose themselves on others, not with the use of their reason, but with force and deceit. It’s infra-human. Authentic Christian life, in contrast, involves a profound act of faith, manifesting the authenticity and strength of one’s belief. “Faith is the principle of human salvation,”9 “the foundation and root of all justification”10 and of a holy life. “Only the light of faith and meditation of the Word of God can enable us to find everywhere and always the God in whom we live and exist;11 only thus can we seek his will in everything, see Christ in all men, acquaintance or stranger, and make sound judgments on the true meaning and value of temporal realities both in themselves and in relation to man’s end.”12 Faith is meant to yield fruit: “For faith, unless hope and charity be added to it, neither unites one perfectly with Christ, nor makes one a living member of his Body. Thus it is most truly said that faith without works is dead13 and of no profit.”14 “The truths of faith and moral

8 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 102. 9 St Fulgentius, De Fide ad Petrum, 1. 10 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 8, Dz 801 (1532). 11 Acts 17:28. 12 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 4. 13 Jas 2:17.

10 On Christian life

norms are inseparable in an authentic and sincere Christian, since knowledge of divine things is the necessary foundation for a good life, pointing out and illuminating the path to be followed.”15 May we therefore strive to always grow in our knowledge of the doctrinal foundations of Christian morality, so as to forge a deeper unity of life. And let us try to move other souls, awakening in their conscience the obligation to try to live as coherent Catholics.16 These pages attempt to present clearly and simply the fundamental principles governing human activity, under the prudent and sure guidance of the Magisterium. The Church’s teaching authority enjoys in this area the same role and prerogatives it has in defining and explaining truths of faith: it authentically and infallibly interprets God’s revealed word and the precepts of natural law. We have to teach others with our words and our life, especially now when there is wide-spread laxity and unconditional surrender. Teach, teach, teach. Our entire work is a great catechesis. It doesn’t matter if others see your defects and mine. With our personal defects and with our errors, we have the duty to teach the pathways of God without error and without defect. And we must teach them as Christ taught: first – in spite of our shortcomings and evident mistakes – with the testimony of our life. Then, with our words, with doctrine, because we don’t want to produce a counterfeit Christ. We want to bring forth, with his grace, an exact copy so we can spread far and wide the Redeemer’s good news.17

14 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1531). 15 Pius XII, Address, 12 April 1941. 16 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1942, 17. 17 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 136.

## FOUNDATIONS OF THE MORAL ORDER {#cuaderno.2.1}

Being a Christian is not merely a way to personal contentment; it implies a mission ... Our Lord invites all Christians to be salt and light for the world. Echoing that commandment and the Old Testament, St Peter spells out its implications: You are a chosen race, a royal priesthood, a holy nation, God’s own people, that you may declare the wonderful deeds of him who called you out of darkness into his marvellous light (1 Pet 2:9). Being a Christian is not something incidental; it is a divine reality that takes root deep in our lives. It gives us clear vision and strengthens our will to act as God wants. So we learn that a Christian’s pilgrimage in the world must express itself in a continuous service in all kinds of ways, varying with each person’s circumstances, but always motivated by love for God and for our neighbour. Being a Christian means forgetting petty objectives of personal prestige or ambition and even possibly nobler aims, like philanthropy and compassion for the misfortunes of others. It means setting our mind and heart on reaching the fullness of love which Jesus Christ showed by dying for us.1 Jesus’ birth, his life in Nazareth, his preaching and miracles, and above all his Passion and Death, are a summons for men through love

1 Christ is passing by, 98.

12 On Christian life

to direct their lives to God’s glory. Our Lord said: If I glorify myself, my glory is nothing. It is my Father who glorifies me ... I know him, and I keep his word.2

God’s glory, the last end of creatures The whole of Christian life, its purpose and value, rests on a truth of faith solemnly defined by the Church: “To manifest his perfection through the gifts bestowed on creatures – not to increase his happiness or acquire any perfection – this one true God, by his goodness and almighty power and by a completely free decision, ‘from the very beginning of time has created both orders of creatures in the same way out of nothing, the spiritual or angelic world and the corporeal or visible universe. And afterwards he formed the creature man, who in a way belongs to both orders, as he is composed of spirit and body’3.”4 Creatures receive their being, and thus all their perfections, from God; in themselves they are but non-being, limitation, contingency. The ultimate and definitive reason for their creation is God’s goodness and wisdom; he “fills creatures with his perfections”5 so they might show forth his glory. The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament proclaims his handiwork.6 Man likewise is created and destined for the supreme purpose of glorifying God; but in man God desires to show his perfections in a more excellent way. Then the Lord God formed man of dust from the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life.7 God created

2 John 8:54-55. 3 Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 428 (800); cf Innocent III, Letter Eius Exemplo, 18 December 1208, Dz 421 (790); Council of Florence, Bull Cantate Domino, Dz 706 (1333). 4 First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, Dz 1783 (3002). 5 St Ireneus, Adv. Haer., 4, 14, 1. 6 Ps 18:1-2; cf Ps 73:16-17. 7 Gen 2:7.

Foundations of the Moral Order 13

man in his image. In the image of God he created him.8 He gave man intelligence and free will so that he could discern and love God in his goodness. “Sacred Scripture teaches that man was created to the image of God, as able to know and love his Creator, and as set by him over all earthly creatures that he might rule them, and make use of them, while glorifying God.”9 Man is unique among God’s earthly creatures because he can understand and freely desire God’s glory as his end. Yet he alone can rebel, not because he can thwart God’s plans, but because God leaves to man the voluntary acceptance of his destiny as a creature.10 “The supreme law in man’s life is to have his gaze focused on God and to tend toward him. Created in the image and likeness of his Maker, man is powerfully inclined to possess him by his very nature. He does not tend toward God by bodily movement but rather by his intellect and will – both, movements of the soul.”11 As man draws closer to God, his happiness grows, for “the glory of God is very closely connected to the happiness of his creatures. To the extent that man glorifies God, man’s happiness and merits increase. And the more gifts God bestows on man, the more he reveals his goodness and adds to his glory. One is a source of increase for the other. But if we were to consider which of these two aims is pre-eminent, we would have to say: the ultimate aim of creation is God’s glory. The happiness of creatures, especially that of man, should be ordered to it: since the glorification of God refers to God himself, it is superior to the happiness of creatures. So it is fitting

8 Gen 1:27. 9 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 12. 10 Cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 1, 8 and 13, Dz 174, 181, 186 (371, 378, 383); Council of Quiercy, year 853, can. 1 and 2, Dz 316, 317 (621, 622); Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 6, Dz 325 (633); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 4-6, Dz 814-816 (1554-1556). 11 Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890.

14 On Christian life

that man’s happiness should be subordinate to God’s glory. One should not conclude from this that God’s goodness toward us is diminished, for the gifts that God gives us are not lessened by the fact that they are ordered to his glory. On the contrary, the more we strive to use them to give glory to God, the richer we become, for God does not seek his glory in the world by trying to acquire some good he does not yet possess; God demands only that the right order of things be maintained.”12 The glory is for God, the benefit for us. The ultimate end of human existence, that which directs it and gives it meaning, is God’s glory, and secondarily, true personal happiness in its fullest and deepest sense: the possession of God himself.13 Any man, by the very fact of existing, is bound to glorify God with all his strength. If life didn’t have as its aim to give glory to God, it would be detestable – even more, loathsome.14 This is not a duty man imposes on himself on discovering his created condition; rather, it stems directly from God’s plan of creation. God’s purposes intrinsically shape and define the human person. Thus says God, the Lord, who created the heavens and stretched them out, who spread forth the earth and what comes from it, who gives breath to the people upon it and spirit to those who walk in it ... I am the Lord, that is my name; my glory I give to no other.15 Living for God is, therefore, the primary foundation of morality, a norm of universal and absolute value. All men without exception are to strive for their ultimate end; insofar as their actions are aimed or not at God’s glory, they are made good or evil. “Without doubt, the fundamental duty of man is to order both his person and his life toward God.”16

12 Provincial Council of Cologne, 10 August 1860, ch. 3. 13 Cf Second Vatican Council, Decl. Nostra Aetate, 1. 14 The Way, 783. 15 Is 42:5-8. 16 Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 December 1947.

Foundations of the Moral Order 15

Supernatural elevation, fall, and restoration in Christ “God, out of his infinite goodness, destined man to a supernatural end, that is, to a participation in the good things of God, which altogether exceed the grasp of man’s mind. For eye has not seen nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man, what things God has prepared for those who love him17.”18 Our faith teaches that besides creating man, God destined him to a supernatural goal by elevating him to the order of grace.19 This end completely surpasses the knowledge of, and union with, God possible to our merely natural powers: its possession consists in “seeing the triune God himself as he truly is”,20 redounding to God’s supernatural glory and man’s supernatural happiness. This order, newly-established by God – and with it the natural order too – was broken at the dawn of history when “the first man Adam immediately lost the justice and holiness in which he had been constituted.”21 But God took it upon himself to right the disharmony: “the heavenly Father, the Father of mercies and the God of all comfort,22 when the glorious fullness of time23 had come, sent Jesus Christ his Son to men. Christ had been announced and promised to many holy Fathers before and under the Law.24 The Messiah was sent so that the Jews, who were labouring under the Law, might be redeemed, and so that the Gentiles, who were not pursuing justice, might secure justice,25 and that

16 On Christian life

all might receive adoption of children26.”27 Christ redeemed all men. Once again we have been offered the means to strive for and obtain our supernatural end; we can now glorify God and participate in his perfections, not simply as creatures but as adopted children.28 We could never have known of this supernatural vocation to a life of grace if God had not revealed it.29 “Christ the Lord, Christ the new Adam, in the very revelation of the mystery of the Father and of his love, fully reveals man to himself and brings to light his most high calling.”30 “Christ, who died and was raised for the sake of all,31 shows man the way and strengthens him through the Spirit in order to be worthy of his destiny.”32 Christ is the only way for each one to fulfil his supernatural end. Each person must enter into a living relationship with our Lord. Although Christ died for all,33 yet not all receive the benefit of his death, but those only to whom the merit of his passion is communicated.”34 Thus Christ is the one “mediator and the way of salvation ... present to us in his body which is the Church.”35 He is also the goal and model of human life. “The Church likewise believes that the key, the centre and the purpose of the whole of man’s history is to be found in its Lord and

Foundations of the Moral Order 17

Master. She also maintains that beneath all that changes there is much that is unchanging, much that has its ultimate foundation in Christ, who is the same yesterday, and today, and forever36.”37 This supernatural call imposes on all men, in some way, the duty to seek Christ, to find Christ, to love Christ. This encounter is fully achieved only in the Catholic Church, “to which we are all called in Christ Jesus, and in which by the grace of God we acquire holiness.”38 The Church is “the universal sacrament of salvation”;39 thus “they could not be saved who, knowing that the Catholic Church was founded as necessary by God through Christ, would refuse either to enter it or to remain in it.”40 Furthermore, “even though incorporated into the Church, one who does not persevere in charity is not saved. He remains indeed in the bosom of the Church, but ‘in body’, not ‘in heart’.”41 On the other hand, “those who, through no fault of their own, do not know the Gospel of Christ or his Church, but who nevertheless seek Christ with a sincere heart, and, moved by grace, try in their actions to do his will as they know it through the dictates of their conscience – those too may achieve eternal salvation42.”43 All these may attain their supernatural end and give glory to God as his true children.

Man’s supernatural end assumes his merely natural end All men are called to participate supernaturally in God’s perfections;

36 Heb 13:8. 37 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 10. 38 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 48; cf Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 430 (802). 39 Ibid. 40 Ibid, 14. 41 Ibid. 42 Cf Letter from the Holy Office to the Archbishop of Boston, August 8, 1949 (Dz 3866ff). 43 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 16; cf Pius IX, Address Singulari Quadam, 9 December 1854; Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, August 10, 1863, Dz 1677 (2866).

18 On Christian life

but they are not all obliged to seek his glory in the same way. “God does not command the impossible.”44 Since knowledge of man’s divine destiny is possible only through revelation, God does not demand the same response from those inculpably deprived of Christ’s message as from those with the gift of faith. However, there are not two vocations, two different destinies. “For since Christ has died for all, and since all men are in fact called to one and the same destiny, which is divine, we must hold that the Holy Spirit offers to all the possibility of being made partners, in a way known to God, in the paschal mystery.”45 The assumption of man’s natural ultimate goal into his supernatural one is the dynamic foundation for the unity of moral life: “the supernatural order ... far from destroying or weakening the natural order ... rather elevates and perfects it; both orders mutually help each other and have a complementary role in keeping with the nature of each, since both proceed from God, who cannot be inconsistent with himself: the works of God are perfect and all his ways straight46.”47 Natural morality concerns the relationship of man with his Maker, known and grasped as his ultimate goal through man’s natural faculties. Supernatural morality concerns man’s new relationship when raised to the status of God’s child and drawn into intimacy with the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit, known and possessed through faith and grace. These relationships belong to substantially different orders; they are not, however, like two parallel paths without any point of contact other than that of leading to God’s glory and man’s happiness. If man, by the mere fact of his existence, is obliged to direct his life to God, then his call to a life of grace, more than adding a new or different obligation, affirms the original obligation, makes it more explicit, and gives it added dimension. “The dignity of

44 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1536). 45 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 22. 46 Deut 32:4. 47 Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929, Dz 2206 (3689).

Foundations of the Moral Order 19

man rests above all on the fact that he is called to communion with God. The invitation to converse with God is addressed to man as soon as he comes into being. For if man exists, it is because God created him through love, and through love continues to hold him in existence. He cannot live fully according to truth unless he freely acknowledges that love and entrusts himself to his Creator.”48 All natural morality has been assumed into the supernatural moral order. In fact, man has but one final end – his supernatural end. This incorporates what would have been man’s end if he had not been raised to the supernatural order. Since God revealed the mystery of his holiness and love through the works of Creation and Redemption, there is only one moral order operative always and everywhere, but in different ways and in different degrees since Christ’s preaching has not reached all men. This moral order is one and absolute. Both, because it tends towards one, absolutely immutable end: union with God; and because it proceeds from God who necessarily loves good and hates evil: in him there is no change, nor shadow of alteration.49 The moral order is no product of man’s fancy or freedom; rather, it is a reality founded on God, who is infinitely good, wise and holy, and who shows himself to us through Creation and Redemption. Just as in the merely natural order man would not be morally free to respond positively or negatively to the end fixed by God, “when God touches the heart of man with the illumination of the Holy Spirit, man himself neither does absolutely nothing while receiving that inspiration, since he can also reject it, nor yet is he able by his own free will and without God’s grace to move himself towards justice in God’s sight. Hence, when it is said in Sacred Scripture, Return to me ... and I will

48 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 19. 49 Jas 1:17.

20 On Christian life

return to you,50 we are reminded of our freedom; when we answer, Convert us, O Lord, to thee, and we shall be converted,51 we acknowledge that God’s grace prepares us.”52 Without grace, we cannot lead a Christian moral life or grow therein. Without grace, it is impossible to fulfil God’s will.53 Moreover, when God aids human freedom “man’s voluntary actions are not less free on this account, for the power of divine grace acts intrinsically in man and is compatible with his natural tendencies, because it has its source in the very same Creator of our intellect and will, who moves everything in accordance with its proper nature.”54 It follows, then, that “to those who work well unto the end55 and trust in God, eternal life is to be offered, both as a grace mercifully promised to God’s children through Jesus Christ and ‘as a reward’56 promised by God himself, to be faithfully given for their good works and merits.”57

Universal call to holiness “All Christians in any state or walk of life are called to the fullness of Christian life and to the perfection of love58.”59 God’s glory should be shown in his children in the most perfect and complete way. For God has made us sharers in his nature,60 and through his Only-Begotten

Foundations of the Moral Order 21

Son, “teacher and model of all perfection,”61 he has revealed to us the import of being raised to the supernatural order: You therefore are to be perfect, even as your heavenly Father is perfect.62 God’s will that we be holy is what governs all Christian life, and should be followed by each Christian according to his particular state and circumstances. But all without exception should realize that “the followers of Christ, called by God not in virtue of their works but by his design and grace, and justified in the Lord Jesus, have been made sons of God in the baptism of faith and partakers of the divine nature, and so are truly sanctified. They must therefore hold on to and perfect in their lives that sanctification they have received from God.”63 “Therefore, all the faithful are invited and obliged to holiness and the perfection of their own state in life.”64 This is a moral obligation, since it bears on the specific end to which God has destined us and which he has revealed for his glory and our happiness. As a moral principle, the universal call to holiness entails more than seeing Christian life as merely a struggle to avoid evil. Looking only to what ought to be avoided is a minimalist and negative view of the order proposed by God in the economy of salvation. The call to holiness awakens a positive conviction regarding the saving power of Christ’s gospel and the sanctifying action of the Holy Spirit, over and above human circumstances and settings. This call to holiness clarifies the meaning of life, which has been created, elevated and redeemed by God. We begin to see our life as a marvellous opportunity to show forth God’s love in its most intimate and hidden perfections, and we experience an insatiable desire to give supernatural glory to the most Holy Trinity and so attain eternal happiness.

61 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 62 Matt 5:48. 63 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 64 Ibid, 42.

22 On Christian life

Our Lord wants us to be his instruments to remind people in a practical way – also by living it – that the call to sanctity is in fact universal, and not exclusive to a few or to a particular state in life, nor is it conditional in general on abandonment of the world: any job, any profession, can be a path to sanctity and a means for apostolate. This, my children, is sure doctrine, light from God. Doctrine ... founded on Sacred Scripture and on the Church’s Tradition, constantly confirmed, despite our human shortcomings, by our experience of Opus Dei’s life.65 The only true ground of Christian morality is God himself. He is present in all things,66 while no less transcendent. Although he dwells in unapproachable light,67 he has created, elevated and redeemed us, destining us for his own glory and our eternal happiness through his Son, Our Lord Jesus Christ, who is the power and the wisdom of God.68 Christ uncovered the treasures of divine Love, restoring all things;69 through him we are made holy70 and are called to an eternal inheritance.71

65 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 26. 66 Cf Acts 17:24-28. 67 1 Tim 6:16. 68 1 Cor 1:24. 69 Cf Eph 1:10; 2 Cor 5:18. 70 Heb 10:10. 71 Heb 9:15.

## THE NATURAL MORAL LAW {#cuaderno.2.2}

“The moral order, whose principles are universal, absolute, and unchangeable, has its ultimate source in the one true God, personal and transcendent. God is the first truth and the highest good; he alone is the deepest source from which human society can draw its vitality if it is to be well ordered, beneficial and in keeping with human dignity.1 As St Thomas Aquinas says: ‘That human reason is the norm of the human will, by which its goodness is measured, derives from the eternal law, which is the divine reason itself. It is evident, then, that the goodness of the human will depends much more on the eternal law than on human reason’2.”3

The Eternal Law, ultimate norm and objective of morality God disposes all things. His is the marvellous ordering of the universe; he reaches from end to end mightily and governs all things well.4 His is man’s creation and elevation to the supernatural order; he calls us to share his intimate life. All things, indeed, have been predisposed by God wisely and definitively from eternity. The Lord created me, sings divine Wisdom, at the beginning of his work, the first of his acts of old. Ages ago I was set up, at the first, before the beginning of the earth. When there were no depths I was brought forth, when there were no springs abounding with water. Before the mountains had been

1 Cf Pius XII, Christmas Message, 1942. 2 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., II-II, q. 19, a. 9. 3 John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3973). 4 Wis 8:1.

24 On Christian life

shaped, before the hills, I was brought forth; before he had made the earth with its fields, or the first of the dust of the world. When he established the heavens, I was there, when he drew a circle on the face of the deep, when he made firm the skies above, when he established the fountains of the deep, when he assigned to the sea its limit, so that the waters might not transgress his command, when he marked out the foundations of the earth, then I was beside him, like a master workman; and I was daily his delight, rejoicing before him always, rejoicing in his inhabited world and delighting in the sons of men.5 This divine Wisdom orders all things so that each creature fulfils its end of glorifying God in a particular way, according to its nature and the laws governing its activity. As such, divine Wisdom is termed ‘the Eternal Law’: “the eternal reason of God, creator and ruler of all the world.”6 St Augustine calls it “God’s eternal reason or will, which ordains that the natural order be maintained, and forbids it to be disturbed.”7 The Eternal Law governs the purely physical order of inanimate creation and the biological order of living creatures. Likewise does it govern the moral order to which spiritual beings belong. Because it is identified with divine Wisdom, Eternal Law is absolute, immutable and universal. God never varies in his counsels and resolutions.8 Christian faith confesses “one true and living God, eternal, immeasurable and unchangeable,”9 “who with his providence sustains and governs all that he has created.”10 “As God’s sun shines on all that bears human countenance, so does

5 Prov 8:23-31. 6 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 3247). 7 Contra Faustum, 22, 27. 8 Cf Heb 6:17. 9 First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, Dz 1782 (3001); cf Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 428 (800). 10 Ibid, Dz 1783 (3002).

The Natural Moral Law 25

his law know no privileges or exceptions. Rulers and ruled, great and small, rich and poor – all are equally subject to his word. From the sum total of his rights as Creator flows connaturally the sum total of his claims to obedience on the part of the individual and society.”11 The Eternal Law, then, is the ultimate and objective basis of all moral order. The goodness or evil of our actions is measured, ultimately, by their conformity to the Eternal Law. All other laws derive their obligatory character and regulatory power from their participation in the Eternal Law. “The binding force of human laws lies in this, that they are applications of the eternal law and cannot be made to sanction anything not contained in the eternal law, principle of all law.”12 Thus, “the highest norm of human life is the divine law itself – eternal, objective and universal – by which God orders, directs and governs the whole world and the ways of the human community according to a plan conceived in his wisdom and love.”13 By its very nature the Eternal Law cannot be known by reason in God himself, nor can it be known in a perfect way. However, “God has enabled man to participate in this law of his so that, under the gentle disposition of divine providence, many may be able to arrive at an ever deeper knowledge of unchangeable truth.”14 This participation has been brought about in two different ways: one natural, through creation; the other supernatural, through divine revelation. God revealed himself to men in a progressive way, culminating – when the fullness of time came – in his Son, Jesus Christ, the way, the truth and the life.15 The Church teaches that “Christian moral law is

11 Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937. 12 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 3249); cf Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939 (Dz 3781). 13 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 14 Ibid. 15 John 14:6.

26 On Christian life

found both in the law of the Creator inscribed on every man’s heart and in revelation, the body of truths and precepts taught by the divine Master.”16

Natural Law, participation of rational creatures in the Eternal Law When God created man, he gave him free will – the capacity to act or not, to choose between this or that – and it became necessary for God to communicate his law to every human creature, enlightening man as to what he ought or ought not to do, so as to gain his final end. This law, as “a fixed rule teaching what is to be done and what is to be left undone, cannot affect the lower animals in any true sense, since they act out of necessity, following their natural instinct, and cannot of themselves act in any other way.”17 Man, however, thanks to his spiritual intelligence, can know “what is right or wrong by its very nature, and what is in practice good and therefore to be chosen, and what is in practice evil and therefore to be avoided. This ‘ordination of reason’ is called law. In man’s free will, therefore, or in the moral necessity for our voluntary acts to be in accordance with reason, lies the very root of the necessity for law. Nothing more foolish can be uttered or conceived than the notion that because man is free by nature, he is therefore exempt from law. Were this the case, it would follow that to become free we must be deprived of reason; whereas the truth is that we are bound to submit to law precisely because we are free by our very nature. For law is the guide of man’s actions; it turns him towards good by its rewards, and deters him from evil by its punishments. Foremost in this office comes the ‘natural law’, which is written and engraved in the mind of every man; and this is nothing but our reason,

16 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952; cf Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939; Address to the Roman Rota, 13 November 1949; First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1785 (3004). 17 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 3247).

The Natural Moral Law 27

commanding us to do good and forbidding us to do evil. But the precepts of human reason can have force of law only inasmuch as they are the voice and interpreter of some higher power on which our reason and liberty necessarily depend ... It follows, therefore, that the law of nature is the same thing as ‘the eternal law’, inscribed in rational creatures.”18 In more recent times, attempts have been made to obscure, deform and even deny this clear and transparent Catholic doctrine. False teachers of morality are directly or indirectly undermining the principle of authority – in the Church, in the family, in the school – or the validity of the precepts of both natural law and divine law. They are thus preparing the ground for the abandonment of all objective norms, and spreading a mentality based on the false presuppositions of relativism, historicism and immanentism.19 In these circumstances, the best service we can render the Church and mankind is to give doctrine. Much of the evil afflicting the world is due to ignorance of Christian doctrine, even among those who want, or give the impression of wanting, to follow Christ closely. For there are some who, instead of giving sound doctrine, take advantage of the ignorance of others to spread confusion. Some even go so far as to deny the existence of natural law, imprinted by God on each soul.20 The principles of natural law regulate and illuminate not only the moral conduct of individuals and their most basic rights and duties, but also social institutions, principally the family and civil society. The existence of the natural law is a truth of faith,21 repeatedly affirmed by

18 Ibid. 19 From our Father, Letter 30 April 1946, 23. 20 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 27. 21 Cf Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 12 December 1690, 2, Dz 1292 (2302); Pius IX, Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863; Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 3247); Enc. Immortale Dei 1 November 1885 (Dz 3165); Enc. Rerum Novarum, 15 May 1891, Dz 1938a (3265-6): Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929, Dz 2206 (3689); Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930, Dz 2225, 2239, 28 On Christian life

the Church’s Magisterium as one of the fundamental pillars of the whole moral order: “Before all else, it is certain that the radical and ultimate cause of the evils which we deplore in modern society is the denial and rejection of a universal norm of morality for individual and social life as well as for international relations. We mean the disregard, so common nowadays, and the forgetfulness of the natural law itself, which has its foundation in God, almighty Creator and Father of all, supreme and absolute lawgiver, all-wise and just judge of human actions. When God is hated, the whole basis of morality is undermined; the voice of conscience is stilled or at any rate grows very faint: that voice which teaches even illiterate and uncivilized tribes what is good and what is bad, what is lawful and what is forbidden, and makes men feel themselves responsible for their actions to a supreme judge.”22 To say that natural law is imprinted on men’s hearts and is like a voice pointing out the goodness and wrongness of things, is not to use a mere poetic phrase empty of content. It is a simple and graphic way of expressing the existence of a natural inclination in man to evaluate correctly the moral content of created realities, to judge whether or not they help or hinder the most fundamental tendency of his being, the tendency impelling him toward God and his final end. Instinctively, every man appreciates that loving his fellow men, telling the truth, fulfilling promises, and so on, are good and desirable things; and, conversely, that lies, betrayal, falsity, and the like, are repugnant. By means of his intellect, each person can discover whether a particular act should be done or not, and accept or

2243 (3700-3701, 3715, 3716, 3720); Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937; Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939, Dz 2279 (3780-1); Address, 13 November 1949; Address, 23 March 1952; Address, 18 April 1952; Decree of the Holy Office, 2 February 1956 (Dz 3918-21); John XXIII, Enc. Mater et Magistra, 15 May 1961; Enc. Pacem in Terris 11 April 1963 (Dz 3770); Paul VI, Enc. Populorum Progressio, 26 March 1967; Enc. Humanae Vitae, 25 July 1968, 4. 22 Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939, Dz 2279 (3780-1).

The Natural Moral Law 29

reject it by the power of his free will. Natural law, then, is expressed in the moral judgments of reason. St Paul sums up this doctrine in his letter to the Romans: When Gentiles who have not the (revealed) law do by nature what the law requires, they are a law to themselves, even though they do not have the law. They show that what the law requires is written on their hearts, while their conscience also bears witness and their conflicting thoughts accuse or perhaps excuse them.23

Properties of the Natural Law All men, without exception, are subject to natural law. Its first principles are unchangeable and valid,24 regardless of time and place, since “the fundamental obligations of the moral law are based on man’s nature, that is, on his essence and on relationships proper to his essence. Consequently, they are valid wherever man is to be found. These essential relationships – between man and God, between man and man, husband and wife, parents and children, as well as community relationships in the family, Church, and civil society – all give rise to many things being forbidden by the divine lawgiver. Among these are hatred of God, blasphemy, idolatry, defection from the true faith, denial of the faith, perjury, homicide, false witness, calumny, adultery and fornication, abuse of matrimony, masturbation, robbery, and plunder, deprivation of the necessities of life, defrauding of wages, monopolization of basic necessities of life and unjust price increases, fraudulent bankruptcy, and unfair speculation. There is no reason to doubt that they are prohibited by the divine Lawgiver. Whatever the situation that faces the individual, his only option is to obey.”25

23 Rom 2:14-15. 24 Cf Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939, Dz 2279 (3780-1). 25 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952; cf Pius XII, Decree of the Holy Office, 2 February, 1956 (Dz 3918-21).

30 On Christian life

The universality and immutability of these fundamental principles of natural law that are indelibly inscribed in human nature constitute the firm basis of the rights and duties of the person. These rights and duties, since they “are universal and inviolable, are also absolutely inalienable.”26 All the fundamental rights of the person “have their root, their sustenance and their indestructible force in the natural law, which confers or imposes them.”27 This explains the importance of Catholic teaching on natural law not only from a doctrinal, but also a practical and apostolic point of view. My children, our Father has written, you must have very clear ideas on a minimum of fundamental questions; only then will you be able to defend the rights of Christ and the Church through your activity in the world: the sanctity of marriage, Catholic education, social justice, respect for private property, the right to have children and to educate them, the right to work, the right to assistance in illness and old age, to rest and relaxation, and so on. And do not forget that the Church condemns today, as it always has, theoretical or practical materialism, and specifically Marxism, because it rejects God and his loving plan for all men; because it persecutes Christians and disregards fundamental rights of the human person.28 These rights belong to natural law. We Christians are particularly obliged to ensure that civil laws respect and protect them, since the law imprinted in human nature is the standard to which all man-made laws must conform. “Every positive law from any lawgiver can be examined as to its moral implications and authority to bind in conscience, in light of the norms of natural law. Human laws that directly contradict natural law bear an initial defect that no violent means, no outward display of power, can remedy.”29

26 John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3957). 27 Ibid, (Dz 3970). 28 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 37. 29 Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937.

The Natural Moral Law 31

The fulfilment of the Natural Law necessary for salvation “By God’s will, the observance of the natural law pertains to the path whereby man reaches his supernatural end.”30 If you wish to enter into life, our Lord said to the rich young man, keep the commandments.31 The precepts of the natural law, made explicit in the commandments God gave to Moses on Mount Sinai,32 have salvific value for pagans as well as believers.33 They embody God’s wisdom and will, directing man towards his final supernatural end. Thus, their observance is a necessary means for achieving eternal happiness. “No one, even though he be justified, should consider himself exempt from keeping the commandments. And no one should say that it is impossible for the just man to keep God’s commandments, for that is a rash statement censured with anathema by the Fathers.34 God does not command the impossible. When he commands, he cautions you to do what you can, and to pray for what you cannot do,35 helping you so you can do it. His commandments are not burdensome;36 his yoke is easy and his burden light.37 For those who are God’s children love Christ; and those who love him, as he himself testifies, keep his words,38 which they can certainly do with God’s help. In this mortal life, however just and holy men may be, they sometimes commit at least slight daily sins, also called venial sins. Yet not on that account do they cease to be just, for the just say truthfully and humbly: Forgive us our debts.39

30 Pius XII, Address, 2 November 1954. 31 Matt 19:17. 32 Cf Exod 20:2-17; Deut 5:6-18. 33 Cf Council of Arles, year 475, Dz 160 (336-341). 34 Cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, Dz 200 (397). 35 Cf St Augustine, De Natura et Gratia, 43, 50. 36 Cf 1 John 5:3. 37 Cf Matt 11:30. 38 Cf John 14:23. 39 Matt 6:12; cf Sixteenth Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 7, Dz 107 (229).

32 On Christian life

Hence, the just themselves should feel a greater obligation to walk in the way of justice; set free from sin and made servants of God,40 living temperately and justly and piously,41 they can advance through Christ Jesus, through whom they have access unto grace42.”43 For those who through no fault of their own do not yet know Christ, natural law is the only objective norm of morality; their actions must conform to it, if they are to achieve their last end. “We all know that those who are afflicted with invincible ignorance with regard to our holy religion, if they carefully keep the precepts of natural law inscribed by God in the hearts of all men, if they are prepared to obey God, and if they lead a virtuous and upright life, can attain eternal life by the power of divine light and grace.”44

Knowledge of the Natural Law The Church teaches that natural law can be known by human reason, at least in its fundamental principles. “It is well known that the Church makes much of human reason in the following connections: when demonstrating the existence of one personal God, when irrefutably establishing from divine signs the foundations of the Christian faith itself, and when giving correct expression to the natural law inscribed in men’s hearts by the Creator.”45 This doctrine is based on a dogmatic declaration of the First Vatican Council: “God, the origin and end of all things, can be known by the natural light of human reason from the things that he created.”46

40 Cf Rom 6:22. 41 Cf Tit 2:12. 42 Cf Rom 5:2. 43 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1536-7). 44 Pius IX, Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863, Dz 1677 (2866); cf Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, prop. 2, Dz 1292 (2302). 45 Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2320 (3892). 46 First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1785 (3004); cf can. 1 De Revelatione, Dz 806 (3026).

The Natural Moral Law 33

The ordinary magisterium of the Roman Pontiffs has legitimately interpreted this declaration as referring to the first principles of the natural law.47 “Though, absolutely speaking, human reason by its own natural force and light can arrive at a true and certain knowledge ... of the natural law, which the Creator has written in our hearts, still there are not a few obstacles to prevent reason from making efficient and fruitful use of its natural ability. The truths that have to do with God and the relations between God and men completely surpass the sensible order and demand self-surrender and self-abnegation in order to be put into practice and to influence practical life. Now the human intellect, in gaining knowledge of such truths, is hampered both by the activity of the senses and imagination, and by evil passions arising from original sin. Hence men easily persuade themselves in such matters that what they do not wish to believe is false or at least doubtful. It is for this reason that divine revelation must be considered morally necessary so that those religious and moral truths which are not of their nature beyond the reach of reason in the present condition of the human race, may be known by all men readily with a firm certainty and with freedom from all error.”48 History teaches that, even after Christ had consummated our Redemption on the Cross, “men have often fallen into very many errors about the true God, human nature and the principles of morality.”49 Hence our Lord also entrusted to his Church the power to interpret the natural law authentically and legitimately, a power that the Church’s magisterium

47 Cf Pius XI, Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930; Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950. 48 Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2305 (3875-6); cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1786 (3005); Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937. 49 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 7.

34 On Christian life

has exercised since its foundation. “It is in fact indisputable, as our predecessors have many times declared,50 that when Jesus Christ communicated to Peter and the apostles his divine authority and sent them to teach his commandments to all nations,51 he constituted them guardians and authentic interpreters of the whole moral law; this includes not only the law of the Gospel, but also the natural law. The natural law is also an expression of God’s will, and its fulfilment is equally necessary for salvation52.”53 Every Christian is bound to defend and affirm this sure doctrine, which is an integral part of Catholic doctrine. The salvation of souls depends on it. As you see, my beloved children, the Church’s teaching on topics discussed by public opinion is scarcely known; people voice, argue, attack, and defend all sorts of views, as if the Magisterium, ordinary or solemn, had said nothing or might have nothing to say. Even the most insistent and energetic clarifications by the Roman Pontiff are often passed over in silence.54 All Christians should make known and spread the teachings of the magisterium. Our Father has said more than once: And so you will defend whatever the Church says, for when it comes to divine truths, She is the sole Teacher. And you will do so with your example, with your word, with your writings: with all the noble means at your disposal.55 Among the truths that the magisterium teaches with the Holy Spirit’s assistance is that there exists an absolute, universal and immutable

50 Cf Pius IX, Enc. Qui Pluribus, 9 November 1846; Pius X, Enc. Singulari Quandam, 24 September 1912; Pius XI, Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930; Pius XII, Address Magnificate Dominum, 2 November 1954; John XXIII, Enc. Mater et Magistra, 15 May 1961. 51 Cf Matt 28:18-19. 52 Cf Matt 7:21. 53 Paul VI, Enc. Humanae Vitae, 25 July 1968, 4. 54 From our Father, Letter 30 April 1946, 18. 55 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 8.

The Natural Moral Law 35

eternal law which stands forever, through all generations,56 and that this eternal law is communicated to mankind not only by revelation but also by a law inscribed in its very nature, whereby man can know God’s will and love and fulfil it. Teach me your paths, guide me in your truth and teach me, for you are God my Saviour, and for you I wait all the day.57

56 Ps 32:11. 57 Ps 24:4-5.

## THE LAW OF THE GOSPEL {#cuaderno.2.3}

May my tongue sing of your promise, for all your commands are just. Let your hand be ready to help me, for I have chosen your precepts.1 I love your commandments above gold, above fine gold.2 God has destined man for more than the natural perfection and happiness he might have achieved by following the way “written by the finger of the Creator himself on the tables of men’s hearts and discernible by sound reason undarkened by sin and passion.3 Wishing to open up the way to heavenly salvation, he manifested himself to our first parents from the very beginning. After the fall, he buoyed them up with the hope of salvation by promising redemption;4 and he has never ceased to take care of the human race. For he wishes to give eternal life to all those who seek salvation by patience in well-doing5.”6 Sacred Scripture preserves for us the memory of that succession of divine interventions in men’s lives; God invites men through messengers and prophets to recognize the way of salvation traced out for us in his infinite mercy. “After God had spoken many times and in various ways through the prophets, in these last days he has spoken to us by his Son.7 For he sent

1 Ps 118:172-173. 2 Ibid, 127. 3 Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937. 4 Cf Gen 3:15. 5 Cf Rom 2:6-7. 6 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 3. 7 Heb 1:1-2.

The Law of the Gospel 37

his Son, the eternal Word who enlightens all men, to dwell among men and to tell them about the inner life of God 8 ... He himself completed and perfected revelation and confirmed it with divine guarantees.”9 By revealing his most intimate secrets and his desire to bring man to a supernatural destiny, God asks us to respond with faith.10 This entails a new way of life and moral code, which we are able to follow with the help of God’s grace.11 These new “obligations, surpassing those of natural law, are based on the supernatural order established by the divine Redeemer.”12 “Man must, therefore, take his standard of a loyal and religious life not only from the eternal law, but also from each and every one of those laws which God, in his infinite wisdom and power, has been pleased to enact and make known to us by such clear and unmistakable signs as to leave no room for doubt. And the more so, because laws of this kind have the same origin, the same author, as the eternal law, are absolutely in accordance with right reason, and perfect the natural law. They embody the teaching of God, who graciously guides and directs man’s intellect and will, lest he fall into error.”13 Although natural law and supernaturally revealed law are distinct and belong to different orders, they do not contradict each other. Both direct man, God’s creature and child, towards his only final end: a supernatural goal of glorifying the most holy Trinity and enjoying eternal happiness. They also have the same origin: “the principles of natural law and revealed truths flow, as it were, in two parallel streams, from

8 Cf John 1:1-18. 9 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 4. 10 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1789 (3008); Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 5; Rom 16:26; 1:5; 2 Cor 10:5-6. 11 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7 and can. 18-20, Dz 799, 800, 828-830 (1528-31, 1568-70). 12 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952. 13 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888.

38 On Christian life

the same divine source”,14 which is the eternal law. Moreover, in the present economy of salvation, the natural law has been assumed and perfected by the supernatural law.15

The Old Law, preparation for the law of the Gospel God began to reveal his salvific will from the beginning of man’s life on earth.16 But it was through Moses that God revealed the commandments of the Decalogue: with a loud voice from the midst of the fire and the dense cloud 17... he wrote them upon two tablets of stone.18 God wished to sanction these precepts of natural law solemnly: You shall be careful to do therefore as the Lord your God has commanded you; you shall not turn aside to the right hand or to the left. You shall walk in all the way which the Lord your God has commanded you, that you may live, and that it may go well with you, and that you may live long in the land which you shall possess.19 He also dictated precepts concerning divine worship and legal ceremonies, and also the juridical organization of the chosen people. These served to “prepare for and declare in prophecy the coming of Christ, redeemer of all men, and of the messianic kingdom,20 and ... indicate it by means of different types.21 For in the context of the human situation before the era of salvation established by Christ, the books of the Old Testament provide an understanding of God and man and make clear to all men how a just and merciful God deals with mankind. These books, even though they contain matters imperfect and provisional, nevertheless show us

14 Pius XII, Whitsun Message, 1941. 15 Cf Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929, Dz 2206 (3689). 16 Cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 3. 17 Deut 5:22. 18 Ibid. 19 Ibid, 5:32-33. 20 Cf Luke 24:44; John 5:39; 1 Pet 1:10. 21 Cf 1 Cor 10:11.

The Law of the Gospel 39

authentic divine teaching22.”23 As St Paul says: Before faith came, we were confined under the law, kept under restraint until faith should be revealed. So that the law was our custodian until Christ came.24 The Old Testament’s code of moral laws coincides with the most important precepts of natural law. It is therefore called by St Paul good;25 the law is holy, and the commandment is holy and just and good.26 It was added because of transgressions, till the offspring (Christ) should come, to whom the promise had been made; and it was ordained by angels through an intermediary (Moses).27 But if the Law was given through Moses, grace and truth came through Jesus Christ.28 Until the Redeemer’s coming, the commandments showed the path to follow to reach God, but they did not of themselves give the strength needed to follow that way.29 St Paul writes: We know that the law is spiritual; but I am carnal, sold under sin. I do not understand my own actions. For I do not do what I want, but I do the very thing I hate. Now if I do what I do not want, I agree that the law is good. So then it is no longer I that do it, but sin which dwells within me. For I know that nothing good dwells within me, that is, in my flesh. I can will what is right, but I cannot do it. For I do not do the good I want, but the evil I do not want is what I do. Now if I do what I do not want, it is no longer I that do it, but sin which dwells

22 Cf Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937. 23 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 15. 24 Gal 3:23-24. 25 1 Tim 1:8. 26 Rom 7:12. 27 Gal 3:19; cf Leo IX, Letter Congratulamur Vehementer, 13 April 1053, Dz 348 (685); Innocent III, Letter Eius Exemplo, 18 December 1208, Dz 421 (790); Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (854); Council of Florence, Bull Cantate Domino, Dz 706, 712, 713 (1334, 1348, 1350). 28 John 1:17. 29 Cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 16 and 21, Dz 189, 194 (386, 391); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521).

40 On Christian life

within me. So I find it to be a law that when I want to do right, evil lies close at hand. For I delight in the law of God, in my inmost self, but I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members. Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death? Thanks be to God through Jesus Christ our Lord! So then, I of myself serve the law of God with my mind, but with my flesh I serve the law of sin.30

Christ’s New Law installed “With the death of the Redeemer, the Old Law was abolished and the New Testament took its place; it was then that Christ’s Law, with its mysteries, laws, institutions, and sacred rites, was ratified for the whole world by the blood of Jesus Christ. So long as the Divine Saviour was preaching in a restricted territory (for he had been sent only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel),31 both the Law and the Gospel were in force together;32 but by his death on the cross he made void the decrees of the Old Law33 and fastened the handwriting of the Old Testament to the cross,34 establishing the New Testament in his blood which he shed for the whole human race35.”36 Christ’s Incarnation, and particularly his Death and Resurrection, radically transformed man’s relationship with God and consequently the very foundations for moral life. As St Paul explains: when we were children, we were slaves to the elemental spirits of the universe. But

30 Rom 7:14-25. 31 Cf Matt 15:24. 32 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 103, a. 3 ad 2. 33 Cf Eph 2:15. 34 Cf Col 2:14. 35 Cf Matt 26:28; 1 Cor 11:25. 36 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943.

The Law of the Gospel 41

when the time had fully come, God sent forth his Son, born of woman, born under the law, to redeem those who were under the law, so that we might receive adoption as sons.37 “This Christian economy, since it is the new and definitive covenant, will never pass away.”38 The new moral order ushered in by Christ definitively declares, restores, perfects, sanctifies and elevates to a much higher order the commandments of the Old Law. Think not that I have come to abolish the law and the prophets; I have come not to abolish them but to fulfil them,39 Christ said, adding: For I tell you, unless your righteousness exceeds that of the scribes and Pharisees, you will never enter the kingdom of heaven.40 Indeed Jesus Christ was not only “given by God to men as a redeemer in whom they are to trust, but also as a law-giver whom they are to obey.”41 This is a dogma of faith defined by the Council of Trent. Thus the Gospel he charged the apostles to preach throughout the world is “the source of all saving truth and moral teaching”.42 The New Law is known through revelation by means of faith, under the guidance of the Church’s magisterium, which is entrusted with the office of authentically interpreting God’s word.43

37 Gal 4:3-5. 38 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 4; cf Pius II, Letter Cum Sicut Accepimus, 14 November 1459, prop. 9, Dz 717 (1369). 39 Matt 5:17; cf Rom 3:31; Matt 5:18-43; 7:12ff; 12:1-8; 19:8ff; 19:17; Mark 7:14ff; Luke 16:29. 40 Matt 5:20. 41 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 21, Dz 831 (1571). 42 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 7; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Canonicis Scripturis, Dz 783 (1501). 43 Cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 10; cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1792 (3011); Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2314 (3886).

42 On Christian life

The law of the Gospel, an interior law of grace and freedom The Old Law guided men to the knowledge of God’s will, but did not of itself confer the graces needed to fulfil it. But the New Law, the constitution for God’s children, “is predominantly the very grace of the Holy Spirit, given to those who believe in Christ. The New Law, however, has certain precepts disposing us to receive the grace of the Holy Spirit and helping us to live according to that grace ... Therefore Christ’s faithful had to be instructed both orally and in writing about what to believe and how to act. Hence the New Law is first and foremost an inward law, and secondarily a written law.”44 The Council of Trent sanctioned this doctrine when it defined as a dogma of faith that justification, as a fruit of Christ’s new order, is not realized “through the imputation of Christ’s justice alone, or through the remission of sins alone, excluding grace and charity poured forth in their hearts by the Holy Spirit.”45 On the contrary, “the justification of the unjustified takes place when, by the merit of the same most holy Passion, God’s charity is poured forth by the Holy Spirit into the hearts of those who are justified46 and remains in them.”47 The Gospel’s Law is essentially an interior force, God’s grace and sanctity: “not the justice whereby he himself is just, but the justice whereby he makes us just; namely, the justice we have as a gift from him and whereby we are renewed in the spirit of our mind.”48 This gift, grace, moves us to know and follow God’s most holy will and enables us supernaturally to carry it out. The same Council of Trent, however, recalls that “faith without works

44 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 106, a. 1 c. 45 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 11, Dz 821 (1561). 46 Cf Rom 5:5. 47 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1530). 48 Ibid, Dz 799 (1529).

The Law of the Gospel 43

is dead49 and useless.”50 And in other documents the magisterium has condemned the error of those who try to rid Christ’s Law of its normative content, reducing it to a blind and mysterious tendency, thus confusing interiority with the most radical subjectivity.51 God gives us grace by means of sensible, exterior signs, and principally through his Son: And the Word was made flesh ... full of grace and of truth ... and of his fullness we have all received.52 Thus his Law is made manifest by exterior signs: on the one hand, the sacraments of the New Law, which confer grace and are necessary for salvation,53 and on the other hand, the moral precepts and norms that prohibit, command, advise or permit certain actions, according to whether they conform or not to interior grace. Amen, amen, I say to you, he who believes in me, the works that I do he also shall do ... If you love me, keep my commandments.54 He who says that he knows him and does not keep his commandments, is a liar and the truth is not in him.55 Christ’s Law is the perfect law of liberty.56 St Thomas explains: “since the grace of the Holy Spirit is a kind of interior disposition infused in us which inclines us to act rightly, it makes us do freely whatever accords with grace and avoid whatever is contrary to it. The New Law, then, is called a law of freedom in two senses. Firstly, because it does not constrain us to do or avoid anything apart from what of itself is necessary or contrary to salvation ... Secondly, because

49 Jas 2:17ff. 50 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1531). 51 Cf Council of Vienne, Const. Ad Nostrum Qui, prop. 3, Dz 473 (893); Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 28 July 1687; Const. Coelestis Pastor, 20 November 1687, prop. 1-8, Dz 1221- 1228 (2201-2208); Innocent XII, Brief Cum Alias, 12 March 1699, Dz 1327-1349 (2351-2373). 52 John 1:14-16. 53 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis in Genere, can. 1-8, Dz 844-851 (1601-8). 54 John 14:12-14. 55 1 John 2:4. 56 Jas 1:25.

44 On Christian life

even such precepts or prohibitions it contains, we fulfil freely, inasmuch as we fulfil them by an inner stirring of grace.”57 Christ, conqueror of sin58 and of the devil,59 broke the chains keeping us in “bondage in the power of him who from that time had the empire of death60.”61 Thus, St Paul tells us, sin will have no dominion over you, since you are not under law but under grace.62 Through Christ you did not receive the spirit of slavery to fall back into fear, but you have received the spirit of sonship.63 Thus our Lord’s promise is fulfilled: if the Son makes you free, you will be free indeed,64 in virtue of the freedom wherewith Christ has made us free.65 Freed from the tyranny of lust and the power of the devil and absolved from the legal precepts of the Old Law,66 the New Law of Christ urges us to seek always and in everything God’s glory, rejoicing in the glorious liberty of the sons of God.67 Moral norms, then, are not mere boundaries fixing limits to what is permitted or forbidden. Rather they manifest the path leading to God. A Christian therefore heeds much more than what is licit or illicit: All things are lawful for me, but I will not be enslaved by anything.68 “There is no human law so powerful to safeguard the personal dignity and freedom of man as the Gospel which Christ entrusted to the Church;

57 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 108, a. 1 ad 2. 58 Cf Rom 6:6; Heb 9:26; John 1:29; 1 John 3:5. 59 Cf John 12:31; Col 2:15. 60 Cf Heb 2:14. 61 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 1, Dz 788 (1511); cf ibid, can. 3, Dz 790 (1513); Decr. De Iustificatione ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521). 62 Rom 6:14. 63 Rom 8:15. 64 John 8:36. 65 Gal 4:31. 66 Cf Council of Florence, Bull Cantate Domino, Dz 712 (1348). 67 Rom 8:21. 68 1 Cor 6:12.

The Law of the Gospel 45

for the Gospel announces and proclaims the freedom of the sons of God, it rejects all bondage resulting from sin,69 it scrupulously respects the dignity of conscience and its freedom of choice, it never ceases to encourage the employment of human talents in the service of God and man, and finally, it commends everyone to the charity of all.70 This is nothing other than the basic law of the Christian scheme of things.”71

The Precept of Charity: summary and fullness of the law of the Gospel This is Opus Dei’s great apostolate: to show the multitudes awaiting us what is the path that leads straight to God. Therefore, my children, realize that you are called to the divine task of proclaiming God’s mercies: misericordias Domini in aeternum cantabo (Ps 88:2), I will sing forever the mercies of the Lord.72 All the precepts of Christ’s Law are, as it were, included in the commandment to love. You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, and with all your soul, and with all your mind. This is the great and first commandment. And a second is like it, You shall love your neighbour as yourself. On these two commandments depend all the law and the prophets.73 It could not be otherwise. The new order inaugurated by Christ is the law of grace, applicable to each soul along with God’s love ... poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit who has been given to us.74 Since the aim of the New Law is the supernatural glorification of God by our coming to know and love him with the help of grace, charity is the very source and foundation of Christian life.75

69 Cf Rom 8:14-17. 70 Cf Matt 22:39. 71 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 41. 72 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1930, 3. 73 Matt 22:37-40; cf Rom 13:8-10. 74 Rom 5:5. 75 Cf 1 Cor 13:1ff.

46 On Christian life

The Church ever insists on the primacy of love in the New Law. “Before all else it is urgent that charity be quickened and maintained, because it is the main foundation of Christian life, apart from which the other virtues do not exist or remain barren. For this reason the Apostle Paul, after having exhorted the Colossians to flee all vice and cultivate all virtue, adds: “Above all these put on love, which binds everything together in perfect harmony.”76 Charity is the bond of perfection, for it binds intimately to God those whom it has embraced, and with loving tenderness causes them to draw their life from God, to act with God, to refer all to God. And yet the love of God should not be severed from the love of our neighbour, since men have a share in the infinite goodness of God and bear in themselves the impress of his image and likeness. This commandment we have from him, that he who loves God should love his brother also.77 If any one says ‘I love God,’ and hates his brother, he is a liar.78 And this commandment concerning charity its divine proclaimer styled new, not in the sense that a previous law, or even nature itself, had not enjoined that men should love one another, but because the manner in which Christians were to love one another was truly new, and quite unheard of until then. For the charity with which Jesus Christ is loved by his Father and wherewith he himself loves men, he obtained for his disciples and followers, that they might be of one heart and one mind in him by charity, just as he himself and his Father are one by their nature.”79 The Work of God, our Father wrote, was born to spread throughout the world the message of love and peace our Lord left us.80

76 Col 3:14. 77 1 John 4:21. 78 Ibid, 20. 79 Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890; cf Benedict XV, Enc. Pacem Dei Munus, 23 May 1920. 80 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 3.

The Law of the Gospel 47

The precept of charity commands Christians to seek to act out of love for God always and in everything. Christ’s morality implies a radical decision, without compromise: No man can serve two masters.81 Therefore no moral actions, properly speaking, are neutral or indifferent. Everything is done either out of love for God or love for self. The law of charity does not prescribe mere sentimentality. Rather it is full of practical consequences. Christ wishes all men to be saved (1 Tim 2:4) and none to be lost. He hastens to give his life for all, in an outpouring of love that is a perfect holocaust ... We, my daughters and sons, must do the same, because we are also urged by that love of Christ: caritas Christi urget nos (2 Cor 5:14).82 Love for God is shown in deeds of love for our neighbour. For how can he who does not love his brother, whom he sees, love God, whom he does not see? And this commandment we have from him, that he who loves God should love his brother also.83 Christian morality, expressed in the Law of the Gospel, proposes and makes viable an ideal of life far surpassing the most perfect and desirable ethical system. It does so through Christ, “perfect God and perfect man,”84 author and final goal of all grace, “source and origin of all holiness.”85 Jesus sums up the Law in his person; he is the image to which all men can and should conform. This truth is an essential part of the Gospel to be preached to all people.86 The ideal of sanctity, which is one and the same for all Christians, is accessible through the various states or kinds of life. One need not leave them, because each of them is a divine way leading us to our

81 Matt 6:24. 82 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 2-3. 83 1 John 4:20-21. 84 Symbolum Quicumque. 85 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 47. 86 Cf Matt 28:28.

48 On Christian life

Lord. We need only fulfil, in each state or condition, the duties that our state and work require. By doing this we fulfil the will of God our Father. And so ... following St Paul’s advice – estote ergo imitatores Dei sicut filii carissimi (Eph 5:1) – we imitate God (cf St Gregory of Nyssa, De Professione Christiana), the only holy one (cf Lev 11:44; Mark 10:18), as beloved children, taking for our model Jesus Christ, true image of the invisible God (cf Col 1:15).87 “Only through Christ will man achieve personal fulfilment; through him, his actions will have life; his relations with his neighbour and with material things will be rightly ordered; his worthy aspirations will be satisfied. In a word, through Christ and from Christ man will have fullness and perfection of life, even before a new heaven and earth appear on the horizon of eternity88.”89

87 From our Father, Letter 2 February 1945, 9. 88 Cf Rev 21:1. 89 Pius XII, Easter Message, 1957; cf Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 41.

## MORAL CONSCIENCE {#cuaderno.2.4}

“Our divine Saviour has brought his truth and his grace to man, ignorant and weak as he is: truth, to point out the way that leads to his goal, and grace to give him the strength to reach it. Travelling this road means, in practice, accepting Christ’s will and commandments and conforming one’s life to them in each of the actions, internal and external, which man’s free will chooses and determines.”1 To reach the supernatural goal God has marked out for him, man counts on the guidance of God’s law. This law, by enlightening and strengthening man, gently but imperatively leads him to glorify the most Holy Trinity, and thus attain supernatural and everlasting happiness. Instruct me, O Lord, in the way of your statutes, that I may exactly observe them. Give me discernment, that I may observe your law and keep it with all my heart.2

Conscience, the proximate and immediate norm of morality The moral law, known through faith and reason, should be the directing and impelling norm for human actions. For this to be effectively so in the great variety of circumstances that can arise, God has endowed human nature with “a spiritual faculty that in each particular case gives guidance to the will so that it may choose and determine its actions in

1 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 2 Ps 118:33-34.

50 On Christian life

conformity with God’s will.”3 By his conscience man comes to know God’s law, both its principles and its particular applications.4 He discovers his duty to observe the law’s commandments, realizing that only in doing so will he reach his final end. And, after taking into account the circumstances of each situation,5 he discerns to what extent his actions and intentions agree with what the objective moral law lays down. “Conscience is the judgment we make on the rectitude – that is, the morality – of our actions, whether looked at generally or as individual acts.”6 Therefore, morality teaches that the judgment of conscience is the proximate and immediate (subjective) rule of our actions. No objective norm or law can ever regulate an act except through its application by the person acting. To be a valid norm of behaviour, this application should precede and accompany the act, and not only follow it. Divine law, both natural and revealed, sets man on the way leading to God. Conscience – especially that of a Christian, who enjoys the help of grace and the light of faith – actualizes at every moment and in every situation his orientation toward God by indicating what one may or ought to do.7 It either confirms good actions8 or warns about bad ones.9 The possession of this spiritual faculty confers on man a special dignity.10 As a result, in his relations with his Creator, his freedom ranges from

3 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 4 Cf Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952; John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3956); Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16; Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 5 Cf Pius XII, Address, 12 April 1952; Instruction of the Holy Office, February 2, 1956 (Dz 3918). 6 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 7 Cf Gen 3:7-10; Ps 25:1-7; Mark 9:44, 46, 48; Rom 2:15; Rom 13:5, 7; Rom 14:20-23; 1 Cor 8:7-12. 8 Cf 1 Pet 2:19; 1 Pet 3:16, 21. 9 Cf Gen 4:13-14; 2 Sam 24:10; Job 27:6; Wis 17:10; Is 65:14. 10 Cf Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16, 17 and 41; Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 1-3.

Moral Conscience 51

what is merely licit to what is most agreeable to God’s loving will. Moreover, he is enabled to sound the depths of his responsibility before God and to attain a unique and non-transferable title to merit or blame for his actions. “Conscience is, as it were, the most intimate and secret core of man. There he takes refuge with his spiritual faculties in absolute solitude. There he is alone with himself or, better, alone with himself and God, whose voice conscience echoes. There man decides to do good or to do evil; there he chooses the road to victory or the road to defeat. Though sometimes he might want to, man can never divest himself of his conscience. Approving or disapproving of what he does, his conscience will accompany him all the days of his life; and it will also be with him, as a true and incorruptible witness, when he appears before God’s judgment seat.”11
