Obligatory character of moral conscience Catholic theology has underlined the essential role of conscience in morality by traditionally recognizing it as the proximate and immediate norm of conduct, closely dependent on the divine law, which is the ultimate and constitutive norm. In so far as his conscience discerns the dictates of moral law for each of his actions, man “is bound to follow his conscience faithfully in all his activity so he may come to God, his last end.”12 “Anyone who acts contrary to his conscience strays from the right road”13 and incurs sin. Therefore, no one may be “forced to act contrary to his conscience ... or be prevented from acting according to his conscience, especially in religious matters.”14 The obligatory moral force of conscience applies to all man’s activity; hence “a Christian

11 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952; cf Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16. 12 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 13 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969; cf Rom 14:23. 14 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3.

52 On Christian life

conscience should always guide man, since not even in the temporal sphere may any human activity be withdrawn from God’s dominion.”15 The obligation to follow one’s conscience does not stem from conscience itself, but from the objective moral law it knows and interprets. The moral law, in expressing the all-wise and unchanging will of God, manifests a specific moral order whereby creatures can approach their Creator and Redeemer. Personal conscience discerns this order, but has no power to change it. “Conscience, in itself, is not the arbiter of the moral value of the actions it proposes. Conscience is the interpreter of an inner, higher norm that it does not create. Conscience is illuminated by intuiting specific normative principles, deeply rooted in human reason,16 but it is not the source of good and evil. Conscience is a pointer; it is like hearing a voice. In fact we speak precisely of the voice of conscience – a reminder that actions should conform to an intrinsic requirement of man, if he is to be true and perfect. Conscience is the subjective and immediate grasping of the law we call natural, although many people today do not want to hear of the natural law.”17 For a Christian, moreover, conscience is an interpreter of Christ’s law, promulgated with the grace poured into his heart by the Holy Spirit.18 It is “the faithful echo, the clear reflection of the divine norm for human actions. Thus expressions like ‘the judgment of a Christian conscience’ ‘to judge according to a Christian conscience’ have this meaning: the norm for the ultimate and personal decision on a moral action is taken from Christ’s word and will.”19 The judgments of conscience are therefore the licit and authentic norm for Christian conduct to the extent that they truly express God’s

15 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 36. 16 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I, q. 79, a. 12 and 13; I-II, q. 94, a. 1. 17 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 18 Cf Rom 5:5. 19 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952.

Moral Conscience 53

law. Conscience, “to be a valid norm for human behaviour, must be right, that is, it must be true and sure of itself, and not doubtful or culpably erroneous.”20 A person who would act contrary to what his conscience dictates would sin. But if conscience is the proximate and immediate norm of moral action, the supreme norm is God’s law. In translating the supreme norm of God’s law into a proximate norm for one’s activity, a person could reach moral judgments that are erroneous, owing to ignorance or error in interpreting either a norm or specific circumstances of an action. In such a situation, if a person has good faith and finds it morally impossible to rise above error, his conscience is ‘invincibly erroneous’. “It often happens that conscience goes astray through ignorance which it is unable to avoid, without thereby losing its dignity. This cannot be said of the man who takes little trouble to find out what is true and good, or when conscience is by degrees almost blinded through the habit of committing sin.”21 A person should follow his conscience when it is certain, even though it be erroneous; that is to say, an erroneous moral judgment – where no guilt is involved – does not destroy the directive value of conscience.22 The Church has always respected the imperative authority of the individual conscience, even that of an invincibly erroneous conscience; this respect is shown in the praxis of the sacrament of penance in certain particular cases especially applicable to people who have very little religious education. This obligation to follow one’s own certain conscience is not based on the subjective conviction that an action is good when in fact it is

20 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 21 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16; cf Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 22 Cf Rom 14:22-23; John 9:41; John 15:22; Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, prop. 2, Dz 1292 (2302); Pius IX, Address Singulari Quadam, 9 December 1854, Dz 1647; Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863, Dz 1677 (2866).

54 On Christian life

bad; it is based rather on an objective fundamental norm of moral law forbidding one to act with the intention of sinning. That precisely is what a person does who acts against his conscience, not realizing that his conscience is erroneous. Such is not the case of doubtful judgments of conscience (where a normal individual, psychologically and morally balanced, is not sufficiently sure about the rightness of an action); nor is it the case of a vincibly erroneous conscience (where one more or less clearly realizes that he may be mistaken). In these situations, morality requires that one resolve the doubt or clarify the error. It is not right to act with an actual or habitual possibility of sinning.23 St Paul refers to those who did not have a properly educated conscience regarding the lawfulness of eating food that was considered impure by the old Mosaic law: I know and am persuaded in the Lord Jesus that nothing is unclean in itself; but it is unclean for anyone who thinks it unclean ... happy is he who has no reason to judge himself for what he approves. But he who has doubts is condemned, if he eats, because he does not act from faith.24 Therefore Paul vigorously recommends overcoming the errors and weaknesses of conscience,25 obtaining from God a conscience that is good26 and clear,27 free from all fault before God and men,28 since a Christian is obliged to fulfil his obligations not only to avoid God’s wrath, but also for the sake of conscience.29 This has nothing to do with a lack of holy freedom of spirit, or with scrupulosity, whatever its source.

23 Cf Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 4 March 1679, prop. 4, Dz 1154 (2104). 24 Rom 14:14-23. 25 Cf 1 Cor 8:10. 26 1 Tim 1:5. 27 1 Tim 3:9. 28 Cf Acts 24:61. 29 Rom 13:5.

Moral Conscience 55

Moral conscience and the objective order Weakness of conscience, its possibility of erring, the deformations to which it is exposed – especially when God is not sought above all things – is a fact of experience. This shows the fallacy of “the supremacy and exclusiveness that people today try to attribute to conscience in the guidance of human conduct. One hears frequently, as an unquestioned maxim, that morality should entirely consist in following one’s own conscience.”30 Behind such statements lies forgetfulness of the consequences of original sin31 and an erroneous interpretation of the nature of conscience. “Conscience is not the only voice that can guide human activity. Its voice becomes clearer and stronger when united to the voice of law and lawful authority. The voice of conscience is not always infallible, nor is it objectively supreme. This is true particularly in the sphere of supernatural action, where reason cannot by itself discern the right path, but must have recourse to faith and revelation to know the norm willed by God: the just man lives by faith32 says St Paul.”33 Over and above one’s conscience is the certain and solid support of the teachings of faith and God’s law. Life of faith, my children. This is all a matter of the head, not the heart. When your opinion goes against a moral principle, it means you are mistaken. It’s all very simple, my son. To deny the principles of Christian morality is very comfortable. But only those who live an upright life will understand this. The primacy of the objective moral law as a principle of human behaviour belongs to the heritage of the Church’s faith. The magisterium

30 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 31 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 1 and 5, Dz 788 and 792 (1511 and 1515); Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521). 32 Gal 3:11. 33 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969.

56 On Christian life

has always appealed to divine law, both natural and revealed, when declaring and interpreting moral principles related to society, the family, the duties of spouses, education, the temporal activity of Christians, economic matters, procreation, etc. “The highest norm of human life is the divine law itself – eternal, objective and universal.”34 Relying on this law, known through reason and faith, conscience specifies in each case the moral content of one’s activity. “Because of its universality, the moral law necessarily and ‘intentionally’ covers all the particular cases where its concepts are actually realized. And in these cases, which are very numerous, it does this with so conclusive a logic that even the conscience of an ordinary member of the faithful perceives immediately and with full certainty the decision to be taken.”35 For these reasons the Church’s magisterium has rejected so-called ‘situation ethics’, which makes conscience the supreme and exclusive norm of morality in personal actions. “Authors who follow this system declare that the ultimate and decisive norm of behaviour is not the objective and right order as laid down by natural law and known with certainty from that law. Instead, they find this norm in a kind of inner light and intellectual judgment of each individual, whereby he understands what he should do in the particular situation he faces. Thus this decision would not be – as objective ethics holds, according to the teaching of the more important authors – an application of objective laws to a particular case while taking into account the specific circumstances of the situation and evaluating them according to the rules of prudence. Rather it would result from that inner light and internal judgment. According to those who advocate ‘situation ethics’, the truth and objective rectitude of that judgment, at least in many cases, is not measured, should not be measured, and is not measurable

34 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 35 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952.

Moral Conscience 57

by any norm existing outside man that is independent of his own subjective convictions, for man is fully self-sufficient.”36 Such teachings denying the directive value of an objective moral order have been rejected by the Church as “contrary to truth and the dictates of sound reason; they follow the line of relativism and modernism, and are alien to much of Catholic doctrine held throughout the centuries.”37 For the same reasons the Church has also rejected so-called “freedom of conscience,” if this is understood to mean the emancipation from any extrinsic moral norm, or the rejection of any authority that can dictate laws to govern the free and spontaneous activity of the human person, as if he should be a law unto himself, brooking no interference with his actions.38 Going further, some people understand ‘freedom of conscience’ as the radical rejection of any norm or authority, even that of conscience itself: a kind of practical amorality. Or they maintain that everything human – instincts, tendencies, impulses – is good by its very nature, and has no need to be regulated, not even by subjective conscience. ‘Freedom of consciences’ is another matter. It is “the right of souls to procure for themselves the greatest spiritual good, according to the teaching and under the guidance of the Church ... and the right of souls so formed to share the treasures of the redemption with other souls.”39 No one can deny that “it is lawful for man, according to his conscience, to follow the will of God and fulfil his commandments in society

36 Instruction of the Holy Office, 2 February 1956 (Dz 3918). 37 Ibid, (Dz 3920). 38 Cf Paul VI, Address , 3 February 1969; Gregory XVI, Enc. Mirari Vos, 15 August 1832, Dz 1613 (2730); Pius IX, Address Maxima Quidem, 9 June 1862; Syllabus, 8 December 1864, prop. 3, 4, 16, 56, 57, Dz 1703, 1704, 1716, 1756, 1757 (2903, 2904, 2916, 2956, 2957); Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 325O); Pius XI, Enc. Non Abbiamo Bisogno, 29 June 1931; Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1953; Address, 12 April 1952. 39 Pius XI, Enc. Non Abbiamo Bisogno, 29 June 1931.

58 On Christian life

without the least impediment. This true freedom, worthy of God’s children, fittingly safeguards the dignity of the human person. Rising above all injustice and violence, it has always been desired and singularly loved by the Church.”40 Since the beginning of his preaching our Father repeatedly taught this doctrine of the Church, which has always been practiced in the Work. One of the prominent features of Opus Dei’s spirit is a great love for personal freedom, which you should practice with great care in your work of directing souls. Teach them to respect – just as we have always done – the “freedom of consciences” of the faithful in general, so that they may go to any priest they want in search of guidance, and so that they may take part in the means of Christian formation wherever they like.41 God created man free and takes his freedom into account in saving him. But it is only possible for man to attain salvation by following the path God himself has marked out. “God calls men to serve him in spirit and in truth. Consequently they are bound to him in conscience but not coerced.”42 The precepts of divine law do not coerce but – principally in religious matters – “they touch and bind man’s conscience”,43 even though man is free to answer this call or not, to attain his goal or go astray. If I were forced to kneel and obliged to recite a prayer, our Father used to say, but in my heart – in my free will – I didn’t accept what I was being made to say or do, my words and actions would have no value in God’s sight, for he reads the depths of conscience.44 God desires that man should seek him by following his “conscience

40 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888 (Dz 3250). 41 From our Father, Letter 2 February 1945, 35; cf From our Father, Letter 15 August 1953, 34; From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 72. 42 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 11. 43 Ibid, 1. 44 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, note 69.

Moral Conscience 59

and free choice, that is, as moved and drawn in a personal way from within, and not by blind impulses in himself or by mere external constraint.”45 Herein is man’s true dignity. Thus “everybody has the duty, and consequently the right, to seek the truth in religious matters so that, through the use of the appropriate means, he may prudently form judgments of conscience which are upright and true.”46

Forming moral conscience “Conscience needs forming. Education of conscience is necessary, just as it is necessary for every man to grow interiorly, since his life develops in an external framework that is both complex and demanding.”47 This need is all the more pressing, given the fact that without a correct, sensitive, sincere and true conscience, an upright life is not possible. Your eye is the lamp of your body; when your eye is sound, your whole body is full of light; but when it is not sound, your body is full of darkness. Therefore be careful lest the light in you be darkness.48 In forming a truly Christian conscience, the first thing to take into account is that “man’s best and surest teacher is God, source and principle of all truth; and the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father: the way, the truth, and the life,49 the true light that enlightens every man,50 whose teaching all must willingly follow: and they shall all be taught by God51.”52 “Hence it follows that forming a Christian conscience consists, above all, in teaching Christ’s will and

45 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 17. 46 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 47 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969. 48 Luke 11:34-35. 49 Cf John 14:6. 50 Cf John 1:9. 51 John 6:45. 52 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888.

60 On Christian life

law, leading souls, insofar as this can be done from outside, to the free and constant carrying out of God’s will”,53 through the development of the virtues necessary for healthy moral growth. “But where is Christian morality to be found, concretely, easily, and certainly? In the law of the Creator inscribed on the heart of each person,54 and in revelation: the ensemble of truths and precepts taught by the Divine Master. Both the law written in man’s heart – the natural law – and the truths and precepts of supernatural revelation have been entrusted by Jesus our Redeemer to his Church as a moral treasure for mankind. The Church has the mission to preach and explain this treasure, transmitting it from generation to generation intact and free from all contamination and error.”55 Hence, “in forming their consciences the faithful must pay careful attention to the sacred and certain teaching of the Church.”56 How can there be moral rectitude when one appeals to one’s own conscience to avoid following – and even to go against – the moral norms faithfully and clearly taught by the Church’s Magisterium, whether ordinary or extraordinary? The presence of a living magisterium in Christ’s Church, in moral matters also, guarantees the correct formation of one’s conscience. Therefore it is hardly possible for Christians to have an invincibly erroneous conscience in matters on which the Magisterium has clearly spoken. If this were to happen, the error would most often be voluntary, because the person is unwilling to form his conscience or to make use of available means and opportunities for instruction, which entails a certain degree of guilt. There are also times, our Father wrote, when these errors are the result of a mistaken formation. In not a few cases these poor people

53 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 54 Cf Rom 2:14-16. 55 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 56 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 14.

Moral Conscience 61

have been without someone to teach them the truth. And so I think that on judgment day many souls will reply to God as did the paralytic beside the pool – hominem non habeo (John 5:7), I had no one to help me – or as those unemployed workers answered the owner of the vineyard – nemo nos conduxit (Matt 20:7), no one has called us to work. Although their errors may be culpable and their perseverance in evil be deliberate, in the depths of these unfortunate souls there is a profound ignorance that only God can measure.57 Each man possesses in his nature the possibility of knowing God and the moral order willed by him. Therefore he can personally form an upright and true conscience. In practice, however, forming one’s conscience is a delicate and difficult task that calls for the constant practice of Christian virtues – especially prudence, sincerity, and fortitude – so as not to be led astray by one’s passions or be induced into error by an intellect weakened by original sin. Therefore in matters of morality, the Magisterium’s help is morally necessary: it is a light Christ has entrusted to his Church to help draw all souls to himself. “To advance safely when walking at night – to advance in the mystery of Christian life – eyes are not enough: we also need a lamp, a light. And the ‘light of Christ’ does not distort or go against the light of our conscience. On the contrary, it provides clarity, enabling us to follow Christ by the right path on our journey to eternal contemplation.”58 Together with a deep disposition to follow Christ, and a clear and firm knowledge of his teachings as put forward by the Church, we need to develop the natural and supernatural virtues indispensable for living an upright life. First of all, humility: If you aren’t humble, deeply humble, your conscience can easily get deformed. Perhaps in our life,

57 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 24. 58 Paul VI, Address, 13 February 1969.

62 On Christian life

our Father warned us, through weakness, we may do wrong. But we must have clear ideas, a clear conscience: we can never do bad things and claim they are holy.59 Closely united with humility should go charity, simplicity and the freedom and responsibility proper to God’s children. St Paul, echoing Christ’s teaching, writes: Put off your old nature which belongs to your former manner of life and is corrupt through deceitful lusts, and be renewed in the spirit of your minds, and put on the new nature, created after the likeness of God in true righteousness and holiness.60 To strengthen one’s conscience and confirm it in goodness, Christians also rely on prayer and the sacraments, especially “penance and the eucharist, which give what nature cannot give: the strength not to fall and, if one were to fall, the strength to get up. Without the help of these supernatural energies one would never manage to be a good Christian or even merely an upright person living in peace. Assisted by this strength, all Christians can aspire to that great task, their highest honour: building up Christ in their life.”61 The Church needs well-formed souls who reflect in themselves Christ’s image without distortion. To be of use to the Mystical Body what is needed is an upright conscience, correctly formed, which yields the fruit of good works and knows to respect the freedom of others’ consciences.62 Forming an upright and true conscience, sensitive to the moral law, means laying a solid basis for developing a mature Christian life. Only thus do we reach mature manhood, to the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ.63 Vatican II urged “everyone, especially those

59 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 37. 60 Eph 4:22-24. 61 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 62 From our Father, Letter 31 May 1943, 8. 63 Eph 4:13.

Moral Conscience 63

responsible for educating others, to try to form men with a respect for the moral order who will obey lawful authority and be lovers of true freedom: men, that is, who will form their own judgments in the light of truth, direct their activities with a sense of responsibility, and strive for what is true and just in willing cooperation with others.”64 Our Father reminded us of this serious obligation toward the people around us, especially those close to Opus Dei: We have, then, an obligation to form these souls, in a way which helps them be good Catholics, correcting their conduct, fostering in them the need for interior life, and instilling in them the conviction that daily work is the most suitable means to achieve the fullness of Christian life and to do good to all souls. With a well-formed conscience a Christian can better “assume the great and grave task of showing forth in his personal life, in his professional life, and in social and public life, the truth, spirit and law of Christ, insofar as it lies within him. This is a Christian’s personal responsibility.”65

64 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 8. 65 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952.

## MAN’S NATURE {#cuaderno.2.5}

“Sacred Scripture teaches that man was created in the image of God, as able to know and love his Creator, and as set by him over all earthly creatures1 that he might rule them and make use of them, while glorifying God.2 What is man that thou art mindful of him, and the son of man that thou dost care for him? Yet thou has made him little less than the angels and dost crown him with glory and honour. Thou has given him dominion over the works of thy hands; thou has put all things under his feet3.”4

Man, a creature made up of soul and body Man’s privileged role in creation is based on his bodily and spiritual nature as willed by God, who “created all creatures, whether spiritual or corporeal, out of nothing: angelic creatures and earthly creatures, and then humans, embracing both realms, because they are made up of spirit and body.”5 It is a dogma of faith that man is composed of body and soul.6 In so

1 Cf Gen 1:26; Wis 2:23. 2 Cf Sir 17:3-10. 3 Ps 8:5-6. 4 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 12. 5 Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 428 (800); cf XV Council of Toledo, year 688, Dz 295 (567); Council of Vienne, Const. De Summa Trinitate et Fide Cath., Dz 481 (902); First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, Dz 1783 (3002). 6 This doctrine, along with that of the rational nature of the soul, has been defined by the Church in her declarations that Christ, in his incarnation, took on a body and a soul: cf Man’s Nature 65

declaring, the Church holds to divine revelation in Genesis,7 where we are told that God formed man of dust from the ground, and breathed into his nostrils the breath of life; and man became a living being.8 Though composed of matter, man is much more than a material being: God has infused him with a spirit of life, the soul. The first man Adam, St Paul reminds us, was formed with a living soul.9 And St James teaches us, the body apart from the spirit is dead.10 Catholic faith absolutely rejects the notion that “nothing exists beyond matter.”11 But neither does the Church consider man solely spirit, exiled from heaven and imprisoned in a body. Man is not a mere animal, nor is he a pure spirit. “He is a person, wonderfully adorned by the Creator with gifts of body and spirit, a real “microcosm,” as the ancients would say, that is, a little world which greatly exceeds in value the entire inanimate world.”12 In the human being, the world of invisible realities and that of visible realities compenetrate in a wonderful unity. Though matter and spirit are principles differing in nature, man’s soul and body are joined so as to constitute one single being. This truth, rich in consequences for moral life, has always been defended by the Church. Against those who are bent on destroying this unity by seeing in

Council of Chalcedon, Dz 148 (301); Second Council of Constantinople, can. 4, Dz 216 (424); First Lateran Council, can. 2, Dz 255 (502); Third Council of Constantinople, Dz 290 (554); St Leo IX, Letter Congratulamur Vehementer, 13 April 1053, Dz 344 (681); Alexander III, Letter Cum Christus, 18 February 1177, Dz 393 (750); Innocent III, Letter Eius Exemplo, 18 December 1208, Dz 422 (791); Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 429 (801); Council of Vienne, Const. De Summa Trinitate et Fide Catholica, Dz 481 (902). 7 Cf Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939, Dz 2280; Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2329 (3898).. 8 Gen 2:7. 9 1 Cor 15:45. 10 Jas 2:26. 11 First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, can. 2, Dz 1802 (3022). 12 Pius XI, Enc. Divini Redemptoris, 19 March 1937 (Dz 3771).

66 On Christian life

man various souls or principles of life, the Fourth Council of Constantinople declares: “the Old and New Testaments teach that man has only one soul, rational and intellectual, and all the Fathers and Teachers and the Church, divinely inspired, affirm the same opinion.”13 The distinction so widely held today between the psychic and the spiritual in man, if handled correctly, does not imply two different radical principles of life or behaviour. Rather it affirms the existence of a twofold order of human activity: one organic, closely tied to bodily functions; another inorganic or spiritual, proper only to the soul and intrinsically independent of the body.14

The spiritual soul, vital principle of man The Church further explains that body and soul are so united that, without losing their essential properties, they constitute but one single being, the human person. In the Council of Vienne she condemned “as erroneous and an enemy of the truth of the Catholic faith any doctrine or proposition that rashly states or suggests that the substance of the rational or intellectual soul is not truly and in itself the form of the human body.”15 The Church has continued to reaffirm this teaching,16 but without sanctioning a particular philosophical teaching as dogma. The Church’s teaching authority declares that since man’s bodilyspiritual structure is a dogma of faith, we must hold that the soul, by its very nature and without need of any intermediary, is the root or source of all human acts and operations. This does not mean that it is the soul that thinks, just as it is wrong to say it is the body that walks: it is the

13 Fourth Council of Constantinople, can. 11, Dz 338 (657). 14 Cf St Pius X, Decree from the Congregation of Studies, 27 July 1914, thesis 17 (Dz 3617). 15 Council of Vienne, Const. De Summa Trinitate et Fide Cath., Dz 481 (902). 16 Cf Fifth Lateran Council, Bull Apostolici Regiminis, Dz 738 (1440); Pius IX, Brief Eximiam Tuam, 15 June 1857, Dz 1655 (2828); Leo XIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 14 December 1887, prop. 21-24, Dz 1911-1914 (3221-3224).

Man’s Nature 67

whole man, the person, who carries out these acts, thanks to his body and soul. Yet, in the final analysis, the explanation for man’s actions – whether thinking or walking – is that he possesses a vital spirit infused by God, whence come all human powers. “The view that places in man a single principle of life, namely, a rational soul, from which the body also receives movement, life and sensation, is very common teaching in God’s Church. The majority of teachers – and certainly those with most prestige – hold it is so linked to Church teaching that it is the legitimate and only true interpretation of dogma, and therefore cannot be denied without erring in the faith.”17 If a biological, material structure can acquire the configuration proper to the human body, this is due to the soul. So it would not be right to speak of man exclusively as if he were the most perfect and highly evolved animal. The spirit intersects the hierarchy of physical nature and places man on a higher plane. The direct intervention of God is required in the creation of each man: “the Catholic faith teaches that souls are immediately created by God.”18 All typically human actions such as thought, free will, speech, sentiments and inclinations, social life, artistic creation, and so on, find no satisfactory explanation unless they stem from the spiritual nature of the human person. According to Catholic teaching, the most noble and elevated part of man is his soul, whose perfection is not limited to giving form to the body. Being spiritual, the soul does not originate from corporeal things; moreover “it does not depend on them for its conservation, so that far surpassing the condition of things material, it has a life and action of its own”19 that is rational and free. The souls of irrational creatures “are only the principle by which

17 Pius IX, Letter Dolore Haud Mediocri, April 30, 1860 (Dz 2833). 18 Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2327 (3896); St Leo IX, Letter Congratulamur Vehementer, 13 April 1053, Dz 348 (685). 19 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888.

68 On Christian life

they are living beings; but, since they depend completely on matter, when the composite perishes, they also perish. The human soul, on the other hand, subsists by itself ... and by its nature is incorruptible and immortal.”20 The immortality of the human soul is another declared truth of faith.21

Man as a rational being That man possesses a spiritual soul is apparent, first of all, from his intelligence. Because of it, he transcends the limitations of his bodily condition and through created realities comes to the knowledge of his Creator as the goal of his moral life.22 “Man’s intellect is not confined to the range of what can be observed by the senses. It can, with genuine certainty, grasp realities known only to the mind, even though, as a result of sin its vision has been clouded and its powers weakened.”23 Due to the spirituality of his soul, man enjoys a reflexive, psychological awareness allowing him to assess his own actions and thoughts, and to confirm or correct them in order to direct them toward God. He can, effectively, turn “to those deep recesses of his being where God, who probes the heart,24 awaits him, and where he himself decides his own destiny in the sight of God.”25 Thus he alone can lay claim to and be responsible for his own decisions and actions. The interior activity found in all spiritual beings takes on specific characteristics in the case of man. “Although the human intellect is able to know itself, it has the starting point of its knowledge outside itself. It

20 St Pius X, Decree from the Congregation of Studies, theses 14 & 15 (Dz 3614-3615). 21 Cf Fifth Lateran Council, Bull Apostolici Regiminis, Dz 738 (1440); Symbolum Quicumque, Dz 40 (76); First Council of Constantinople, Symbolum Nicaeno-Constantinopolitanum, Dz 86. 22 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, can. 1, Dz 1785 and 1806 (3004 and 3026); Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, August 12, 1950, Dz 2305 (3875). 23 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 15. 24 Cf 1 Kings 16:7; Jer 17:10. 25 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 14.

Man’s Nature 69

can know nothing without the help of sense images. For this reason, the spiritual life of angels is more perfect; their intellect does not need any external impulse to arrive at self-knowledge – they know themselves through themselves. But angels still do not reach the ultimate degree of perfection ... for in angels being and knowing are not identical. The supreme perfection of life, therefore, pertains only to God, in whom being and knowing are not distinct.”26 Man’s interior activity points to transcendence. He is not closed within himself: starting from created things, within or without, he can decipher traces of his Creator. Man as spiritual being is not meant to withdraw into himself but to open out towards his Lord and Creator. Both dimensions are basic to our moral life: we must be aware of our own being and action no less than the goal to be sought. Hence Yahweh breathed a spirit into man, for “man does not approach God by bodily movement but by his intelligence and will, which are movements of his soul.”27 Human freedom The spirituality of man’s soul is also the root of his freedom. “The unanimous judgment and common sense of men, which is the clear voice of nature, recognizes this freedom to exist only in those who are endowed with intelligence or reason, and this is why man is rightly regarded as responsible for his actions. For, while other animate creatures follow their senses, seeking good and avoiding evil only by instinct, man has reason to guide him in each and every action of his life. Reason sees that of all the things held to be good upon earth, each equally could exist or could not exist; and discerning that none of them are of necessity for us, it leaves the will free to choose what it pleases. But man can judge of this ‘contingency’, as it is called, only because he

26 St Thomas Aquinas, Summa Contra Gentiles, I, 4, 11. 27 Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890.

70 On Christian life

has a soul that is simple, spiritual, and capable of thinking ... so that, knowing the unchangeable and necessary reasons for what is true and good, it sees that no particular kind of good is necessary to us. When, therefore, it is established that man’s soul is immortal and endowed with reason and not restricted by things material, the foundation of natural freedom is at once most firmly laid.”28 Man’s freedom, like the soul’s spirituality and God’s existence, is supported by arguments based on human reasoning.29 However, so crucial is it for understanding man’s true nature and his relationship to God that the Church has repeatedly affirmed, as a truth of faith, the existence of free will. While teaching that “almighty God created man upright, sinless, with free will,”30 and that original sin wounded him in such a way that “the whole man, that is to say, the body and the soul were changed for the worse,”31 she acknowledges that man’s free will “was also weakened,”32 but “in no sense was it extinguished.”33 Moreover, it is Catholic teaching that, at the very start of the justification of those separated from God, his grace is given to them. Thus they “may be disposed to their own justification by freely assenting to and cooperating with that grace. The result is that, when God touches the heart of man with the illumination of the Holy Spirit, the man who accepts that inspiration certainly does something, since he could reject it; on the other hand, by his own free will, without God’s

28 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888. 29 Cf Pius IX, Decree from the Congregation of the Index, prop. 2, Dz 1650 (2812). 30 Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 1, Dz 316 (621). 31 Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 1, Dz 174 (371). 32 Ibid, can. 8, Dz 181 (378); cf Indiculo, Dz 129-130, 133, 134, 140 (238, 239, 242, 243, 247); Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 2, Dz 317 (622). 33 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521); cf. can. 5, Dz 815 (1555); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 27, 28, 39, 65 and 66, Dz 1027, 1028, 1039, 1065 and 1066 (1927, 1928, 1939, 1965 and 1966); Innocent X, Const. Cum Occasione, 31 May 1653, prop. 3, Dz 1094 (2003); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 38 and 39, Dz 1388-1389 (2438 and 2439).

Man’s Nature 71

grace, he could not take one step toward justice in God’s sight.”34 Man has never ceased to be free: not when he was created, nor after he lost original justice, and even less when endowed with the grace of redemption through Jesus Christ. But his freedom is not absolute, owing to the limitations of his bodily nature and the consequences of original sin. Furthermore, each person is conditioned by his education, society, culture, and the like. Despite these factors, we must hold that “the normal man does not possess a mere theoretical freedom, but truly has the use of freedom.”35 When a person doesn’t feel free, even if he’s only a five-year old child, he doesn’t feel responsible. If he does not feel free, he knows he can neither please nor offend God. Moral life is based on man’s capacity not only to distinguish between good and evil, but also to behave accordingly. Otherwise one could not properly speak of responsibility, merit or guilt, reward or punishment. Man would be little more than an irrational animal. What would be the sense of knowing moral laws, discerning good from evil, knowing the way that leads to God and the way that leads only to the dead-end of selfishness, if one’s decisions were but products of invisible and uncontrollable forces? Whence the Church states that “freedom, the highest of natural endowments, being the portion only of intellectual or rational natures, confers on man this dignity – that he is in the hands of his counsel and has power over his actions ... Indeed, man is free to obey his reason, to seek moral good, and to strive unswervingly after his last end. Yet he is also free to turn aside to all other things, and, by pursuing the empty semblance of good, to disturb rightful order and to fall headlong into the destruction which he has voluntarily chosen.”36

34 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 5, Dz 797 (1525); cf can. 3-5, Dz 813-815 (1553-1555). 35 Pius XII, Address, 10 April 1958; cf Address, 15 April 1953. 36 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888.

72 On Christian life

Man is a being made for God. Therefore, it is a mystery of divine Wisdom that, on creating man in his image and likeness (cf Gen 1:26), God has chosen to run the sublime “risk” of human freedom. We are obliged to contribute to making the Truth well known, thus corresponding to the goodness and trust God has shown us.37

Man as a corporeal creature Some people have tried to present the Christian way of life as something exclusively spiritual – or better, spiritualistic – something reserved for pure, extraordinary people who remain aloof from the contemptible things of this world, or, at most, tolerate them as something that the spirit just has to live alongside, while we are on this earth.38 Often, deformed versions of Christian life stem from a defective view of human nature and its relationship to God the Creator. That is why the Church has dogmatically defined the spirituality, immortality and freedom of the soul. That is also why she has firmly defended the body’s goodness as the work of an all-wise and almighty God, from whose hands only good things come.39 Even more: the Word, in order to redeem mankind, took on a body of flesh,40 taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men.41 He himself bore our sins in his body on the tree, that we might die to sin and live to righteousness.42 After this, to disparage the body is to similarly disparage its Redeemer, his suffering, his death and his glorious resurrection.43

37 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1965, 3. 38 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 113. 39 Cf Gen 1:31; Council of Braga, year 561, anathem. 6, 12 and 13, Dz 236, 242 and 243 (456, 462 and 463). 40 Col 1:22. 41 Phil 2:6-7. 42 1 Pet 2:24. 43 Cf Council of Chalcedon, Dz 148 (301); Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 429 (801).

Man’s Nature 73

When God calls a person to holiness he does not address the soul alone: the whole man is to be perfect. May the God of peace, St Paul prays, sanctify you wholly; and may your spirit (the whole being) and soul and body be kept sound and blameless.44 This solicitude on God’s part for the whole human creature is the ground for a Christian’s hope in the resurrection of the flesh. This is a divinely revealed truth,45 one of the fundamental articles of the Creed. If there is no resurrection of the dead, then Christ has not been raised; if Christ has not been raised, then our preaching is in vain and your faith is in vain.46 Catholic theology, basing itself on the soul’s nature and certain passages of Sacred Scripture, usually gives three arguments to show the appropriateness of this truth of faith.47 First, since the soul is only part of man, so long as it is separated from the body it cannot enjoy the complete happiness the whole person is destined to possess. Second, since souls are created to be joined to bodies, an unending separation would violate the kind of existence proper to human souls. Above all, it more accords with God’s wisdom, justice and mercy for souls to be united again to their bodies. In this way both soul and body, the entire man, can share in reward or punishment according to the person’s fulfilment of the moral law. In any event, it is a Catholic dogma that souls after death do not simply dissolve or lie dormant until the resurrection of the body. Rather they are immediately received into heaven or purgatory or, if they are impenitent in mortal sin, betake themselves

44 1 Thess 5:23. 45 Cf Symbolum Quicumque, Dz 40 (76); XI Council of Toledo, year 675, Dz 287 (540); St Leo IX, Letter Congratulamur Vehementer, 13 April 1053, Dz 347 (684); Innocent III, Letter Eius Exemplo, 18 December 1208, Dz 427 (797); Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 1, Dz 429 (801); Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (859); Benedict XII, Dogm. Const. Benedictus Deus, 29 January 1336, Dz 531 (1002). 46 1 Cor 15:13-14. 47 Cf Catechism of St Pius V, 1, 12, 5.

74 On Christian life

immediately to hell.48 The same teachings show how, in the Christian conception of man, the body is not a mere instrument of the soul. Although the body receives its power to act from the soul, it also contributes to the human person’s existence and development. Through the body man makes contact with earthly reality, which is to be subdued, worked on and sanctified, as God has ordained.49 Through the body man enters into communication with other souls and cooperates with his fellow men in building and developing the social community. Our Father has written: I often said to the university students and workers who were with me in the ’thirties that they had to know how to “materialize” their spiritual lives. I wanted to warn them of the temptation, so common then and now, to lead a kind of double life: on the one hand, an inner life, a life related to God; and on the other, as something separate and distinct, their professional, social and family lives, made up of small earthly realities. No, my children! We cannot lead a double life. We cannot be like schizophrenics if we want to be Christians. There is only one life, made of flesh and spirit. And it is that life which has to become, in both body and soul, holy and filled with God: we discover the invisible God in the most visible and material things.50 Finally, by means of the body man receives the grace of the sacraments. Therefore, particularly in the case of a Christian, the body is meant for the Lord, and the Lord for the body ... Do you not know that your bodies are members of Christ? ... Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit within you, which you have from God?

48 Cf Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (858); Benedict XII, Dogm. Const. Benedictus Deus, 29 January 1336, Dz 530 (1000); Council of Florence, Bull Laetentur Coeli, Dz 693 (1305-1306). 49 Cf Gen 1:28. 50 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 114.

Man’s Nature 75

You are not your own; you were bought at a great price. So glorify God in your body.51 However, due to its material nature, the creature’s perishable and limited existence is seen most clearly in the body. After the fall, the consequences of original sin are particularly evident in it. Our Lord himself points this out: the spirit is willing enough, but the flesh is weak.52 And St Paul describes the weight of the fomes peccati, which has also damaged the powers of the soul: I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members. Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death? Nothing else than the grace of God, through Jesus Christ our Lord.53 Grace, while respecting human nature, raises it to the supernatural order, healing the wounds of sin and infusing the theological virtues.54 Thus it gives Christians a deeper and more sublime vision of man. Our faith tells us that a soul in the state of grace is truly a divinized soul: God has granted to us his precious and very great promises, that through these you may ... become partakers of the divine nature (2 Pet 1:4). This theological notion of man is almost as far removed from a purely natural and human concept of him as God himself is removed from humanity. We are men of flesh and blood, not angels. But through the influence of the soul in grace, this divinization also affects the body, as a kind of anticipation of its glorious resurrection.55 Since man has a body, he has only a certain amount of time – how long, only God knows – to achieve the mission God entrusted to him by creating and blessing him with his grace. Only so long as the soul is

51 1 Cor 6:13-20. 52 Matt 26:41. 53 Rom 7:23-24. 54 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 and 800 (1528-1530). 55 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 3.

76 On Christian life

united to the mortal body can man merit, for the night is coming, when no one can work.56 In creating man, God infused into his body a soul that brings the light of reason and free will, with the power to love him and to rule and watch over the material world. God thereby enables man to reach his destiny as a creature, to turn to the worship of the living God, who made sky and earth and sea and all that is in them.57 And when God redeemed man by taking on human nature in all its integrity – for he never took on the nature of the angels, but he did take on the seed of Abraham58 – he confirmed and consecrated our human way of existing, which shows forth God’s perfection and glory. Worthy art thou, our Lord and God, to receive glory and honour and power, for thou didst create all things, and by thy will they existed and were created.59

56 John 9:4; cf Gal 6:10; Rev 2:10. 57 Acts 14:14. 58 Heb 2:16. 59 Rev 4:11.

## SOCIAL ASPECTS OF MORAL LIFE (I) {#cuaderno.2.6}

After creating man and setting him in the garden of Eden to till and keep it, the Lord God said: It is not good that the man should be alone; I will make him a helper fit for him ... The Lord God caused a deep sleep to fall upon the man, and while he slept took one of his ribs and closed up its place with flesh; and the rib which the Lord God had taken from the man he made into a woman.1 God’s plan for creation established that creatures themselves should play a part in the preservation and spread of the human race. Mankind was to fill the earth and subdue it and have dominion over the fish of the sea and over the birds of the air and over every living thing that moves upon the earth.2 Divine Wisdom also saw to it that the relationships binding men to one another would not be casual and passing, based on mere physical proximity. For these relationships give rise to the lasting and firm ties that form the basis of social life. Since God created man in such a way that, living alone, he cannot “provide himself with the necessary requirements of life ... divine providence ordained that man be born with the natural inclination to associate with others in family and civil society – among whom alone his various needs can be adequately supplied.”3

1 Gen 2:18-22. 2 Gen 1:28. 3 Leo XIII, Enc. Immortale Dei, 1 November 1885 (Dz 3165).

78 On Christian life

Man’s social nature “By his innermost nature man is a social being; and if he does not enter into relations with others he can neither live nor develop his gifts.”4 That man is meant to live in society has a deep moral significance transcending self-interest or utilitarianism. Scripture tells us of the benefits bestowed by life in society: Two are better than one; they get a good wage for their labour. If the one falls, the other will lift up his companion. Woe to the solitary man! For if he should fall, he has no one to lift him up.5 Again: A brother is a better defence than a strong city.6 But there is an even more radical truth that underlies and guarantees these benefits: “In the Creator’s plan, society is the natural means which every person can and must use for the attainment of his appointed end.”7 Life in society furnishes the material and spiritual means for a person to grow in moral perfection.8 A source of many benefits, it is also a source of obligations that bind a person to other individuals and to various communities: family, professional community, nation, Church, and so forth. These duties have a moral dimension, because they lead toward God, man’s final end: “I am the Alpha and the Omega, the beginning and the end,” says the Lord God.9 The whole world was created for God’s glory.10 Thus society itself, as well as man’s life in society, always points toward the Creator,

4 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 12; cf Leo XIII, Enc. Diuturnum illud, 29 June 1881, Dz 1856 (3151); Enc. Immortale Dei, 1 November 1885 (Dz 3165); Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888; Pius XI, Enc. Quadragesimo Anno, 15 May 1931, Dz 2270 (3743); John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3971, 3973, 3979); Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 18. 5 Eccl 4:9-10. 6 Prov 18:19. 7 Pius XI, Enc. Divini Redemptoris, 19 March 1937 (Dz 3772). 8 Cf Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 25. 9 Rev 1:8. 10 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, can. 5, Dz 1783 and 1805 (3002 and 3025).

Social Aspects of Moral Life (I) 79

source of being and life.11 And “after God, the common good is the first and last law of society.”12 “All individuals and intermediate groupings in a society have the duty of contributing to the common good,”13 which “embraces all the conditions of social life that enable individuals, families, and organizations to achieve complete and efficacious fulfilment.”14

The law of human solidarity and of charity This general obligation is grounded on the solidarity and mutual interdependence of men, stemming from their common origin and nature. These bonds were healed and strengthened by Christ through his incarnation and redemptive sacrifice offered for all men’s sins. As our Father teaches: We are all God’s children, we are all redeemed by Christ: here is the deepest reason for unity among men. No other reason is needed.15 “The very first page of Scripture records, with that nobility of expression native to it, that God crowned his work of creation by making man in his own image.16 The Bible further teaches that man was enriched with supernatural gifts and privileges, and thus destined for an ineffable and eternal happiness. It tells us moreover that the rest of the human race took its origin from the marriage of the first man with his partner. It describes to us, with vivid and significant wealth of detail,

11 Cf Leo XIII, Enc. Immortale Dei, 1 November 1885. 12 Leo XIII, Enc. Au milieu des sollicitudes, 16 February 1892; cf Letter N(o^tre Consolation, 3 May 1892; Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929, Dz 2209 (3692); Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937; Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939 (Dz 3782); John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3983-3985); Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 26, 71, 74, 75, 78; Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 6. 13 John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963 (Dz 3983). 14 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 74; cf John XXIII, Enc. Mater et Magistra, 15 May 1961. 15 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 19; cf Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 8. 16 Cf Gen 1:26-27.

80 On Christian life

how mankind was divided into various nations or tribes, scattered throughout the different parts of the world. And how, even when his creatures were guilty of a wretched apostasy, he did not abandon his fatherly care, but determined, in the decrees of his divine mercy, to unite them again to himself at a later time by a covenant of friendship.17 The Apostle to the Gentiles was the great champion of this truth, which represents all men as bound together in a single family. He tells the Greeks that God “made of one blood the whole race of men, to dwell upon all the face of the earth, determining the periods and the regions of their habitation on it, so that they might search for God.”18 All this gives us, in a remarkable way, direct insight into the unity of the human race. We see it as one in its common origin from the hand of a Creator: “One God and Father of us all, who is above all and reaches through all and dwells in all of us.”19 We see it as one in the nature all men share, involving the union of a body with an immortal, spiritual soul; we see it as one in the proximate end which is assigned to all of us, and in the common duties we are called upon to perform in this life. We see it as one in the sharing of a common habitat, this world of ours, whose resources every man has a natural right to make use of, insofar as they are needed for his preservation and self-development. And we see the human race as one in the possession of a common supernatural end, God himself, to whom we must all direct our actions, and in the means he has given to all of us in common, enabling us to reach that end at last. The same Apostle proves the unity of the human family from the links binding us to him who is the image of the invisible God, Jesus Christ, in whom all things were made.20 He proves it from the fact of our common redemption, telling us how Christ through his bitter passion restored to

17 Cf Gen 12:3. 18 Acts 17:26-27. 19 Eph 4:6. 20 Cf Col 1:16.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (I) 81

us God’s friendship, reconciling us to God: There is only one God, and only one mediator between God and men, the man Jesus Christ.21 And he, that same negotiator of all our peace and salvation, when preparing to make the supreme sacrifice of himself, uttered in the silence of the cenacle words designed to tighten the bonds of friendship between God and the human race; words that echo down the centuries from those sacred lips of his and spur on our hearts, unloving as they are and soiled with hatred, to heroic deeds of charity: This is my commandment, that you love one another as I have loved you.22 These utterances of eternal truth establish solid foundations and close bonds for our human oneness that is secured by the love of God and our divine Redeemer, from whom, one and all, we receive our salvation, for the building up of Christ’s body, so that we may all attain to the unity which lies in faith and in the knowledge of God, reaching perfect manhood, the full stature of Christ.23 We ought not to think of this unity, found “de iure” and “de facto” in the human race, as the unity of a heap: a person is not one grain of sand among countless other grains of sand. All men are united – by both natural and supernatural impulse and destiny – in harmonious and mutual relationships.”24

Consequences of human solidarity If we were to look at our moral duties in a purely individualistic way, independently of the lives and destinies of other men, we would be turning our backs on human solidarity, flying in the face of Catholic morality. Each person is bound to contribute to the common good of society to

21 1 Tim 2:5. 22 John 15:12. 23 Eph 4:12-13. 24 Pius XII, Enc. Summi Pontificatus, 20 October 1939.

82 On Christian life

the extent of his possibilities. He is expected to foster the development and progress of his fellow men in every aspect. The Church, it is true, does not dictate the particular temporal structures by which political societies are to be organized at any specific time in history. Neither does she indicate political or economic solutions to be adopted in any particular case. But the Church’s teaching authority has always opposed any ideology, such as secular humanism or Marxism, that attempts to remove social life from the realm of Christian morality.25 The bonds of human solidarity affect the whole person; they include spiritual as well as material aspects. Thus it would be wrong to limit social interaction to the material sphere alone, as though material wellbeing were the sole purpose of society. Such a restriction on man’s potential, whether as an individual or as a member of society, is completely unjustified and has repeatedly been rejected by the Church.26 She teaches that “human society is to be considered above all as a primarily spiritual reality.”27 As a result, one’s duties in society are not limited to seeking material well-being; on the contrary, this goal is to be pursued only so long as it does not impede access to spiritual values. The good things of this world, far from being an obstacle, should be a means for living morally and for attaining eternal happiness and salvation. Just as God created man to live in society, so too “he willed to make men holy and save them, not as individuals without any bond or link between them, but rather to make them into a people,”28 into the Church founded by Christ. “Although she does not, at present, include

25 Cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 36 and Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 36. 26 Cf Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890; Pius XI, Enc. Divini Redemptoris, March 19, 1937. 27 John XXIII, Enc. Pacem in Terris, 11 April 1963. 28 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 9; cf Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 32.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (I) 83

all men ... she is a most sure seed of unity, hope, and salvation for the whole human race.”29 Every human being is personally called to form part of the one true people of God. Each is invited to cooperate freely with this call, so as to reach the true happiness of heaven. There, “saved by grace, mankind will offer perfect glory to God as the beloved family of God and of Christ their brother.”30 We must learn to recognize Christ when he comes out to meet us in our brothers and sisters, in the people around us. No human life is ever isolated. It is bound up with other lives. No man or woman is a single verse; we all make up one divine poem that God writes with the cooperation of our freedom.31 Each person, and in a special way each Christian, should feel responsible for the moral behaviour of others. A person cannot lead a truly moral life unless he cares about those around him. God has made this fraternal and apostolic dimension so essential to moral life, that it would be inconceivable for a person to orient his life toward God while ignoring the bonds that unite him to his fellow men. For how can he who does not love his brother, whom he sees, love God whom he does not see?32 Our Father has encouraged us to consider that many great things depend – don’t forget it – on whether you and I live our lives as God wants.33 Among these great things are the happiness and eternal salvation of many souls.

Social duties, a part of one’s personal vocation Human society has as its foundation the common origin, destiny and interdependence uniting all mankind. But life in society, properly speaking, depends on the participation of each of its members, who places his

29 Ibid. 30 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 32. 31 Christ is passing by, 111. 32 1 John 4:20. 33 The Way, 755.

84 On Christian life

God-given talents at the service of the community. These gifts in turn are nurtured by the use of his intelligence, by his work and by the help he receives from the community, always with the help of God’s grace. Thus the progress of human society is not the result of a sum of many identical factors, but is achieved through the diverse contributions of all its different members.34 Nor is social development indifferent to God’s plan for the world. It responds, ultimately, to his will for man, and is a condition for the achievement of both spiritual and material goals on the part of the individual. Thus each person’s contribution to the common good of society is a true and inalienable moral obligation. “Life in society is not something accessory to man himself; through his dealings with others, through mutual service, and through fraternal dialogue, man develops all his talents and becomes able to rise to his destiny.”35 The various kinds of duties we have in society are thus also truly personal duties. On the last day, we will have to give an account of them to our Lord, the just Judge.36 These duties are not all of equal importance; they are hierarchically ordered, according to their object, their final end, and the circumstances accompanying them. Some oblige in strict justice; others are demanded by the virtue of charity. Since charity is an all-embracing and radical virtue in Christian life, it is not foreign to the exercise of the duties of justice, but includes and exceeds these duties. While the fulfilment of obligations of charity should not be confused with matters of strict justice, it is not always a question of mere counsel, since there is a true moral obligation involved. “The best way to fulfil one’s obligations of justice and charity is to contribute to the common good according to one’s means and the needs

34 Cf Leo XIII, Enc. Rerum Novarum, 15 May 1891. 35 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 25. 36 Cf Matt 25:31-46.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (I) 85

of others, even to the point of fostering and helping public and private organizations devoted to bettering the conditions of life. There is a kind of person who boasts of grand and noble sentiments and lives in practice as if he could not care less about the needs of society. There are many in various countries who make light of the social laws and directives and are not ashamed to resort to fraud and cheating to avoid paying just taxes and fulfilling social obligations. There are others who neglect the basic norms of social conduct, such as those regulating public hygiene and speed limits, forgetting that they are endangering their own lives and the lives of others by their carelessness. Let everyone consider it his sacred duty to count social obligations among man’s chief duties today and observe them as such. For the more closely the world comes together, the more widely do men’s obligations transcend particular groups and gradually extend to the whole world. This will be realized only if individuals and groups practice moral and social virtues and foster them in social living. Then, under the necessary help of divine grace, there will arise a generation of new men, the moulders of a new humanity.”37 God calls every human being to seek the highest degree of moral perfection: sanctity. Each person’s contribution to social progress, both material and spiritual, forms part of this vocation. “For, according to Christian doctrine, man, endowed with a social nature, is placed here on earth in order that, spending his life in society and under an authority ordained by God,38 he may cultivate and develop to the full all his faculties to the praise and glory of his Creator; and that, by fulfilling faithfully the function of his trade or other calling, he may attain to temporal and eternal happiness.”39

37 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 30. 38 Cf Rom 13:1. 39 Pius XI, Enc. Quadragesimo Anno, 15 May 1931.

## SOCIAL ASPECTS OF MORAL LIFE (II) {#cuaderno.2.7}

Christian morality has as its purpose the spreading of Christ’s kingdom on earth: “the kingdom of truth and life, the kingdom of holiness and grace, the kingdom of justice, love and peace.”1 “In this kingdom, creation itself will be delivered from the slavery of corruption into the glorious freedom of the children of God.2 The faithful must, then, recognize the inner nature, the value and ordering of the whole of creation to the praise of God. Even by their secular activity they must aid one another to greater holiness of life, so that the world may be filled with the spirit of Christ and may the more effectively attain its destiny in justice, in love and in peace.”3 The sanctification of society and the persons living in it will spontaneously result from each person’s effort to live the Gospel message integrally. God’s glory is a motive more than sufficient to make a Christian put his life at the service of others, fulfilling his personal vocation in the heart of society. But he will also need to grasp the true meaning of society and the moral implications of his fellowship with those around him.

Equality and pluralism There exists a basic equality among men “because all are endowed

1 Roman Missal, Preface for the Feast of Christ the King. 2 Cf Rom 8:21. 3 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 36.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (II) 87

with a rational soul and are created in God’s image; they share the same nature and origin and, being redeemed by Christ, they enjoy the same divine calling and destiny.”4 It is this shared human condition that explains our common dignity. Nobody is more important than anyone else, nobody! We are all equal! our Father said in a get-together with farm workers in Montefalco during his first trip to Mexico. And he added, each of us has the same worth: we are worth Christ’s blood. Think about that: how wonderful it is! For there aren’t different races or languages. There is only one race: the race of God’s children. A consequence flowing from the basic equality of all men, and from their common dignity, is that “all forms of social or cultural discrimination in basic personal rights on the grounds of sex, race, colour, social conditions, language, or religion must be curbed and eradicated as incompatible with God’s design.”5 Furthermore, there is a series of human rights that are universal and inviolable, and these must be equally recognized and protected in all cases; there are also duties corresponding to these rights, which are incumbent on every person to a greater or lesser degree according to his role and position within society. In practical terms, man must have “ready access to all that is necessary for living a genuinely human life: for example, food, clothing, housing, the right to choose freely his state in life and set up a family, the right to education, to work, to his good name, to respect, to proper knowledge, the right to act according to the dictates of his conscience and to safeguard his privacy, and rightful freedom even in matters of religion.”6 Only the teachings of the Catholic Church do full justice to the depth

4 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 29. 5 Ibid. 6 Ibid, 26.

88 On Christian life

and transcendence of these rights and duties, which must be put into practice and taught to others. “It is regrettable” – deplores the Church in the Second Vatican Council – “that basic personal rights are not yet being respected everywhere ... furthermore, while there are rightful differences between people, their equal dignity as persons demands that we strive for fairer and more humane conditions. Excessive economic and social disparity between individuals is a source of scandal and militates against social justice, equity, human dignity, as well as social and international peace.”7 At the same time, “it is evident that not all men are alike as regards physical capacity and intellectual and moral powers.”8 So, together with the basic equality of all men, there is also a diversity to be taken into account. This diversity is not purely accidental; it springs from God’s providence and is a determining factor in social progress. The social dimension of human life requires some type of order, so that within a society each person may be able to reach his own goal. Order, in this sense, goes beyond a mere juxtaposition of parts and a grouping of human contributions. It consists in an “ever-growing inner unity, which should never exclude the genuine diversity found among men, based as it is on the will of our Creator and on the supernatural norms he has established.”9 Any social theory attempting to eliminate not only unjust differences

– which should be remedied – but also legitimate ones, whether individual or social, would be just as mistaken as one attempting to maintain unjust disparities among men. Such a false view would betray the nature and goal of individual and social life. It would also go against the will of God, who not only respects our varied personalities

7 Ibid, 29. 8 Ibid. 9 Pius XII, Christmas Message, 1942.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (II) 89

and aptitudes: he has created them. He wishes man to grow and come to maturity while he exercises his freedom, and he wishes human history to follow its course.10 Differences of intelligence, character and culture between men, considered from the natural point of view, cannot simply be judged as limiting factors which modify the activity of a person and even his responsibility. They are part of one’s God-given vocation and therefore have a profound moral significance. Insofar as they include deficiencies, they should be corrected; while positive, distinctive aspects should be developed and used to serve others. All Christians “should be conscious of their specific and proper role in the political community: they should be a shining example by their sense of responsibility and their dedication to the common good.”11

Directed to society and directed to God If we look at man’s social nature from the perspective of Christian faith, we see clearly that each person’s life is meant to be oriented to the service of others out of love for God. “The social order requires constant improvement: it must be founded on truth, built on justice and enlivened by love.”12 Christian life, it is true, entails a personal commitment that each one lives facing God, with complete freedom and responsibility, eschewing anonymity. But it is not a life that isolates a person; every stage of Christian life is by its very nature a rejection of selfishness. Seeing others as God’s fellow children and our brothers and sisters, we are moved to authentic fraternal charity toward all. Otherwise, our Christian life would lack moral rectitude; it would fail to lead us to God, our true and final end.

10 From our Father, Letter 24 October 1965, 52. 11 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 75. 12 Ibid, 26.

90 On Christian life

Indeed, the light of faith and the stimulus of charity help a Christian to understand the depth and supernatural richness of the social ties that found the unity of mankind. A Christian is led to see all men as fellow creatures, as brothers and sisters in Christ – who is the first-born among many brethren13 – and as children of the one God and Father of us all, who is above all and through all and in all.14 However a Christian should never make the mistake of seeing “human existence as exclusively social in its aims. Society is not its own end; man is not doomed to place all his hopes in this life.”15 Each person’s very real responsibility for those around him should not exhaust his activities. “God did not create society as our final end, but to provide the means we need to perfect ourselves.”16 Service to the community cannot endow full meaning to one’s life: only when this service is directed to God’s glory and the good of souls does it acquire its full moral significance. “The community is willed by the Creator as the means to full development of personal and social endowments. Each person, in a spirit of give and take, has to employ them both for his own good and for that of others. There are some higher and more comprehensive values that cannot be reached by the individual on his own, but only by the community as a whole. These, too, are intended by God for the person’s natural and supernatural development and perfection. Departing from this order will loosen the supports on which the community is built, and thereby imperil its tranquillity and security, and even its very existence.”17 It would also be a mistake to think of social ties as running counter to the aims of the individual person. The Church upholds the primacy

13 Rom 8:29. 14 Eph 4:6. 15 Pius XII, Radio message on the fiftieth anniversary of the Encyclical Rerum Novarum, 1 June 1941. 16 Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890. 17 Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (II) 91

of the person, and in doing so she protects society as well. By reminding men of their social duties, she is encouraging them in their personal search for perfection. For “there is an interdependence between personal betterment and the improvement of society. Insofar as man by his very nature stands completely in need of life in society, he is and ought to be the beginning, the subject, and the object of every social organization. Life in society is not something accessory to man.”18 It is only in heaven that the well-being of the individual and society will harmonize perfectly. St Thomas Aquinas teaches that “eternal life consists in perfect vision of God, in highest praise and complete security. Also, there will be the joyful company of all the blessed. That society will be truly happy to the highest degree, for each will love his neighbour as himself, and rejoice over the good of his neighbour just as he would over his own.”19

The fundamentally personal character of morality While obliged to acknowledge the far-reaching consequences of man’s social nature, we must remember that morality is fundamentally personal. “For it is only individual human beings, not any association of them, that are endowed with reason and moral freedom.”20 Moral law, whether natural law or revealed, only becomes binding on the person through the mediation of his conscience. This is a “spiritual faculty that directs the will to choose, in each case, a course of action pleasing to God.”21 It is the proximate, immediate norm of morality, since “man has in his heart a law inscribed by God, by which he will be judged.”22

18 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 25. 19 St Thomas Aquinas, In Symb. Apost., a. 12. 20 Pius XI, Enc. Divini Redemptoris, 19 March 1937. 21 Pius XII, Radio Message, On the Formation of Christian Conscience in Youth, 23 March 1952. 22 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16; cf Rom 2:15-16.

92 On Christian life

Thus it is easy to see that “conscience needs education,”23 for “it is only in the words and in the will of Christ that each man will find the guidelines for an upright moral choice. It is for each man, and not just for man in a collective sense, that Christ is the way, the truth, and the life.”24 Conscience is always strictly personal;25 but this does not mean it is autonomous or independent, as the heralds of a ‘new morality’ might suggest.26 “Christians have, in fact, the wonderful responsibility of incarnating Christ’s truth and law. Catholic morality simply proposes that this spirit should illumine their personal lives, professional activities and, indeed, their total contribution to social and public life. As a consequence it relies to a great extent on each person’s initiative and responsibility.”27 Christ’s law is certainly universally valid and objective; it transcends particular circumstances of time and place, and takes into account man’s social nature. But its obligations fall on each person, since it is only the person, with his freedom and responsibility, who is capable of doing right or wrong. It is the person who is called to see and enjoy God, to know and love him. This is the ultimate aim of man, whose life on earth is a journey toward that goal. Centuries ago, the Church rejected false theories holding that, since the soul is spiritual, there must be only one soul, which is either identical to God or common to all men. These errors, denying as they did the basic truth that each individual soul is directly created by God, also undermined any personal morality and left no room for personal responsibility. In response to this error, the Church’s Magisterium recalled that “the

23 Pius XII, Radio Message, On the Formation of Christian Conscience in Youth, 23 March 1952. 24 Ibid. 25 Cf Ibid; Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16. 26 Cf Pius XII, Address to the International Congress of the World Federation of Young Catholic Women, 18 April 1952. 27 Ibid.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (II) 93
