human soul is not part of God, but was created from nothing;”28 and that it exists in each person as a reality distinct from other souls, since it is not a spirit shared in by all men, but an individual principle that God infuses into each human body.29 Following on the heels of a variety of pantheistic and monistic theories, Marxist materialism has for the past hundred years brought this erroneous conception to an even greater extreme. It has reduced the person to a mere part within an evolving material whole. All of history then becomes no more than pure evolution, governed by a blind, irrational force. Clearly this view leaves no room for true personal responsibility in the moral sphere. Faced with these misconceptions, it is necessary to reiterate that each person is endowed with an individual soul, as different from other souls as his body is different from other bodies,30 and that he can and should possess a direct and immediate relationship with God. We cannot, of course, forget the solidarity of the human race with its head, which helps us understand the mystery of the origin of sin, and also that of the redemption by which we are saved. Because of this solidarity, all the descendants of Adam – with the exception of the Virgin Mary31 – are affected by Original Sin from the moment of conception. Adam’s sin, which was a personal sin, “is transmitted by generation to all his posterity, and it dwells in every single human being.”32 In a parallel manner, all those who are born again in Christ through baptism receive the grace that makes them pleasing to God by virtue of the Passion of the new Adam, Christ the Redeemer, who is head of the Mystical Body.33

28 St Leo IX, Letter, Congratulamur Vehementer, 13 April 1053, Dz 348 (685). 29 Cf Fifth Lateran Council, Bull Apostolici Regiminis, Dz 738 (1440). 30 Cf Man’s Nature, chapter 5 of this volume. 31 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 32 Ibid, can. 3, Dz 790 (1513). 33 Cf Ibid, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 3, Dz 795 (1523); Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 9 June 1943.

94 On Christian life

But along with solidarity, we must also acknowledge that each person is unique and unrepeatable, and is capable of a personal relationship with God. This in turn founds individual responsibility which gives rise to personal merit or guilt. Personal sin can indeed have consequences for others, because of the common bond of human nature, and it can certainly increase the evil we all experience. But unlike Original Sin, its guilt only affects the sinner and not his descendants.34 As we read in Scripture: The soul that sins shall die. The son shall not suffer for the iniquity of the father, nor the father suffer for the iniquity of the son; the righteousness of the righteous shall be upon himself, and the wickedness of the wicked shall be upon himself.35 Likewise, the personal merit one gains by his good works can contribute to the benefit of others. And the good deeds of a Christian, who acts by and with Christ’s merits, contribute to the sanctification of the whole Church, his Mystical Body, although the application of these merits will depend on each person’s willingness to share in them.36 In one of his parables our Lord explains how the Master will ask his servants to account for the administration of the goods entrusted to them: each will be given the reward or punishment he deserves.37 In the same way, each of us “will have to give an account before God’s judgment seat of the good or evil that he has done38.”39 Our Father always stressed the fundamentally personal character of one’s own sanctification, ascetical struggle and union with God. These certainly affect others, but the role of each person’s conscience is vital

34 Cf St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop 52, Dz 1052. 35 Ezek 18:20. 36 Cf Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943. 37 Cf Luke 19:11-28. 38 Cf 2 Cor 5:10. 39 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 17.

Social Aspects of Moral Life (II) 95

and cannot be substituted.40 Holiness is not a community matter: if anyone were to say so, he would be mistaken. Sanctity is a personal encounter of each soul with God, and each soul for its part must contribute all it can: its deeds, its personal effort. My children, you have heard me say a thousand times: each soul needs to speak with God in a personal dialogue, never using the shield of anonymity or diluting one’s personal responsibility in the activity of a faceless crowd. All the divine ways of teaching of our Mother the Church – in her doctrine and liturgy – aim at fostering a personal dialogue of faith, love, adoration, atonement and petition. Even when formulating particular prayers for the faithful, she wishes each of them personally to turn to God with a child-like heart. Consequently, whenever she invites them to pray together, with the priest, it is to help them live as the united Mystical Body, but without neglecting to converse with Jesus in a trusting and filial way.41 God, who created man to live and develop in society, ratified and perfected man’s social dimension through the Word made flesh: Jesus “willed to share in human fellowship ... and sanctified human ties, above all family ties, which are the basis of social structures.”42 Our Creator has established that each of us, in accord with his or her particular vocation, should contribute to the personal and moral development of our brothers and sisters. We will have to give an account to God for the fulfilment of this task. He will render to every man according to his works: to those who by patience in well-doing seek for glory and honour and immortality, he will give eternal life.43

40 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 99. 41 From our Father, Letter 30 April 1946, 14-15. 42 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 32. 43 Rom 2:6-7.

## MORAL BEHAVIOUR {#cuaderno.2.8}

“Our divine Saviour has brought his truth and his grace to man, ignorant and weak as he is: truth, to point out the way that leads to his goal, and grace to give him the strength to reach it. Travelling on this road means, in practice, accepting Christ’s will and commandments and conforming one’s life to them in each of the actions, internal and external, which man’s free will chooses and determines.”1 Man is a moral creature. Created and raised to the order of grace and destined to a supernatural end that is God himself, man has received a twofold law: the natural law imprinted on his very nature2 and the supernatural law manifested to him by divine revelation.3 He has received the power to live his life freely in accordance with these two laws. In his free will “lies the cause of man’s being held the true author of whatever he does.”4 That is, he is responsible for his actions and morally accountable for the consequences flowing from them. Hence, according to Christian teaching, morality consists in the relationship between free human behaviour and the moral norm.

Morality of human actions God is the beginning and end, the alpha and omega of all things;5 to

1 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 2 Cf Rom 2:14-16. 3 Cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 2-6; Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16. 4 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888. 5 Cf Rev 1:8; 21:6.

Moral Behaviour 97

him belong salvation and glory and power,6 which he has communicated to men through his only begotten Son, Jesus Christ. There is salvation in no one else, for there is no other name under heaven given among men by which we must be saved.7 In him we have redemption through his blood, the forgiveness of our trespasses, according to the riches of his grace which he lavished upon us. For he has made known to us in all wisdom and insight the mystery of his will, according to his purpose which he set forth in Christ as a plan for the fullness of time, to unite all things in him, things in heaven and things on earth.8 The realization of God’s plans has not been left to the arbitrary choice of men, though we are all called to cooperate so that the treasures of grace may enrich all souls. Indeed, “these riches cannot be acquired or preserved except at the price of very specific moral obligations. Any other line of action would overlook a predominant principle which Jesus, our Lord and Master, always insisted upon. Jesus taught that to enter the kingdom of heaven it is not enough to say ‘Lord, Lord’: the will of his heavenly Father has to be done.9 He spoke of the ‘narrow gate” and of the ‘hard road’ leading to life.10 And he added: Strive to enter by the narrow door, for I say to you that many will seek to enter and will not be able.11 Christ gave the observance of his commandments as the touchstone and distinctive sign of love for himself.12 Likewise, to the rich young man who questioned him, he said: If you would enter life, keep the commandments. And to the further question ‘Which commandments?’ he replied: You shall not kill,

6 Rev 19:1. 7 Acts 4:12. 8 Eph 1:7-10. 9 Cf Matt 7:21. 10 Cf Matt 7:13-14. 11 Luke 13:24. 12 John 14:21-24.

98 On Christian life

you shall not commit adultery, you shall not steal, you shall not bear false witness, honour your father and your mother, and you shall love your neighbour as yourself.13 He imposed on anyone wishing to imitate him the condition of renouncing self and taking up one’s daily cross.14 He insisted that man must be ready to give up, for him and on account of him, all that is most dear: father, mother, one’s own children and even the ultimate good, life itself15.”16 God is not indifferent to human behaviour. Through the moral law he has shown us how to give him glory and thus to achieve our own happiness and perfection. We can obey God by keeping his law, in which case we do good; or we can rebel by doing wrong, committing sin. In this conformity or nonconformity with God’s will lies the basic division between good and bad human acts. He who does the will of my Father who is in heaven, he shall enter the kingdom of heaven.17 The moral evaluation of our actions does not consist, therefore, in arbitrarily labelling some “good” and others “bad.” The moral order is rather a reality based on God’s wisdom and goodness. “No one is good except by sharing in the life of the one who alone is good.”18 Good human actions share in God’s goodness. Bad actions, in contrast, go against the source and origin of all good. The Church considers heretical the assertion that “in every work, even an evil one – evil according to the guilt, as well as the punishment

– the glory of God is equally made manifest and resplendent.”19 Only

13 Matt 19:17-19. 14 Cf Luke 9:23. 15 Cf Matt 10:37-39. 16 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 17 Matt 7:21; cf Rom 2:13; Jas 1:22. 18 Indiculus, ch. 2, Dz 131 (240). 19 John XXII, Const. In Agro Dominico, 27 March 1329, prop. 4, Dz 504 (954).

Moral Behaviour 99

by observing the moral law can man truly glorify, praise and love God. For this is the love of God, that we keep his commandments. And his commandments are not burdensome.20 In the light of Christian faith the moral law is not a marvellous but unattainable ideal. The moral order is not a useful fiction but a reality, as real as the divine Wisdom that establishes it and his most holy Will that desires it. Real and objective also is our end, our nature and the grace that makes us God’s children.21

Moral value of the object and of the circumstances According to Catholic teaching, the moral value of a specific action is determined by both its object and the particular circumstances that accompany and influence it.22 The principal criterion for moral evaluation is the object, that is, the reality, personal or otherwise, toward which the action as such tends.23 For example, God is the object of prayer; rights and duties are the object of justice in general; truth is the object of veracity, etc. The circumstances influencing an action may come from the person performing it: for example one’s intention, certain qualities or prerogatives, family, social or professional bonds or obligations, and so on. Equally relevant circumstances are such external conditions as time, place, occasion, means employed or effects likely to follow. All of these factors influence the action. Quite often circumstances, particularly personal ones, increase or diminish the goodness or malice of an action, even changing what is termed the moral species.24 For example, to work under unfavourable external conditions is more meritorious; a

20 1 John 5:3. 21 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 82. 22 Cf St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 62, Dz 1062 (1962). 23 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 18, a. 2. 24 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 18, a. 10-11; q. 73, a. 7; De Malo, q. 2, a. 6-7.

100 On Christian life

task carried out to serve others or to give glory to God acquires a new dimension – that of fraternal charity or of adoration rendered to God, which is more noble than simply working hard. That circumstances help determine the morality of human actions has always been acknowledged by an upright Christian conscience. Moreover, the Church has authoritatively expounded this truth, for example, with regard to the sacrament of penance. The Council of Trent stipulated that “those circumstances also must be mentioned in confession which change the species of the sin, because without them the sins themselves are neither honestly revealed by the penitents, nor are they known to the judges; it would not be possible for them to judge rightly the gravity of the crimes and to impose the punishment proper to those penitents. Hence it is unreasonable to teach that these circumstances have been devised by idle men, or that one circumstance only must be confessed, namely, to have sinned against one’s brother.”25

Moral value of subjective dispositions A person’s subjective dispositions, such as the state of grace, natural and supernatural virtues, uprightness of intention, etc., also constitute the basis of Christian moral behaviour. They do so insofar as they strengthen a person habitually to fulfil the natural law. They also open the door to divine help, which is indispensable for living in accord with Christ’s law. Without grace man can do no deed leading to salvation.26 Christian life stems from true conversion of heart.27 This conversion deeply modifies the interior dispositions of each person. One is thereby

25 Council of Trent, Decr. De Poenitentia, ch. 5, Dz 899 (1681); cf ibid, can. 7, Dz 917 (1707); Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 21, Dz 437 (813). 26 Cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 9 and 20, Dz 182, 193 (379, 390); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, 4, 5, 11, 13, 16, can. 1-3, 22-23, Dz 793, 796, 797, 804, 806, 809-813, 832, 833 (1521, 1524, 1525, 1537, 1541, 1547, 1553, 1572, 1573). 27 Cf Deut 30:6; Jer 31:33; 32:39; Ezek 18:31; 36:25-26; Matt 15:19-20; Luke 8:15.

Moral Behaviour 101

prompted to fulfil God’s will in a stable and coherent way. Moral actions are not just so many isolated deeds without effects on their author. Good acts usually proceed from virtues. These operative habits inform the soul’s powers and grow with grace and the free cooperation of each person. Sins, on the other hand, are voluntary evil human acts, fruit of temptation and the unopposed remnants of original sin, which break the order established by God. Consequently, ascetical practices are very much needed to maintain good moral behaviour, aimed as they are at developing virtues rather than at merely aiding the external performance of sporadic good actions.28 Nevertheless, when we wish to determine the moral value of a specific action, we must go beyond interior supernatural dispositions. Even a sinner can do good,29 though the lack of sanctifying grace deprives his action of supernatural value. So too can a just man lose the state of grace.30 The moral gauge of our actions does not lie with the agent, but in the conformity of these actions (or lack thereof) with God’s will. “Man,” says St Augustine, “becomes good or evil in accordance with the goods he loves and seeks: virtue consists in loving what is worthy of being loved,”31 namely, what God proposes and reveals to us as good by means of his law. In case of doubt, interior dispositions can shed light on the moral perfection or imperfection of an action: whether there was full consent,

28 Cf Matt 12:33. 29 Cf Council of Constance, sess. XV, can. 16, Dz 642 (1216); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 6 and can. 7, Dz 798, 817 (1526, 1557); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 25, 35, 40, Dz 1025, 1035, 1040 (1925, 1935, 2040); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 38, 40, 48, 49, Dz 1388, 1390, 1398, 1399 (2438, 2440, 2448, 2449). 30 Cf Council of Vienne, Const. Ad Nostrum Qui, 6 May 1312, prop. 1-3 Dz 471-473 (891- 893); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, 16, can. 19, 20, 23, Dz 804, 810, 829, 830, 833 (1536, 1537, 1549, 1569, 1570, 1573). 31 Letter 155, 13.

102 On Christian life

complete awareness, blameworthy ignorance, etc. But this judgment will always be a simple evaluation of the subjective elements present in every moral action, and not a determination of its objective content.

Morality and intention The criterion or ultimate norm by which we assess the morality of human actions is God’s law, both natural and revealed. But we must remember, “it is through his conscience that man sees and recognizes the demands of the divine law.”32 Thus a person’s conduct will be good or bad as it accords or not with the judgment of conscience. Now the function of our conscience is not to create the law, but to know and apply it to the circumstances of each moment. Consequently all men have a grave obligation to form for themselves a correct and true conscience,33 modelled on Christ, the way, the truth and the life.34 “Expressions like ‘the judgment of a Christian conscience’ or ‘to judge in accordance with Christian conscience’ have this meaning: the norm for the ultimate and personal decision with regard to a moral action is taken from the word and will of Christ.”35 Catholic teaching stresses both the objective character of moral norms and the importance of the personal relationship of each soul with God. What is totally opposed to this teaching is so-called ‘situation ethics’,”which asserts that “in the determination of conscience, each individual deals directly with God. Thus the issue is decided without the intervention of any law, authority, community, cult or denomination in any manner whatsoever. According to this viewpoint, the decision concerns only the ‘I’ of a man and the ‘I’ of a personal God – not the

32 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 3. 33 Cf Ibid. 34 John 14:6. 35 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952.

Moral Behaviour 103

God of law, but rather God the Father with whom man is to unite himself with filial love. Looked at in this way, the decision of conscience is a personal risk that is taken according to the individual’s own knowledge and evaluation and with full sincerity before God. These two elements, a right intention and a sincere response, are what God considers: the action itself is of no importance to him. Consequently the response can be to change Catholic principles for other ones, such as obtaining a divorce, having an abortion, refusing obedience to the competent authority in the family, the Church, the State .... As we have described it, this new ethics is so far removed from faith and Catholic principles that even a child who knows his catechism can see and realize it.”36 No act that is wrong in itself can become good, however lofty the end proposed, however spontaneously or sincerely performed. The Church’s teaching authority has repeatedly declared that an evil act cannot be justified by a good intention.37 Morality does not fundamentally depend on the person, but on the divine law of which one’s conscience is only the interpreter. Consequently, Catholic teaching opposes situation ethics in three fundamental ways: “First, we concede that God always and above all wishes us to have a right intention. But that is not enough: he also wants actions which are good. Secondly, it is never permitted to do evil so that good may come of it;38 whereas situation ethics, perhaps without realizing it, follows the principle that the end justifies the means. Thirdly, there can be circumstances in which a man, and especially a

36 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952. 37 Cf Council of Constance, Bull Inter Cunctas, 22 February 1418, Dz 664 (1254); Fourth Lateran Council, ch. 22, Dz (815); Urban VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 23 July 1639, Dz (1998); Pius IX, Syllabus, 8 December 1864, prop. 64, Dz 1764 (2964); Pius XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 24 July 1929, Dz 2201 (3684); Pius XII Address, 29 October 1951; Paul VI, Enc. Humanae Vitae, 25 July 1968. 38 Cf Rom 3:8.

104 On Christian life

Christian, cannot be unaware that he should sacrifice everything, even life itself, to save his own soul. All the martyrs remind us of this; and martyrs are very numerous, even in our own time.”39 Thus there are moral norms so absolute and so binding on our conscience that we can never disregard them without grave sin. Consequently, Christ pointed out to those who wished to follow him that they had to be ready for anything, even giving up life itself: Whoever would save his life will lose it and whoever loses his life for my sake will find it.40 An upright intention is not sufficient to make an action good, among other reasons, because a person’s intention is not upright if he does not sincerely seek to know, love and do God’s will. This will is clearly shown for all, in the sources of Revelation and in the Magisterium of the Church for a Christian, and it is shown in the natural moral order for all men. A person who neglects the habitual formation or the actual exercise of his conscience and confers on his own personal decisions a divine value without further ado, while following in fact his limited insights or his own inclinations, can never have a right intention. It is not enough to appeal to a vague and ill-defined motive of love, of charity, that would transcend any moral imperative. Christ’s words are clear: if you would enter eternal life keep the commandments (Matt 19:17). He who has my commandments and keeps them, he it is who loves me (John 14:21).41

Need for an upright intention Although an upright intention is not sufficient to make an action good, it is always necessary. First of all because we do not always have absolute moral norms that apply independently of specific circumstances.

39 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952. 40 Matt 16:25. 41 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 86.

Moral Behaviour 105

A right intention is then necessary for judging with a correct conscience, since one should judge only after attentively examining the corresponding moral norms as they apply to each specific case. Only when a person is determined to seek God’s glory above any personal interest, can he prevent his passions from upsetting the dictates of his conscience, blinding his understanding or sidetracking his will.42 A good intention ensures the goodness of one’s decisions; and in case of error, it facilitates a prompt and cheerful correction. In a word, Christian morality “reveals the meaning of human activity and it includes all that might be just and good in so-called situation ethics, while avoiding all its confusion and errors.”43 Besides demanding the formation of an upright and true conscience, Christian life also necessarily leads us to refer all things to God. Whatever your task, work heartily, as serving the Lord and not men, knowing that from the Lord you will receive the inheritance as your reward; you are serving the Lord Christ. For the wrong-doer will be paid back for the wrong he has done, and there is no partiality with God.44 So, whether you eat or drink, whatever you do, writes St Paul, do all for the glory of God.45 The Apostle’s words reflect a specific moral duty: all men are obliged to direct their lives and conduct toward God, the final end of every creature. But it is impossible to do so in a conscious and voluntary manner in each and every one of our actions. We need to give direct attention to the task at hand, or to future projects, and to matters related with our work, family, social relations, and so on, which fill the greater part of our day. Since God does not ask the impossible,46 our habitual intention to serve him suffices to comply with the divine command. This intention, in fact, is implicit in all our actions that are not bad in themselves, unless we

42 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., II-II, qq. 47-57; Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952. 43 Pius XII, Address, 18 April 1952. 44 Col 3:23-25. 45 1 Cor 10:31. 46 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1536).

106 On Christian life

consciously reject it. An action that is morally indifferent in itself becomes illicit only when we deliberately and exclusively seek in it our own enjoyment, satisfaction or profit. A specific application of this principle is the Magisterium’s teaching that it is not licit “to eat and drink to satiety for pleasure only, even if health is not damaged.”47 The Catholic Church teaches that while there may be naturally good actions – actions, that is, which are pleasing to God, though not meritorious as regards grace, since they are not inspired by supernatural charity – sinful actions, in contrast, are always a personal affront to God. The Church has rejected the notion of sin that only infringes the natural order but does not offend the Creator.48 Mortal sin is always iniquity, an offence against God. It is the cause of destruction of friendship with God, turning man again into a child of wrath. Venial sin, from which no man is free without a special privilege, makes charity grow cold and easily inclines the soul toward mortal sin.49 Human existence is so radically ordained to God that in actual fact we draw either closer to or move further away from God in all our actions, whether internal or external. Either our love for God becomes more deeply rooted or, on the contrary, it grows weaker, and is even quenched. We cannot avoid making a choice between good and evil, between love for God or love for self. He who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters.50 Christian life consists in deciding, under the influence of grace, always to fulfil God’s most holy will, thus following the example of Christ: I seek not my own will but the will of him who sent me.51

47 Cf Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 2 March 1679, prop. 8, 9, Dz 1158-1159 (2108-2109); cf Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 24 August 1690, prop. 1, Dz 1289 (2290). 48 Cf Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 24 August 1690, prop. 2, Dz 1290 (2291). 49 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 86. 50 Luke 11:23. 51 John 5:30.

## FREEDOM AND MORAL RESPONSIBILITY {#cuaderno.2.9}

“Moral obligations are an essential part of the divine Redeemer’s revelation; this revelation has been entrusted not to each man individually, but to his Church, to which he has given the mission of leading men to embrace faithfully that holy deposit. It was also to the Church herself, and not to each individual, that the assistance of the Holy Spirit was promised, so that revelation might be preserved from errors and distortions. A wise providence this was, because the Church, a living organism, can thus securely and easily both illumine and penetrate deeply into moral truths and, while maintaining their substance intact, apply them to changing conditions of time and place.”1 In moral matters the Magisterium of the Church enjoys the same functions and prerogatives that it has in defining and explaining the truths of faith. It authentically and infallibly interprets the revealed divine word and the precepts of natural law. Nevertheless, when regulating moral behaviour, usually the Magisterium indicates not so much the content of moral principles as how to apply them in specific circumstances in order to resolve a specific moral problem. But in doing so, it always appeals ultimately to the truth of moral principles, and, through the problems at issue, illustrates how these principles should apply to Christian life. Such is the case, for example, in Catholic teaching on moral imputability or responsibility.

1 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952.

108 On Christian life

Personal freedom, the basis of responsibility The Church’s teaching on moral responsibility in human acts – on their merit or guilt, which bring either reward or punishment – is based on the principle of freedom. God wishes to be served in freedom.2 Without freedom there is no responsibility; likewise there should be no freedom that is not responsible, our Father often said. This universal principle of natural law refers not only to specific areas of human endeavour, such as the economy, politics and social relations, but to all moral behaviour. As the Church’s Magisterium has stated repeatedly, man was created free;3 his freedom survived the fall, although in a weakened state.4 Man remains free under the influence of grace, even efficacious grace.5 Grace heals our nature and strengthens our inclination to good, which does not come easy to us because of sin. But God does not impose his grace; we must decide to correspond to it. In creating us, God runs the risk and adventure of our freedom. He has willed that history be a real history, made up of authentic decisions, and not just a fiction or a game. Each man must experience his own autonomy, with all life’s chances, guesswork and, at times, perplexity.6 “Freedom is the noblest good of our nature, for it belongs exclusively to intelligent or rational beings. It bestows on man the

2 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 14. 3 Cf Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 1, Dz 316 (621); Indiculus, Dz 130 (239). 4 Cf Indiculus, ch. 1, 4, 9, Dz 129, 130, 133, 134, 140 (238, 239, 242, 243, 247); Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 8, Dz 181 (378); Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 2, Dz 317 (622); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521); can. 5, Dz 815 (1555); St Pius VI, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1657, prop. 27, 28, 39, 65, 66, Dz 1027, 1028, 1039, 1065, 1066 (1927, 1928, 1939, 1965, 1966); Innocent X, Const. Cum Occasione, 31 May 1653, prop. 3, Dz 1094 (2003); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 38 and 39, Dz 1388 and 1389 (2438 and 2439). 5 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 5 and can. 4-6, Dz 797, 814-816 (1525, 1544- 1556); Innocent X, Const. Cum Occasione, 31 May 1653, prop. 3-5, Dz 1093, 1095 (2002-2004). 6 From our Father, Riches of the Faith, published in ABC, Madrid, 2 November 1969.

Freedom and Moral Responsibility 109

dignity of self-determination and control over his own actions.”7 Man is fully responsible for his own decisions, be they good or bad. No one can say: “Because of the Lord I left the right way,” for he will not do what he hates. Do not say, “It is he who led me astray,” for he has no need of a sinful man. The Lord hates all abominations, and they are not loved by those who fear him. It was he who created man in the beginning, and he left him in the power of his own inclination. If you will, you can keep the commandments, and to act faithfully is a matter of your own choice.8 The importance of personal freedom and responsibility in all moral life, clearly acknowledged by human reason, is confirmed by revelation. In the plan of redemption, God’s infinite love not only opens to us the gates of heaven, but also heals and elevates human freedom, injured as it is by sin. With the freedom wherewith Christ has made us free,9 we have been rescued from the triple slavery of sin, the law and death. God, writes St Paul, has delivered us from the dominion of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of his beloved Son, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins.10 Though we still bear the wounds of original sin, particularly concupiscence, now that redemption has taken place, we can enter into living contact with Christ through the sacrament of baptism. We are freer to fulfil the moral law, because we can more readily know it, love it and respond docilely to the impulses of grace that awaken and strengthen our decision to do God’s will.11 The New Law brought by Christ is a law of freedom. In contrast

7 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888. 8 Sir 15:11-15; cf Matt 19:17; Jas 1:13-18. 9 Gal 5:1. 10 Col 1:13-14. 11 Cf Rom 8:2.

110 On Christian life

with the Old Law, good and holy though imperfect as it was,12 the new covenant not only points out the path to be followed, but also disentangles man from evil and gives him the strength needed to do good and reach his end. Live as free men, yet without using your freedom as a pretext for evil.13 Before and after redemption, united or not to Christ, man is free and responsible for his actions. So essential are freedom and responsibility to human behaviour that mere conformity to the law is not enough to make our actions fully moral; to be so, they must be truly free. We need to recognize the moral value of the end and the means proposed to reach it, and then decide to seek that end or to use such means, assuming responsibility for all the consequences flowing from our choice. The freer and more deliberate an action (or omission) is, the more responsible is its author. However, this criterion cannot be applied without due regard for the specific person in question, and above all for his virtues. Virtue not only strengthens a person’s freedom, confirming his will in goodness. In addition, virtue won by his efforts in cooperation with grace also makes a man more responsible for his actions. A virtuous person usually performs good deeds with greater ease (though special trials may come, when God makes increased demands on souls) and with greater responsibility.

Responsibility, a condition for freedom Personal freedom is essential for Christian life. But do not forget, my children, that I always speak of a responsible freedom.14 If freedom is essential to responsibility, it is also clear that where a responsible attitude is missing, freedom cannot be rightfully claimed.

12 Cf Rom 7:7-25; 3:20; 1 Tim 1:8; Gal 3:10-14. 13 1 Pet 2:16. 14 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 117.

Freedom and Moral Responsibility 111

Moreover, while the responsible exercise of personal freedom is praiseworthy and good, to make decisions without assuming personal responsibility is sinful. “In availing of any freedom,” says the Second Vatican Council, “men must respect the moral principle of personal and social responsibility.”15 A person is responsible for his actions because he is their author. His decisions, his actions and their consequences, whether intended or not, depend on him personally, exclusively and inalienably. But a person is also responsible for giving an account of the use he has made of his freedom. First, he must answer to God who, as in the parable of the steward, asks everyone to account for his stewardship.16 Secondly, he must answer to others, to a greater or lesser extent depending on the bonds of justice and charity that unite him to them. For example, the reality of Christ’s Mystical Body and the consequent communion of saints gives rise to a particular responsibility among the faithful to use their freedom to grow in Christian life. Thanks to his conscience, a man’s actions are morally attributed to him. Conscience is “the spiritual faculty that in each particular case points out to the will – so it can choose and decide – which acts conform to God’s will.”17 It enables human actions to be fully moral: the free adherence, or non-adherence, to God’s law. An ancient and widespread tradition in theology gives the name ‘formal morality’ to the relationship between free actions and law. ‘Material morality’, on the other hand, denotes actions lacking freedom or due knowledge, performed by a person either incapable of possessing, or not obliged to possess, the corresponding moral knowledge.18 It is only in the first case that actions are morally imputable and consequently deserving of

15 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 7. 16 Cf Luke 16:2, 19-31; Matt 25:31-46. 17 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952. 18 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, Quaest. Quodlibet, 8, q. 6, a. 15.

112 On Christian life

God’s reward or punishment. To be able to know God’s law and freely accept it gives man a privileged situation in this world: we are the only material creatures capable of glorifying God formally, consciously and freely. Not only does man mirror divine perfections more fully than other material creatures, but he can also recognize and praise God, who made heaven and earth, the sea, and all that is in them; who keeps faith forever.19 No one can deprive a man in grace of the legitimate pride of being the object of God’s love or of the possibility of responding to that love. “True freedom is an exceptional sign of the image of God in man. For God willed that man should be left in the hands of his own counsel so that he might of his own accord seek his Creator and freely attain his full and blessed perfection by cleaving to him.”20

Responsibility, merit and guilt An immediate consequence of Catholic teaching on moral responsibility is the reality of merit or blame. He who cannot make use of his legitimate freedom has no right to a reward for his good actions, nor can he receive punishment for his bad actions or for his omissions.21 Why would God have proclaimed his Law and made man free and responsible for its fulfilment under the influence of grace, if human choices did not entail certain consequences before God, even the winning or losing of his friendship? He who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters.22 In the parable of the talents, our Lord points out that each servant receives a reward proportionate to the profit gained by his own efforts.23 He who sows

19 Ps 145:6. 20 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 17. 21 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 52. 22 Luke 11:23; cf Mark 9:40; Matt 6:24. 23 Cf Luke 19:11-25.

Freedom and Moral Responsibility 113

sparingly will also reap sparingly, and he who sows bountifully will also reap bountifully.24 Every free and morally good deed merits a reward.25 For it to be also supernaturally meritorious, a person must perform it in the state of grace or friendship with God,26 receive the impulses of actual grace,27 and do it for a supernatural reason.28 No creature on its own, without God’s help, can perform any supernaturally meritorious act. On the other hand, by good works born of divine assistance and his own free cooperation, a Christian acquires, as it were, a right to grow in grace and to receive whatever divine help he needs. And if he perseveres in good works to the end, responding to God’s constant help, God will give him the grace of eternal salvation and increased glory in heaven.29 By his meritorious actions, man can satisfy divine justice offended by sin. The catechism of the Council of Trent teaches: “In satisfaction two things are particularly required: the one, that he who satisfies be in a state of grace, a friend of God, since works done without faith and charity cannot be acceptable to God; the other, that the works performed be of their own nature somewhat painful or laborious. They are compensation for past sins and – to use the words of the holy martyr Cyprian – ‘the redeemers of past sins’,30 as it were, and must therefore

24 2 Cor 9:6. 25 Cf Matt 5:12; 19:29; 25:34-35; Luke 6:38; Rom 2:6; 1 Cor 3:8; Eph 6:8; Col 3:24; Heb 10:35; 11:6. 26 Cf 1 Cor 13:1-3; Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 16 and can. 26 and 32; Dz 809, 836 and 842 (1545, 1576 and 1582); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 13-15, Dz 1013-1015 (1913-1915). 27 Cf 1 Cor 12:3; Phil 2:13; Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 9, 20, Dz 182 and 193 (379 and 390); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1-3, 5, 16; can. 1-3, Dz 793- 795, 797, 809, 811-813 (1521-1523, 1525, 1545, 1551-1553). 28 Cf Matt 10:42; 19:29; Mark 9:40; Luke 9:48; 1 Cor 10:31; Col 3:17. 29 Cf 2 Cor 9:6; Rev 22:12; Jas 1:12; Council of Florence, Bull Laetentur Coeli, Dz 693 (1304); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 10 and 16, can. 24 and 32, Dz 803, 809-810, 834 and 842 (1535, 1545-1546, 1574 and 1582). 30 St Cyprian, Epist., 35.

114 On Christian life

be in some way disagreeable. It does not, however, always follow that they are painful or laborious to those who undergo them. The influence of habit, or the intensity of divine love, frequently renders the soul insensible to the greatest difficulties. Such works do not therefore cease to be satisfactory. It is the privilege of the children of God to be so inflamed with his love, that while undergoing the cruelest tortures, they are either insensible to them, or bear them with the greatest joy.”31 The possibility of supernatural merit shows clearly God’s infinite mercy and man’s absolute dependence on his Creator and Redeemer. The Church states that “recompense is due for good works if these are carried out; however, for them to be carried out they have to be preceded by grace, and grace is due to no man.”32 In other words, grace is the necessary premise and indispensable basis for the supernatural good works whereby a Christian merits eternal life. “God’s goodness towards all men is such that he wants his gifts to be their merits.”33 Hence to think often of the reward awaiting us in heaven is neither wrong nor selfish. Rather it shows love and gratitude toward God. Do everything unselfishly, for pure Love, as if there were neither reward nor punishment, our Father recommends. But in your heart foster the glorious hope of heaven.34 To think of the reward God keeps in store for us if we are faithful is an exercise of faith, an expression of hope, a proof of true love. To hope for heaven is part of the virtue of hope, a theological virtue, which we cannot acquire by our own effort; God gives it to us. Hope presupposes faith and gives us a right to love. Faith, hope and charity: the three theological virtues in act. Just as good moral actions merit a reward, so bad actions, or sins, engender the guilt that deserves, in its turn, punishments of varying

31 Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 5, 73. 32 Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 18, Dz 191 (388). 33 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 16, Dz 810 (1548); cf Indiculus, ch. 9, Dz 141 (248). 34 The Way, 668.

Freedom and Moral Responsibility 115

severity, according to the malice of the faults. Besides depriving the sinner of God’s friendship, sanctifying grace and his previously won merits,35 mortal sins make him deserving of eternal punishment in hell.36 Venial sins, in contrast, do not destroy friendship with God. According to many theologians, especially St Thomas, they do not even directly diminish either charity or sanctifying grace.37 Nevertheless, venial sins make it difficult to practice the theological virtues, create a resistance to actual graces, and render one liable to temporal punishment, which has to be satisfied in this life or, after death, in purgatory.38 In either case, to be pardoned one must be sorry for any offences against God, be they grave or slight. Remorse, the spirit of contrition, even though it bring bitterness to the soul, is a great gift from God so that the sinner may convert and have recourse to the ever-open source of divine mercy (cf 2 Cor 7:8-13; Luke 15:11-32; 7:35-60; 24:40-43, etc.). On the other hand, to fulfil the will of God and achieve holiness, it is not enough to avoid mortal sins. We must also want to avoid venial sins and strive positively to do what God wants of us.39 “It is only in freedom that man turns himself towards what is good.”40

35 Cf Ezek 18:24; Council of Constance, Errors of John Huss, 2, Dz 628 (1202); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 15 and can. 27, Dz 808 and 837 (1544 and 1577); Decr. De Sacramentis in Genere, can. 15 (De Bapt.), Dz 862 (1619); Decr. De Poenitentia, ch. 1 and 5, Dz 894 and 899 (1668 and 1679); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 24 August 1690, prop. 2, Dz 1290 (2291; Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2318 (3891). 36 Cf Matt 25:41 and 45; Mark 9:42; Rev 14:11; 20:22; Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780); Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (854); Benedict XII, Const. Benedictus Deus, 29 January 1336, Dz 531 (1002); Council of Florence, Bull Laetentur Coeli, Dz 693 (1304); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 24-25, Dz 834-835 (1574-1575); Decr. De Poenitentia, can. 5, Dz 915 (1705). 37 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., II-II, q. 24, a. 10. 38 Cf Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 410 (780); Council of Florence, Bull Laetentur Coeli, Dz 693 (1304); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 30, Dz 840 (1580); Decr. De Purgatorio, Dz 983 (1820). 39 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 87. 40 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 17.

116 On Christian life

When man seeks God by observing his moral obligations, he is “moved and drawn in a personal way from within, and not by blind impulses in himself or by mere external constraint.”41 He becomes responsible for his own actions, and by God’s grace, and more particularly by Christ’s grace and merits, becomes worthy of what God has prepared for those who love him.42 “Hence it can be seen how such great weight and dignity belong to the virtuous actions of good men. For Christ our Lord continually infuses his grace into the devout soul united to him by charity, as the head to the members, or as the vine through the branches. This grace always precedes, accompanies and follows our good works, and without it we can have no merit, nor can we satisfy God. Hence nothing seems wanting to the just. Through their works done by the power of God, they are able to satisfy God’s law, as far as their human and mortal condition will allow, and merit eternal life, to the fruition of which they will be admitted if they die in the state of God’s grace. Well-known are the words of the Saviour: he that shall drink of the water that I will give him, shall not thirst for ever; but the water that I will give him shall become in him a fountain of water, springing up onto life everlasting43.”44

41 Ibid. 42 1 Cor 2:9. 43 John 4:14. 44 Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 5, 72.

## FREE WILL AND MORAL KNOWLEDGE {#cuaderno.2.10}

“From the beginning, God placed in all creatures an innate desire of pursuing their own happiness. All, by natural impulse, seek and desire their own end, from which they never stray unless impeded by some external obstacle. This desire of seeking God, the author and father of happiness, was in the beginning all the more noble and stable in man, endowed with reason and judgment as he is. But while irrational creatures, which were by nature good at their creation, remained in that original state and condition, unhappy man went astray and lost original justice, with which he had been supernaturally gifted and adorned by God, and saw his soul’s singular inclination toward virtue obscured. All, says the psalmist, have gone aside, they are become unprofitable together; there is none that does good, no, not one.1 For the imagination and thought of man’s heart are prone to evil from his youth.2 Hence, it is not difficult to perceive that of himself no man is wise unto salvation; that all are prone to evil.”3

Obstacles to moral life To understand correctly the nature of moral life, we must recognize

1 Ps 13:3. 2 Gen 8:21. 3 Catechism of the Council of Trent, part 4, ch. 12, 3.

118 On Christian life

in every human being the co-existence of an inclination to sin and a true desire for happiness that God alone can satisfy. Everybody personally experiences the contrast between these two deeply rooted, conflicting tendencies. St Paul wrote: So I find it to be a law that when I want to do right, evil lies close at hand. For I delight in the law of God, in my inmost self, but I see in my members another law at war with the law of my mind and making me captive to the law of sin which dwells in my members.4 This tension running all through moral life is a basic fact. Without faith to enlighten us, this fact would remain undeciphered. We would never realize it stems from our first parents’ sin. We all inherit original sin, and in its wake, a wounded human nature that has been further damaged by our personal sins. Original sin manifests itself in all our powers and faculties, since “the whole of man, both body and soul, was changed for the worse.”5 Even though our intelligence retains its ability to know God through created realities,6 it has grown dim and falls easily into error. Our will, in turn, does not lose its freedom, but we cannot use that freedom for good without a struggle. Our will is weakened and tends toward evil; it lacks firmness in confronting difficulties and ruling disordered tendencies. Thus, we are all born with disorder in our understanding, will and passions; this disorder, “though not sin, stems from sin and inclines to sin.”7 Sacred Scripture tells us that militia est vita hominis super terram:8 our life on earth is unending warfare against an enemy within the walls of our own city. We do not reach our end spontaneously and directly. To achieve happiness by giving God the glory due him, we must be on

4 Rom 7:21-23. 5 Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 1, Dz 174 (371); Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 1, Dz 788 (1511). 6 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1785 (3004); can. 1, Dz 1806 (3026). 7 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 8 Job 7:1.

Free will and Moral Knowledge 119

guard,9 ready for the battle to rid ourselves of the old man.10 Without personal effort to overcome the obstacles arising from our proneness to sin (the fomes peccati), we cannot develop a moral life firmly set on goodness.11 The harm wrought by original sin is such that merely human efforts to win this battle are doomed to failure. In the state of fallen nature, man could count on the Mosaic Law to show him the good to be done, but it did not provide the grace necessary to heed it.12 But in his infinite mercy, when the time had fully come, God sent forth his Son, born of woman, born under the law, to redeem those who were under the law.13 What was impossible became possible through Christ’s redeeming grace.14 By this grace, man recovers – and more than recovers – his original dignity. He is reborn to supernatural life and becomes a child of God. We share in redemption through baptism, the sacrament whereby we die to sin and rise to a new life.15 However, even with this grace, “concupiscence or the tendency to sin remains in the baptized; but since it is left to test man, it has no power to injure those who do not consent and who, by the grace of Christ Jesus, resist.”16 Even in the state of redeemed nature a Christian has to cooperate freely with God to lead a morally upright life.17 By so doing, he merits the goal for which he was created and redeemed.

9 Cf Matt 26:41. 10 Cf Eph 4:22. 11 Cf Eph 4:22-24; Col 3:9-10; 1 Cor 9:24-27. 12 Cf Rom 7:14-23. 13 Gal 4:4-5. 14 Cf Rom 8:2-4. 15 Cf Rom 6:3-4. 16 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 17 Cf John 8:31-35.

120 On Christian life

Importance of moral knowledge Man’s cooperation with God’s grace to overcome the obstacles to truly Christian behaviour must be directed first toward knowing the norms and principles of both natural and revealed moral law. Since no one can desire or reject what he does not know, without knowledge of the law moral life is impossible. Only by knowing God’s will can man hope to obey it consciously and freely. That is why the Church does not recognize any moral value in the actions of children before they reach the age of reason. The same holds true for adults who, without any guilt on their part, are bereft of reason.18 Likewise a fully endowed person who acts inadvertently is not responsible – at least not fully responsible

– for what he does. He can of course be culpable if he is obliged to carry out a certain task or to know his duties, yet fails to use the available means to do so. “It is in accordance with their dignity that all men, because they are persons, that is, beings endowed with reason and free will and therefore bearing personal responsibility, are both impelled by their nature and bound by a moral obligation to seek the truth, especially religious truth.”19 This is a grave obligation that “binds man’s conscience.”20 The pressing nature of this obligation is accentuated by the ravages caused by ignorance, particularly in the areas of morality and religion. It could well be said, our Father wrote, that the greatest enemy of God is ignorance, since we can love God only when we know him. Ignorance is the origin of many evils and a great obstacle to the salvation of souls.21 For there are some who, instead of giving sound doctrine, take advantage of the ignorance of others to spread confusion.

18 Cf Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 2, Dz 102 (223); Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 4, Dz 791 (1514). 19 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 2; cf ibid, 1 and 3. 20 Ibid, 1. 21 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 47.

Free will and Moral Knowledge 121

Some even go so far as to deny the existence of natural law, imprinted by God on each soul. Thus the world is pervaded with religious indolence, which in reality is simply ignorance or presumption. It is not the satanic non serviam, but the absolute lack of light.22

Vincible and invincible ignorance The obligation to acquire moral knowledge and assimilate the principles of Catholic doctrine is proportioned to each Christian’s particular circumstances. Everyone has the duty and right to know moral law, at least its fundamental principles. However, the breadth and depth of this knowledge depends on many factors. Some people do not have adequate means to acquire this moral knowledge in all its particulars. For this reason Catholic tradition has always distinguished between vincible and invincible moral error. This division arises according as the error derives from culpable ignorance – due to omission or to a positive decision not to seek answers – or from an ignorance that is blameless. When ignorance touches on the essential principles of morality, one can seldom be exonerated, since this law is accessible to the light of natural reasoning,23 written as it is on the hearts of all men.24 Such is not the case, however, with the supernatural law known through faith in Jesus Christ: to believe in him, we need to have the Gospel preached to us. How are they to believe in him of whom they have never heard? And how are they to hear without a preacher?25 When the Church condemned certain statements of Luther and Baius,26 she explicitly confirmed the existence of error or ignorance that

22 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 27. 23 Cf The Natural Moral Law, chapter 2 of this volume. 24 Cf Rom 11:14-16. 25 Rom 10:14. 26 Cf Leo X, Bull Exsurge Domine, 15 June 1520, prop. 35, Dz 775 (1485); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 68, Dz 1068 (1968).

122 On Christian life

excuses from sin. But the Church rejects the statement that “what is done out of ignorance is free of guilt.”27 A person can well be responsible for his lack of moral knowledge and to that same extent be guilty of the faults committed in that state. For example, in light of Christ’s cry from the Cross – Father, forgive them for they know not what they do28 – some have concluded that the Jews who crucified him were guiltless. That interpretation, however, was condemned by the Church in the year 1140 at the Council of Sens.29 Jesus had given more than sufficient proofs of his divinity and divine mission; if they persisted in their errors, they thereby manifested their rejection of God’s revelation and their deliberate rejection of grace. Their ignorance, their rejection of Christ, was in itself sinful. The crucifixion was a woeful consequence that added even more to their guilt: May his blood be upon us and upon our children.30 Therefore they sorely needed our Lord’s fervent prayer for forgiveness, his plea for God’s mercy for their hardened hearts. On the other hand, since invincible error stems from involuntary ignorance, it excuses moral responsibility. “They who labour in invincible ignorance of our most holy religion and who, zealously keeping the natural law and its precepts engraved in the hearts of all by God, and being ready to obey God, live an honest and upright life, can, by the operating power of divine light and grace, attain eternal life, since God who clearly beholds, searches, and knows the minds, souls, thoughts, and habits of all men, because of his great goodness and mercy, will by no means suffer anyone to be punished with eternal torment who has not the guilt of deliberate sin.”31 This principle has been emphasized many times by the Church, most recently by the Second Vatican

27 Council of Sens, 2 June 1140, Errors of Peter Abelard, prop. 9, Dz 377 (729-730). 28 Luke 23:34. 29 Cf Council of Sens, prop. 9, Dz 377 (729-730). 30 Matt 26:25. 31 Pius IX, Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863, Dz 1677 (2865-2866).

Free will and Moral Knowledge 123

Council: “Nor shall divine providence deny the assistance necessary for salvation to those who, without any fault of theirs, have not yet arrived at an explicit knowledge of God, and who, not without grace, strive to lead a good life.”32 This teaching – which developed particularly in relation to the radical question of human existence, namely, conversion to faith in Christ – also has very broad applications in morality. “Yet it often happens that conscience goes astray through ignorance which it is unable to avoid, without thereby losing its dignity. This cannot be said of the man who takes little trouble to find out what is true and good, or when conscience is by degrees almost blinded through the habit of committing sin.”33 Our Father pointed out: It is not enough that one’s conscience be earnest and sincere; one also must try to ensure that it be true, that it agrees with God’s universal norms as they really are. This is the aim of forming one’s conscience, which is an absolutely necessary duty required by the very nature of conscience (cf Gregory XVI, Enc. Letter, Mirari Vos, 15 August 1832, ASS 4 (1868), p. 341ff; Pius IX, Address Maxima Quidem, 9 June 1862, Acta Pii IX, v. I, p. 454; Syllabus, prop. 15, Dz 1715 (2915); Leo XIII, Enc. Letter, Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888, ASS 20 (1887-88), pp. 608-609; etc.). Without knowledge, there can be no conscience.34 The greater one’s human, cultural or professional formation, the less can moral ignorance be justified. The same can be said of a Christian with regard to the principal commandments of Christ’s law, of a professional man with regard to the ethics of his occupation, of a married person with regard to the duties of the married state. Each person should know well the moral implications of his state and walk in

32 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 16. 33 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 16. 34 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 84; “sin ciencia, no hay conciencia”.

124 On Christian life

life so as to direct all things to God and thus fulfil the primary principle of all morality. “The fundamental duty of man is, without doubt, to direct himself and his life to God.”35 One must be concerned, then, to use all available means to escape ignorance in an area as important as that of morality. An upright intention is not sufficient to make an action good, among other reasons, because a person’s intention is not upright if he does not sincerely seek to know, love and do God’s will. This will is clearly shown for all, in the sources of Revelation and in the Magisterium of the Church for a Christian, and it is shown in the natural moral order for all men. A person who neglects the habitual formation or the actual exercise of his conscience and confers on his personal decisions a divine value without further ado, while following in fact his limited insights or his own inclinations, can never have a right intention.36

Morality and free will The cooperation God asks of man so that he may grow in Christian life cannot be reduced to the task of acquiring moral knowledge. We must also freely decide to seek the good, directing our will toward God. “All men are bound to seek the truth, especially in what concerns God and his Church, and to embrace it and hold on to it as they come to know it.”37 “It is by personal assent that men must adhere to the truth they have discovered.”38 For human actions to be morally imputable, they must necessarily be free. Original sin is the only sin we contract without personal consent. Personal sins, also called actual sins, are committed with consent.39

35 Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 November 1947. 36 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 86. 37 Second Vatican Council, Decl. Dignitatis Humanae, 1. 38 Ibid, 3. 39 Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780).

Free will and Moral Knowledge 125

There is a great difference between feeling a temptation and giving in to it. A good act is not meritorious simply because we harbour a good resolution or a desire to carry it out. There must be an effective desire, which leads us to do what we can to find the adequate means to reach the proposed goal. If for some reason beyond our control the good desire does not achieve its goal, the merit is not lost. Likewise, when concupiscence is aroused or temptation is felt, there is no sin.40 Sin occurs only when the will consents to evil.41 Analogously to what happens with desires to do good, we can also offend God even without committing an immoral act. Clearly, whoever without justification places himself voluntarily in a near occasion of sin, already consents to temptation. Any factor which opposes the will and curtails its freedom diminishes the morality of an act. Physical violence, therefore, and sometimes moral coercion, excuse a person from sin.42 On the other hand, there are actions which, along with their good effects, bring on unavoidable evil consequences. Moral theologians call this type of action ‘indirect voluntary’. The term takes its origin from the fact that the evil consequences are not directly willed; they are permitted only because they cannot be avoided. Given the complexity of human behaviour, and the multiple connections to which social activity gives rise, the principles of the ‘indirect voluntary’ find frequent application. According to Catholic teaching, an act having evil effects is permissible only when the following conditions are present: the act in itself has to be good or indifferent; the intention of the doer must be good; the effects sought must be an immediate consequence of

40 Cf St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 50-51, Dz 1050-1051 (1950-1951). 41 Ibid, prop. 46 and 47, Dz 1046 and 1047 (1946 and 1947). 42 Cf Answers of the Sacred Penitentiary, 23 April 1822 (Dz 2715); 8 June 1842 (Dz 2758); 3 April 1916 (Dz 3634); Pius XI, Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930, Dz 2241 (3718).

126 On Christian life

the act, since evil effects can never be used as a means to attain good ones; and finally, there must be a proportionately grave reason – the graver the indirect evil effects, the more serious must be the reasons for performing the act.

Influence of passions and affectivity in moral life To weigh the impact of emotions on morality and see how they affect the voluntary nature of the act or the personal responsibility of the doer, is not an easy task. To avoid error in such an important area, the Church’s magisterium has pointed out some principles to be used in practice. Even though fear, for example, is a threat to freedom, the Church reminds us that no fear, however grave (unless it deprives a person of the use of reason) can justify endangering one’s supernatural life, invalidating the administration of the sacraments or cooperating in an intrinsically evil act.43 In the area of ecclesiastical law, under particular circumstances fear makes contracts invalid or rescindable, insofar as the act’s morally obligatory nature has been nullified.44 Passions and emotions can also diminish freedom and consequently personal responsibility; they can so overwhelm a person as to impede his reason. The acts done in such a state cease to be free. However, moral evaluation does not cease at this point. If passion diminishes or even annuls freedom, the question then arises whether the person did all he could to control his emotions beforehand. For we are often responsible for the sway our emotions have on our actions. Traditional Christian asceticism proposes, as a moral ideal, not the elimination of emotions, but their harnessing so that they be more

43 Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 2 March 1679, prop. 29 and 51, Dz 1179 (2129) and 1201 (2151); Benedict XIV, Const. Detestabilem, 10 November 1752, prop. 3, Dz 1493 (2573); Leo XIII, Letter Pastoralis Officii, 12 September 1891, Dz 1940 (3273). 44 Cf C.I.C., can. 125.

Free will and Moral Knowledge 127

responsive to God’s demands. “It belongs to the perfection of moral good that man should be moved unto good, not only in respect to his will, but also in respect to his sensitive appetite, according to the psalm: My heart and my flesh have rejoiced in the living God45.”46 Our emotions are given us to facilitate our doing what is morally good, even though at times, on account of original sin, our reason loses control over them and allows them to become a source of evil. In themselves, however, passions belong to the integrity of human nature, which God created good,47 and therefore should contribute to the good of man. Thus, for example, a Christian who, in order to help his neighbour, encourages feelings of compassion, does not on that account perform a less meritorious act. For besides helping his neighbour, he has added feelings of compassion that have been freely awakened. Even if our compassion were spontaneous, our consent to it transforms it into something free and pleasing to God. We have always been taught clearly on this matter by our Father. My daughters and sons, we ourselves have to be very human; otherwise we cannot be divine. It’s very bad not to have a heart: we must have a heart and control it. We have to place our heart at the service of God and souls: You need a big, all-embracing heart capable of understanding the weaknesses of others as well as your own.48 Referring to Christian asceticism, St Augustine wrote: “In our school, we are concerned not that a pious soul got angry but why he got angry; not that he is sad but why he is sad; not that he is afraid but why he is afraid.”49 Sacred Scripture itself sometimes recommends vigorous correction, but without thereby sinning against charity: irascimini et

45 Ps 83:3. 46 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 24, a. 3. 47 Cf Gen 1:31. 48 From our Father, Letter 2 February 1945, 31. 49 St Augustine, City of God, 9, 5.

128 On Christian life

nolite peccare, be angry (with the person committing a fault), but do not sin.50 On the other hand, when a person arouses an emotion or passion knowing that sin could follow, then he is morally responsible for the evil consequences of his act, even those resulting from an actual lack of the use of reason. The statement that sin committed under the influence of emotion is always only venial, runs counter to Catholic teaching (cf 1 Cor 6:9-10; Gal 5:9-21; Eph 5:5; Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1953, ASS 44 (1952), p. 275).51 Even if freedom is impaired by emotion, a person may have been responsible for unleashing it in the first place. “We reject as erroneous the affirmation that sins committed during the years of adolescence are inevitable, and that therefore they should be ignored, as though they were not grave faults since – these teachers add – emotions normally destroy the freedom required for an act to be morally imputable.”52 Those who differ from what the Church teaches do not base themselves on psychological reasons, since these, in fact, contradict their point of view. Instead, their starting point is a deformed view of the nature of mortal sin. They seem to think that in order to sin grievously there must be, as an indispensable condition, a direct confrontation with God. They forget that every sin, inasmuch as it goes against a moral norm, implies disobedience to God’s will. This disobedience, even if it is not intended as such, but only as a means to gain personal advantage or pleasure, still offends God. A sinner sets aside divine goodness in preferring a created good. For there to be mortal sin, our Father wrote, there is no need for explicit contempt of God, and even less, for hatred against God. The latter is the last

50 Eph 4:26; Ps 4:5. 51 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 87. 52 Pius XII, Address, 23 March 1952.

Free will and Moral Knowledge 129

extreme of possible malice in man. For mortal sin, which kills divine life in the soul, all that is needed is full knowledge of and full consent to something totally incompatible with man’s friendship with God, or the abandonment of means that are, in themselves, absolutely necessary for reaching the goal for which God has destined us.53 “The Lord Jesus, divine teacher and model of all perfection, preached holiness of life (of which he is the author and maker) to each and every one of his disciples without distinction: You must be perfect as your heavenly Father is perfect54.”55 Genuine Christian life is the growth of the whole person toward the ideal proposed by Christ himself. God calls and helps us with his grace. We must consequently respond with docility, learning to do battle against whatever obstacles impede our way to the goal. We must strive to acquire and ever improve our moral formation, which will help us follow this path faithfully. We must also strengthen our resolve to do good, because not everyone who says to me, “Lord, Lord,” shall enter the kingdom of heaven, but he who does the will of my Father, who is in heaven.56

53 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 86. 54 Matt 5:48. 55 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 56 Matt 7:21.

## GOD’S HELP IN MORAL LIFE {#cuaderno.2.11}

“According to Christian doctrine,” says Pius XI, “man, endowed with a social nature, is placed here on earth so that, while spending his life in society, and under an authority ordained by God,1 he may cultivate and employ all his faculties to the full, to the praise and glory of his Creator; and by faithfully fulfilling the functions of his trade or other calling, he may attain both temporal and eternal happiness.”2 Divine revelation teaches us that man was gratuitously elevated to the supernatural order, far surpassing the realm of nature. “God, out of his infinite goodness,” says the First Vatican Council, “destined man to a supernatural end, that is, to a participation in the good things of God, which altogether exceed man’s understanding.3 Although created by God in a state of rectitude, man, enticed by the evil one, abused his freedom at the start of history. He revolted against God and sought to attain his goal apart from him. Although they had known God, they did not glorify him as God, but their senseless hearts were darkened, and they served the creature rather than the Creator.”4 “What Revelation makes known to us is confirmed by our own experience. For when man looks into his own heart, he finds that he is

1 Cf Rom 13:1. 2 Pius XI, Enc. Quadragesimo Anno, 15 May 1931, Dz 2270 (3743). 3 First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1786 (3005); cf Pius XII, Enc. Humani Generis, 12 August 1950, Dz 2318 (3891). 4 Cf Rom 1:21-25.

God’s help in Moral Life 131

drawn towards what is wrong and sunk in many evils which cannot come from his good Creator. Often refusing to acknowledge God as his source, man has also upset the relationship which should link him to his last end; and at the same time, he has broken the right order that should reign within himself, as well as between himself and other men and all creatures.”5 Because of original sin, which cost us the gift of original justice and sanctity, we also lost our initial integrity. From then on, though every man retains a certain natural moral orientation in his activity, he does not have the strength needed to keep all the moral precepts known to him. Original sin makes moral behaviour more difficult. “Man therefore is divided within himself. As a result, the whole life of man, both individual and social, shows itself to be a struggle, and a dramatic one, between good and evil, between light and darkness. Man finds that he is unable, by himself, to overcome the assaults of evil successfully, so that everyone feels as though bound by chains. But the Lord himself came to free and strengthen man, renewing him inwardly and casting out the prince of this world,6 who held him in the bondage of sin7.”8

Need for divine grace to live morally Everything in this world, including man, is created; therefore all creatures need God’s help to remain in existence and to act.9 According to the common teaching of theologians, accepted by the ordinary Magisterium of the Church, God’s natural action in creatures is realized in three ways: “by his power, in that all things are subject to his rule; by his presence,

5 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 13. 6 John 12:31. 7 Cf John 8:34. 8 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 13. 9 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 1, Dz 1783-1784 (3002-3003); Leo XIII, Enc. Tametsi Futura, 1 November 1900.

132 On Christian life

in that all things are naked and open to his eyes; by his essence, in that he is present to all things as the cause of their being.10 But God is in man in a higher way – as known and loved – since even by our nature we spontaneously love, desire and seek after what is good.”11 Every naturally good action is done in cooperation with God. If man is incapable of doing anything without God’s help, even less will he be able to do good, even natural good, when his back is turned on him who is goodness itself, the source of all goodness. On the other hand, man can do evil by himself, because evil does not produce enriched being or life, but the lack of due good. Evil is disorder, privation, alienation from God as our last end and the source of all life. If God’s help is necessary for man’s “natural life, then much more is it necessary for the life we owe to Christ ... the life of grace, to which we must refer all our thoughts and actions.”12 God’s grace is indispensable in performing supernatural acts, for these by definition wholly exceed the powers of human nature. “No one is freed from sin by himself or by his own efforts; no one is raised above himself or completely delivered from his own weakness, solitude or slavery;13 all have need of Christ who is the model, master, liberator, and giver of life.”14 “Everything that the creature receives from the Creator can be called grace, in the broadest sense of the term. But in the proper and Christian sense of the word, grace comprises the supernatural manifestations of God’s love, his loving-kindness and action whereby he raises man to that intimate communion of life with himself that the New Testament calls divine sonship.”15

10 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I, q. 8, a. 3. 11 Leo XIII, Enc. Divinum illud, 9 May 1897. 12 Leo XIII, Enc. Tametsi Futura, 1 November 1900. 13 Cf St Ireneus, Adv. Haer., 3, 15, 3. 14 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Ad Gentes, 8; cf Rom 3:23-24. 15 Pius XI, Enc. Mit brennender Sorge, 14 March 1937.

God’s help in Moral Life 133

Although human nature is not intrinsically corrupted by original sin, every man experiences, even after baptism, a tendency toward evil and difficulty in doing what is right. This is concupiscence, the fomes peccati, which, without being sin in itself, “comes from sin and inclines to sin.”16 Even though it does not destroy freedom, it diminishes it.17 “With God’s help we must believe and preach the following: man’s free will was made so weak and unsteady through the sin of the first man that, after the fall, no one could love God sufficiently, or believe in God, or perform good works for God unless the grace of God’s mercy anticipated him.”18 Thus in man’s present state, grace – absolutely necessary to carry out supernatural actions – is also morally necessary to perform naturally good actions: actions each man can carry out with his natural powers alone. There is an analogy here with the moral necessity of revelation if man is to know natural religious truths.19 Man’s need of grace in his natural moral life derives not from human nature itself, but from its weakened condition. Grace heals nature’s wounds and restores it to its former strength. “The will’s freedom, weakened in the first man, can only be restored by the grace of baptism; what was lost can only be given back by him who gave it in the first place. Thus, Truth himself could say: If the Son frees you, then you are truly free20.”21 Only by following the moral order with God’s help do we conform our will to his, and direct ourselves effectively to our last end, God himself: the

16 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 17 Cf Council of Arles, year 475, Dz 160a (339); Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 8, Dz 181 (378); Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 2, Dz 317 (622); Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 6, Dz 325 (633); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1 and can. 5, Dz 793 and 815 (1521 and 1555). 18 Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 25, Dz 199 (396). 19 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 2, Dz 1785-1786 (3004-3005). 20 John 8:36. 21 Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 13, Dz 186 (383); cf Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 17.

134 On Christian life

contemplation and enjoyment of God the Father, God the Son and God the Holy Spirit in the glory of Heaven.22
