Characteristics of Christ’s grace In God’s plan, the only person capable of fully attaining moral goodness is “the supernatural man who constantly thinks, judges and acts according to right reason illumined by the supernatural light of Christ’s life and teachings.”23 This involves, Pope Pius XI continues, “the whole human being: a combination of spirit and body united to form one nature; endowed with all the perfections of mind and body belonging to human nature and with others transcending it; man, in other words, as we know him from reason and from divine revelation; man, therefore, fallen from the noble condition of his origin, redeemed by Christ and so restored to the supernatural dignity of being God’s adopted child, but not re-endowed with those preternatural privileges by which his body had formerly been immortal and his mind gifted with perfect balance and integrity.”24 The salvation won by Christ is not only a doctrine of truth and an example of virtues in the face of error and sin. Above all, it is the destruction of sin through the fullness of grace Jesus gained for us. Grace is a “permanent principle of supernatural life,”25 which absolutely transcends nature because it comes from God.26 Every good endowment and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of lights.27 No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him.28 Grace is a totally unmerited gift, to which men have no claim what-

22 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 82. 23 Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929. 24 Ibid. 25 Pius XI, Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930, Dz 2237 (3714). 26 Cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 3, 7 and 25, Dz 176, 180 and 199 (373, 377 and

396). 27 Jas 1:17. 28 John 6:44.

God’s help in Moral Life 135

soever. God gives it to us out of his loving kindness. By grace you have been saved through faith; and this is not your own doing, it is the gift of God, not because of works, lest any man should boast.29 Grace is such a gratuitous gift that it precedes all human dispositions and merits; even merit itself is God’s gift.30 Although grace is all-powerful and the source of everything good in us, it still defers to human freedom.31 The Church leaves it to theologians to study the relationships between God’s sovereign action and man’s free cooperation.32 She only recommends that, in such difficult questions, the faithful avoid a “twofold error: to teach that, in man fallen from original justice, free will is a word without substance – as the first heretics and Jansenists affirmed; or that God’s grace is neither gratuitously given nor able to do everything, as the Pelagians taught.”33

Actual grace The permanent, intrinsic principle of supernatural life by which “God (is) with us, to deliver us from the darkness of sin and death, and to raise us up to eternal life”34 is called habitual, or sanctifying grace. It raises human nature so it may share in God’s nature.35 But this gift is preceded and directed by another supernatural, though transitory,

29 Eph 2:8-9; cf Rom 11:5-6. 30 Cf Sixteenth Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 4, Dz 104 (226); Indiculus, ch. 7-9, Dz 136- 141 (245-248); Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 3-9, 18 and 25, Dz 176-182, 191, 198 and 199 (373-379, 388, 395 and 396); Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 2, 5 and 6, Dz 321, 324 and 325 (626, 632 and 633); Council of Trent, Decr. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1794 (3014). 31 Cf Matt 23:37; Acts 7:51; 2 Cor 6:1-2. 32 Cf Paul V, Formula about the question “De Auxiliis”, 9 September 1607, Dz 1090 (1997); Innocent X, Decree against the Jansenists, 23 April 1654, Dz 1097 (2008). 33 Pius XI, Enc. Ad Salutem, 20 April 1930. 34 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 4. 35 Cf 2 Pet 1:4.

136 On Christian life

motion, by which God prepares man, leads him towards justification and strengthens him in the face of temptation. Actual grace is its name. Actual grace is no less a gratuitous gift from God, bringing about its effects in each soul in a particular manner. There are no prefabricated formulas or rigid rules and methods for drawing souls closer to Christ. God’s encounter with each man is both ineffable and unrepeatable.36 God’s grace not only helps each soul to perform supernaturally good acts; it even confers the very possibility of doing these actions.37 Creatures on their own are incapable of fulfilling the commandments or doing other good supernatural works. Christ said decisively: Without me you can do nothing.38 And St Paul states that salvation depends not upon man’s will or exertion, but upon God’s mercy.39 Actual grace does not consist, then, in understanding the commandments, nor is it a mere help not to sin, although it does produce these effects. Rather, it is a supernatural power whereby we are capacitated “to love and to do what we know ought to be done.”40 Besides enlightening the mind to understand naturally inaccessible truths, actual grace inspires and moves the will in everything related to God. It goes before, accompanies, and perfects all actions leading to salvation. For God is at work in you, both to will and to work, for his good pleasure.41 In 529 A.D. the Second Council of Orange stated that “when, without being in God’s grace we believe, will, desire, strive, labour, pray, keep watch, beg, seek and knock for entrance ... it is through the interior infusion and inspiration of the Holy Spirit that we believe, will or are

36 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 42. 37 Cf Sixteenth Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 5, Dz 105 (227); Indiculus, ch. 7, Dz 136- 138 (245); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 2, Dz 812 (1552). 38 John 15:5. 39 Rom 9:16. 40 Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 4, Dz 104 (226); Indiculus, ch. 7, Dz 136-138, (245). 41 Phil 2:13.

God’s help in Moral Life 137

able to do all these things in the way we ought.”42 We cannot prescind from actual grace if we are to author deeds aimed at salvation: No one can say “Jesus is Lord” except by the Holy Spirit.43 The Church has traced out over the years the various practical applications of this general principle. Grace is needed to rise from original sin44 and to prepare for justification.45 Grace is needed to use one’s freedom properly,46 to desire grace,47 to believe,48 and even to want to believe.49 Grace is needed to pray,50 to fulfil the commandments,51 to resist temptations,52 to avoid mortal sin,53 and to carry out acts of penance.54 Without grace we could not merit grace itself55 or attain to final perseverance.56 No one is

free in this life from venial sin, unless accorded a very special privilege, as was our Lady.57 Catholic teaching on grace leads us finally to confess, in the words of Pope Pius XII, that “Christ is the author and efficient cause of holiness; for there can be no salutary act which does not proceed from him as from its supernatural source. “Without me,” Jesus said, “you can do nothing.”58 If we are moved to sorrow and repentance for the sins we have committed, if we turn to God with filial fear and hope, it is always his power that leads us on. Especially the more eminent members of his Body are constantly enriched by our Saviour with the gifts of counsel, fortitude, fear and piety, so that the whole Body may daily increase in holiness and integrity of life. And when the Church administers the sacraments with external ritual, it is he who produces their effect in the soul.59 He it is, too, who feeds the redeemed with his own flesh and blood, and so allays the irregular and unruly movements of the heart; it is he who increases grace in us and prepares for the attainment of glory in soul and body. And these treasures of divine goodness he must be held to communicate to the members of his mystical Body, not only inasmuch as he intercedes for them with the eternal Father both as Eucharistic Victim on earth and as glorified Victim in heaven, praying for us and showing his wounds, but also because it is he himself who chooses, determines, and distributes grace to each according to the measure of Christ’s gift60.”61

God’s help in Moral Life 139

Divine grace and the cooperation of man The salvation wrought by Christ and communicated by grace is for everyone, since God wills that all men should be saved.62 To bring salvation to all, he has taken on our nature “that he might redeem the Jews, who were under the Law, and that the gentiles, who did not follow after justice, might attain to justice,63 and that all men might receive the adoption of sons64.”65 God’s initiative opens the doors of salvation to all souls. Nobody is excluded from his work of redemption, because for God there is no distinction between Jew and Greek; the same Lord is Lord of all and bestows his riches upon all who call upon him.66 Nevertheless, not all men are actually saved. This is not due to any deficiency of God’s mercy; rather it is men who fail to cooperate. “Just as there neither is, was, nor ever will be any man whose nature was not assumed by Christ, so neither is there, was there, nor will there ever be any man for whom Christ our Lord did not suffer on the cross, even though not everyone will be redeemed by the mystery of his passion.”67 The Church also teaches “that some people should be saved is the gift of him who saves; that some people are lost is the fault of those who are lost.”68 God knows all hidden things, even before they actually occur.69 Thus, he knows “absolutely beforehand that the good were

De Iustificatione, ch. 2, Dz 794 (1522); cf Council of Arles, year 475, Dz 160b (340); Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 3, Dz 318 (623); Innocent III, Letter, Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780). 66 Rom 10:12. 67 Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 4, Dz 319 (624); cf Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 4, Dz 323 (630); Pius II, Letter Cum sicut, 14 November 1459, 2, Dz 717b (1362). 68 Council of Quiercy, year 853, ch. 3, Dz 318 (623); cf Council of Arles, year 475, Dz 160 (333, 339 and 340). 69 Cf Dan 13:42.

140 On Christian life

going to be good by his grace, and that by the very same grace they were going to receive eternal reward; and he foresaw that evil people were going to be evil on account of their own malice, and that his justice would have to condemn them to eternal punishment.”70 Our faith states that the reward of eternal life always derives from divine mercy; God has predestined this reward for those whom he willed, whose names are written in the Lamb’s book of life.71 On the other hand, the condemned are lost through their own fault, because God, source of all goodness and holiness, gives everyone the grace necessary for salvation and predestines no one to evil. “In the election of those who are to be saved,” states the Church’s teaching authority, “God’s mercy precedes good merit; whereas in the condemnation of those who are to perish, the evil merit precedes God’s just judgment.”72 Their depravity alone precludes eternal happiness. Salvation comes from grace; condemnation from man’s refusal to cooperate with God. Perseverance is assured by trying to struggle, our Father says. It is marvellous that we can never be sure. If we knew our perseverance was guaranteed, we would fill with pride. Therefore, not to know it is God’s providence; it helps us verify that we are weak, poor and ungrateful. And when we realize this, we try never to stray from God, we try to struggle, to make amends quickly if we are defeated and return to the true path. Thus we will persevere. Every initiative directed to salvation proceeds from God, but he always takes human freedom into account.73 “It is indeed far from true

70 Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 2, Dz 321 (626). 71 Rev 21:27; cf Rom 8:29-30; Eph 1:4-5. 72 Third Council of Valence, year 855, can. 3, Dz 322 (627). 73 Cf Indiculus, ch. 5-7, Dz 134-138 (243-245); Council of Arles, year 475, Dz 160a (330 and

339); Second Council of Orange, year 529, ch. 3, Dz 200 (397); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 5, 7, 13 and can. 4, Dz 797, 799, 806 and 814 (1525, 1541 and 1554); Innocent XI, Const. Coelestis Pastor, 20 November 1687, nn. 1-17, Dz 1221-1237 (2201- 2217); Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 November 1947, Dz 2299 (3846).

God’s help in Moral Life 141

to say that voluntary movements are less free on account of God’s intervention; the power of divine grace is intimate within man and accords with his natural tendencies, since grace proceeds from the author of our understanding and will, and he moves everything according to its nature.”74 Thus “grace, illuminating the understanding and moving the will towards moral good, strengthening it with a healthy constancy, makes the exercise of freedom both easier and more sure.”75 Accordingly, not only does one “not lose freedom, but one is liberated, so that what is darkness becomes light, what is twisted becomes straightened, what is sick becomes healthy.”76 The natural values of freedom find their fullest realization and their perfect fulfilment only in their elevation to the order of grace ... Only our Lord can give us true freedom, because he came praedicare captivis remissionem et caecis visum, dimittere confractos in remissionem (Luke 4:19), to announce freedom to captives, to give sight to the blind, and to loosen the bonds of the oppressed.77 Owing to the respect God shows for human freedom, man can resist grace and render God’s action fruitless. This was the reproach St Stephen directed against the Jews: stiff-necked race, your heart and ears still uncircumcised, you are forever resisting the Holy Spirit.78 This is a truth of faith reaffirmed by the Church in the face of Jansenist heresy and similar errors.79

74 Leo XIII, Enc. Libertas Praestantissimum, 20 June 1888. 75 Ibid. 76 Indiculus, ch. 9, Dz 141 (248). 77 From our Father, Letter 30 April 1946, 3. 78 Acts 7:51; cf Matt 23:37, John 6:51. 79 Cf Innocent X, Const. Cum Occasione, 31 May 1653, 2 and 4, Dz 1093 and 1095 (2002 and 2004); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 5ff, Dz 1295ff (2305ff); Clement XI, Dogm. Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, 1-25, 30 and 31, Dz 1351-1375, 1380 and 1381 (2401-2425, 2430 and 2439); Pius VI, Const. 142 On Christian life

Man’s free response to God’s grace is to be given “with thought, word and deed.”80 Faith by itself is not sufficient for cooperating effectively with grace. God requires a positive effort on the part of each person; he demands deeds. “Although the treasure of graces which our Saviour merited for his Church by his bitter passion and painful death is quite unlimited, yet God’s providence has so disposed that these gifts of grace shall be bestowed upon us only little by little; and their abundance depends in no small measure upon our good works by which this rain of heavenly gifts, God’s free bounty, is drawn down upon the souls of men. This gracious rain will be brought from heaven in abundance if we pray earnestly to God, especially by taking part devoutly, and daily if possible, in the eucharistic sacrifice; if we strive to relieve the sufferings of the needy by the duties of Christian charity. And it will descend in greater abundance still if, in addition to all this, we prefer imperishable goods to the transitory things of this life; if we tame this mortal flesh by voluntary mortification, denying it what is unlawful and even imposing upon it what is unpleasant and difficult; and finally, if we submissively accept the trials and sufferings of this present life as coming from the hand of God.”81 A man who does all in his power cannot say he has been abandoned by God, because our Lord grants his grace to everyone, even to those outside the Church through no fault of their own.82 If there is a deedfilled response, then God communicates to souls, with his grace, an unshakable sense of security; he sets them ablaze with a fire of fidelity

80 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 14. 81 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943. 82 Cf Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 5, Dz 1295 (2305); Clement XI, Dogm. Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, 29, Dz 1379 (2429); Pius IX, Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863, Dz 1677 (2866); First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1794 (3014 and 3015); Pius XII, Letter of the Holy Office to the Archbishop of Boston, 8 August 1949, (Dz 3866ff); Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 16.

God’s help in Moral Life 143

and dedication; he gives them clear lights to fulfil his most lovable will; and he makes them zealous, so that they strive for goals far beyond human reach.83

Natural possibility of doing good The law of grace does not suppress natural law, nor does God’s influence destroy the powers proper to human nature. On the contrary, grace corrects disordered tendencies, heals the wounds of sin and lifts man’s whole life to a different plane. My children, our Father reminded us, we must never lose sight of the high end for which we are destined. What does it profit a man to gain the whole world and suffer the loss of his soul? Or what shall a man give in return for his life? (Matt 16:26) We have only one final end, a supernatural one that includes, perfects and elevates our natural end, because grace presupposes, includes, heals and elevates nature.84 The natural moral strength remaining even in an unjustified man enables him to perform naturally good actions. The Protestant reformers and Jansenists denied this Catholic dogma. They even affirmed that every human action not raised to the supernatural order is sinful. This pessimistic conception of human activity has lamentable consequences for moral life in general: by eliminating the natural foundation of supernatural life, it strips man of his freedom and personal responsibility. He is not able to respond with free moral initiative to God’s call, and so there is no merit. Neither is he responsible for his actions, and so is incapable of sinning, since every sin presupposes the real possibility of choosing between good and evil. Behind this thinking, rejected by the Church as heretical, lie two serious errors. First there is confusion between the order of nature and

83 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 5. 84 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 59.

144 On Christian life

that of grace, claiming that the divine gifts communicated to man belong to the integrity of his nature and thus are not God’s free gifts. Secondly, original sin is seen as substantially corrupting human nature, doing irreparable damage. Catholic teaching states, on the contrary, that it is possible for every man to perform morally good acts; this is so even when he is not baptized or he is in the state of mortal sin.85 St Paul writes about those who are ignorant of revelation: When the Gentiles who have not the law do by nature what the law requires, they are a law to themselves, even though they do not have the law. They show that what the law requires is written on their hearts.86 The Church authoritatively rejected the thesis that “all works of infidels are sins, and the virtues of philosophers are vices;”87 as well as the Jansenist error that “everything not in accordance with supernatural Christian faith, which works through charity, is a sin.”88 Echoing Catholic tradition, St Thomas addresses this point: “Without doubt, it is false to say that any act not proceeding from the will informed by charity lacks merit. If this were the case, people in mortal sin would commit sin with every act they do, and it would not be appropriate to advise them to do all the good they can; nor could their good deeds dispose them towards grace. This is totally false.”89

85 Cf Leo X, Bull Exsurge Domine, 15 June 1520, 31, Dz 771ff (1481ff); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione ch. 11, can. 7, 25, Dz 804, 817 and 835 (1539, 1557 and 1575); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, 16, 22, 25, 35-37, 61, 68, Dz 1016, 1022, 1025, 1035-1037, 1061, 1068 (1916, 1922, 1925, 1935-1937, 1961, 1968); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 8 and 11, Dz 1298 and 1301 (2308 and 2311); Clement XI, Dogm. Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, 1-7, 39, 59, Dz 1351-1357, 1389, 1409 (2401-2407, 2439, 2459); Pius IX, Enc. Quanto Conficiamur Moerore, 10 August 1863, Dz 1677 (2866). 86 Rom 2:14-15. 87 St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, 25, Dz 1025 (1925). 88 Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 11, Dz 1301 (2311). 89 St Thomas Aquinas, De Malo, q. 2, a. 5 ad 7.

God’s help in Moral Life 145

When explaining Catholic teaching against Protestant and Jansenist heresies, the Church affirms the existence in man of natural moral virtues. These result from the repetition of naturally good actions, engendering moral rectitude.90 Without rejecting the purely natural possibility of doing good, we must also avoid the contrary error of naturalism. “This error consists in stating that, since all the natural inclinations of the will are good and proper, they must never be feared or hindered. From this false principle arise those methods of education ... which have suppressed all separation of the sexes and which take no precautions against the passions that come to flower in children and adolescents; from this position also arises licentiousness in writing and reading and in public entertainment where modesty and innocence run grave risks, occasioning lamentable falls. From here also arises, finally, that immodest fashion in dress, against which Christian women can never fight enough.”91 Neither must we forget that a soul in sin, either original or personal, cannot for any long period of time keep all the commandments, or avoid all mortal sins. Just as grace strengthens nature and heals its wounds, sin exacerbates those wounds and diminishes natural strength. The weakness stemming from original sin becomes more pervasive with each personal sin, making progress in natural goodness difficult. An unregenerate man cannot, in fact, keep himself immune from mortal sin throughout his life with his natural strength alone, even though he retains with each decision or deed the inner capacity to resist temptation and fulfil natural duties. On the other hand, the man justified

90 Cf St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, 16, 25, 36-38 and 62, Dz 1016, 1025, 1036-1038 and 1062 (1916, 1925, 1936-1938 and 1962); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 7-9, Dz 1297-1299 (2307-2309); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 44-67, Dz 1394-1417 (2444-2467). 91 Pius XI, Enc. Ad Salutem, 20 April 1930.

146 On Christian life

by sanctifying grace is invested not only with the necessary strength, but also with the right to all actual graces needed to heal his weakness and persevere in goodness. “Conformed to the image of the Son, who is the first-born of many brothers,92 the Christian man receives the first fruits of the Spirit93 by which he is able to fulfil the new law of love.94 By this Spirit, who is the pledge of our inheritance,95 the whole man is inwardly renewed, right up to the redemption of the body96.”97 This renewal restores human nature to its original integrity and bestows full participation in God’s life. Meanwhile, “the Christian is certainly bound both by need and by duty to struggle with evil through many afflictions and to suffer death; but, as one who has been made a partner in the paschal mystery, and as one who has been configured to the death of Christ, he will go forward, strengthened by hope, to the resurrection98.”99

92 Cf Rom 8:29; Col 3:10-14. 93 Rom 8:23. 94 Cf Rom 8:1-11. 95 Eph 1:14. 96 Rom 8:23. 97 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 22. 98 Cf Phil 3:10. 99 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 22.

## JUSTIFICATION AND CHRISTIAN LIFE {#cuaderno.2.12}

God saved us, not because of deeds done by us in righteousness, but in virtue of his own mercy, by the washing of regeneration and renewal in the Holy Spirit, which he poured out upon us richly through Jesus Christ our Saviour, so that we might be justified by his grace and become heirs in hope of eternal life.1 Grace, which is absolutely necessary if man is to achieve his supernatural purpose, is a lasting principle of divine life infused into the soul, enabling man to think, will and act in a supernatural way. This gift is received at baptism, the gateway to Christian life. And due to the baptismal character, it remains in the soul, actively giving divine value to a person’s entire existence as long as it is not impeded by mortal sin. Grave sin deprives us of charity, and the state of grace itself. And if it is directly opposed to faith or hope, sin deprives us of these virtues as well.

God, the author of justification The infusion of habitual grace, called justification, is always the work of our “merciful God who freely washes and sanctifies,2 signing and anointing with the Holy Spirit of promise, who is the pledge of our

1 Tit 3:5-7. 2 Cf 1 Cor 6:11.

148 On Christian life

inheritance.”3 God is the sole cause of justification. He is holiness itself and the source of all holiness: I, the Lord, sanctify you.4 He it is who takes the initiative and goes in search of those whom he wishes, moved only by his mercy, “without any merit whatsoever on their part.”5 “All children of the Church should nevertheless remember that their privileged condition is not to be attributed to their own merits but to a special grace of Christ. If they fail to respond to that grace in thought, word and deed, not only shall they not be saved, but they shall be the more severely judged.”6 Although God could have justified man by a mere act of his will, he chose instead to open the gate to supernatural life through the passion and death of his most beloved only-begotten Son, our Lord Jesus Christ, who, when we were enemies,7 for the exceeding charity wherewith he loved us,8 “merited justification for us by his most holy passion on the wood of the cross, and made satisfaction for us to God the Father.”9 Christ’s merits are fully applied to us in baptism;10 we are “justified by his grace as a gift, through the redemption which is in Christ Jesus.”11 This grace has profound effects on the soul; it is the very justice of God himself, his own holiness: “not that whereby he himself is just, but that whereby he makes us just; namely, the justice we have from him as a gift whereby we are renewed in the spirit of our mind. And not only are we considered just, but we are truly said to be just, and we are

3 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 4 Exod 31:13. 5 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 5, Dz 797 (1525). 6 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 14. 7 Rom 5:10. 8 Eph 2:4. 9 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 10 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 4 and 7, Dz 796 and 799 (1524 and 1529). 11 Rom 3:24.

Justification and Christian Life 149

just.”12 When someone becomes a Christian, he receives a share in God’s nature. His soul becomes the abode of God’s kingdom – a kingdom of holiness and justice which is “the glory of God and of Christ, and life everlasting.”13

Man’s cooperation in his own justification Although God justifies us from our sins by a sheer gift of mercy, nevertheless, our Lord does not destroy man’s freedom; it is precisely He who has made us free. That is why he does not want to wring obedience from us. He wants our decisions to come from the depths of our heart.14 Justification is a supernatural and mysterious transformation whereby, in a sublime manner, God’s action and man’s cooperation interweave.15 Justification is wrought by God, who also intervenes decisively in man’s preparation to receive it. Through actual graces, he moves and helps man to convert: “men are disposed to turn to their own justification by freely assenting to and cooperating with grace. The result is that, when God touches man’s heart with the illumination of the Holy Spirit, the man who accepts that inspiration certainly does something, since he could reject it; on the other hand, by his own free will, without God’s grace, he could not take one step toward justice in God’s sight.”16 Sacred Scripture bears witness to the directness of this divine action when it puts on men’s lips the prayer: Convert us, O Lord, to you, and we shall be converted.17 Yet it also clearly affirms our freedom: Thus says the Lord of hosts: Return to me ... and I will return to you.18

12 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 13 Ibid. 14 Christ is passing by, 100. 15 Cf God’s help in moral life, chapter 11 of this volume. 16 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 5, Dz 797 (1525); cf ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 17 Lam 5:21. 18 Zech 1:3.

150 On Christian life

If man accepts and freely cooperates with this divine action, he begins to prepare himself to receive Christian life, until the moment arrives when he gives his assent to God’s word: “believing to be true what has been divinely revealed and promised, especially that God justifies the sinner through his grace.”19 “Before this faith can be exercised, man must have the grace of God to move and assist him; he must have the interior help of the Holy Spirit, who moves the heart and converts it to God, who opens the eyes of the mind and makes it easy for all to accept and believe the truth.”20 Actual grace, then, initiates, accompanies and completes the whole process of justification. Faith alone does not justify man.21 God makes us just through faith in Jesus Christ;22 but “we are said to be justified by faith because faith ‘is the beginning of human salvation’,23 the foundation and root of all justification, without which it is impossible to please God24 and join the fellowship of his children.”25 This faith must be expressed in deeds and lead each of the faithful to cooperate voluntarily with grace.26 You see that a man is justified by works and not by faith alone ... For as the body apart from the spirit is dead, so faith apart from works is dead.27 By faith, the sinner acknowledges the existence of a God who rewards the good and punishes the wicked.28 Considering further how

19 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 6, Dz 798 (1526); cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1793 (3012). 20 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Verbum, 5; cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 7, Dz 180 (377); Vatican Council 1, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1791 (3010). 21 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 9 and can. 9, 12-14, Dz 802, 819, 822-824 (1533, 1534, 1559, 1562-1564). 22 Rom 3:22. 23 St Fulgentius, De Fide ad Petrum, 1. 24 Cf Heb 11:6. 25 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 8, Dz 801 (1532). 26 Cf Ibid, can. 9, Dz 819 (1559). 27 Jas 2:24, 26. 28 Cf Heb 11:6; Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 2 March 1679, prop. 22, Dz 1172 (2122).

Justification and Christian Life 151

his sins make him liable to eternal condemnation, “he is seized by fear and, as his soul recalls the terrible punishment awaiting him, he turns away from sin.”29 This initial fear is not something merely negative, nor does it make sinners worse than they were.30 On the contrary, fear acts as a powerful spur to rouse the soul and awaken it from the lethargy of its wretchedness. It invites the sinner to abandon himself and to be filled with hope in God’s mercy, “trusting that God will be merciful to him for the sake of Christ.”31 Hope, in turn, leads to charity, whereby those who put their trust in God “begin to love God as the source of all justice, and, on that account, are moved against sin by a certain hatred and detestation.”32 This repentance includes the resolution to start a new life and to fulfil the precepts of God’s law. It is an indispensable requirement in the case of adults, if they are to receive the sacrament of baptism or penance fruitfully. As soon as their hearts are enkindled with love, “there arises a filial fear of good and dutiful children who dread only to offend God’s majesty.”33 Thus, under the influence of grace, a “new convert sets out on a spiritual journey by means of which, while already sharing through faith in the mystery of the death and resurrection, he passes from the old man to the new man, who has been made perfect in Christ.”34

29 Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 5, 8; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 6, Dz 798 (1526). 30 Cf Leo X, Bull Exsurge Domine, 15 June 1520, prop. 6, Dz 746 (1456); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 8, Dz 818 (1558); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, prop. 14ff, Dz 1304ff (2314ff); Clement XI, Const. 152 On Christian life

Justification, a passing over from the state of sin to the state of grace The Catholic Church teaches clearly that “none of the things that precede justification, whether faith or good works, merit the grace of justification,”35 which is always a gratuitous gift of God. In this is the love, not that we have loved God, but that he has first loved us.36 Divine love, our Father wrote, led the Second Person of the most holy Trinity, the Word, the Son of God the Father, to take on our flesh, our human condition, everything except sin.37 To understand Catholic teaching correctly on this point, “it is necessary to admit that all men lost innocence in the sin of Adam.38 Rendered unclean39 and, as the Apostle says, by nature children of wrath,40 they all became the slaves of sin41 and subject to the power of the devil and death. Thus neither the Gentiles, through the forces of nature, nor even the Jews by the very letter of the Law of Moses itself could free themselves or arise therefrom.”42 Thanks alone to his goodness, God has delivered us from the dominion of darkness and transferred us to the kingdom of his beloved Son, in whom we have redemption, the forgiveness of sins.43 Thus St Paul describes the sinner’s justification. It is a passage “from the state of being born a child of the first Adam to the state of grace and adoption as God’s children44 through the second Adam, Jesus Christ, our Saviour.”45

35 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 8, Dz 801 (1532). 36 1 John 4:10. 37 Christ is passing by, 162. 38 Cf Rom 5:12; 1 Cor 15:22. 39 Cf Is 64:4. 40 Cf Eph 2:3. 41 Cf Rom 6:20. 42 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 1, Dz 793 (1521). 43 Col 1:13-14. 44 Cf Rom 8:15. 45 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 4, Dz 796 (1524).

Justification and Christian Life 153

God’s action upon man is described in the New Testament as a passing from flesh to spirit,46 from injustice to justice,47 from the death of sin to the life of grace,48 from slavery to freedom,49 from uncleanness to purity,50 from darkness to light,51 from the old man to the new man,52 from eternal condemnation to salvation.53 This transition from one state to another involves true remission of sins and authentic interior renewal. It is a doctrine of faith that justification effects forgiveness of sins. The Church specifically defined this truth in answer to Protestantism, which admitted justification only as a “non-imputation” of sins without any real forgiveness. Accordingly, Christ’s justice or holiness would cover man like a cloak, thus hiding his sins from God’s sight, but leaving those sins embedded in human nature. Catholic teaching, on the contrary, affirms there is a true and proper remission of sins: “If anyone denies that the guilt of original sin is remitted through the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ, as conferred in baptism, or asserts that everything that has the true and proper nature of sin is not taken away, but is only superficially brushed over or not imputed, let him be anathema.”54 “God hates nothing in those reborn”55 – those who have received baptism (or the sacrament of penance in the case of a baptized person subsequently guilty of some mortal sin)56 – because all stain of their sin is washed away: “It is I,” says the Lord, “I, who wipe out, for my own

46 Cf John 3:5-6. 47 Cf Rom 5:18-19. 48 Cf Rom 6:10-11. 49 Cf Rom 6:17-18. 50 Cf 1 Cor 6:9ff; Eph 5:26. 51 Cf Col 1:12-14. 52 Cf Eph 4:21ff. 53 Cf Tit 3:4-7. 54 Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 55 Ibid. 56 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 14, Dz 807 (1542).

154 On Christian life

sake, your offences.”57 He will seek his sin, and it will not be found;58 as far as the east is from the west, so far has he put our transgressions from us.59 There is therefore now no condemnation for those who are in Christ Jesus, who do not walk according to the flesh.60 Our faith also teaches that “justification is not only a remission of sins, but also the sanctification and renewal of the inner man through the voluntary reception of grace and the gifts.”61 These are not two different divine acts, but only one action of God, who forgives sins absolutely and bestows the new life of grace. Through this real interior renewal, “an unjust man becomes just, and an enemy becomes a friend, that he may be an heir in the hope of everlasting life62.”63 We can call it “real” because, as a result of this internal renewal of the soul, “not only are we considered just, but are truly called and are just, having received justice within us, each according to his own measure, which the Holy Spirit distributes to everyone as he wills64 and according to each one’s disposition and cooperation.”65 The better the preparation, the more abundant the divine life infused into the soul. If we allow God’s word to enter our hearts, we can truly say that we do not walk in darkness. For God’s light will shine out over our weaknesses and our personal defects, as the sun shines above the storm.66 Justification, therefore, is not merely an external or purely moral

57 Is 43:25. 58 Ps 9:15. 59 Ps 102:12. 60 Rom 8:1. 61 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1528). 62 Cf Tit 3:7. 63 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1528). 64 Cf 1 Cor 12:11. 65 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 66 Christ is passing by, 45.

Justification and Christian Life 155

renewal, or a favour from God lacking roots in the soul. Rather, it truly renews a man from within and converts him – a sinner and rebel – into a good and faithful servant (cf Matt 25:21).67 Justification is a spiritual and interior regeneration that transforms a Christian into a new man,68 created after the likeness of God in true righteousness and holiness.69 It is a new creation, as St Paul writes: If then any man is in Christ, he is a new creature; the former things have passed away: behold, they are made new!70

Growth, loss and restoration of justification Because it is a new life, the holiness that suffuses the Christian soul is meant to develop, attaining ever greater life in Christ. He who is just, let him be justified still; and he who is holy, let him be holier still.71 God himself desires the holiness and happiness of every single soul: You shall make and keep yourselves holy, because I am holy.72 Through his constant action on the soul and through the sacraments, God fosters the growth of the supernatural seed sowed in the soul at baptism. But Christian life grows strong and flourishes only when man, using his freedom, behaves in accordance with his new life and shuns whatever could inhibit or even expel it. Man cooperates with grace by fulfilling the commandments and authoring good deeds.73 St Peter exhorts us: Therefore, brethren, strive even more by good works to make your calling and election sure. For, if you do this, you will not fall into sin at any time. Indeed, in this way, the entrance into the everlasting kingdom

67 Christ is passing by, 162; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7 and 16, can. 11, Dz 800, 809, 821 (1530, 1547, 1561). 68 Cf Col 3:9-10. 69 Eph 4:24. 70 2 Cor 5:17. 71 Rev 22:11. 72 Lev 11:44. 73 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 10, Dz 803 (1535).

156 On Christian life

of our Lord and Saviour Jesus Christ will be amply provided for you.74 The quest for goodness and, more particularly, the observance of the commandments, is a necessary condition to advance in grace. “Wherefore no one should flatter himself with faith alone, thinking that by faith alone he is made an heir and will obtain the inheritance ... for, as the Apostle says, Christ himself, Son of God though he was, learned obedience from the things that he suffered; and when perfected, he became to all who obey him the cause of eternal salvation75.”76 True love for God spurs us to upright action. And awareness of our divine filiation reminds us of the need to preserve and increase the inheritance received from our heavenly Father. “No one, however much justified, should consider himself exempt from observing the commandments.”77 On the contrary, “the just should feel greater obligation to walk in the way of justice, for freed from sin and made servants of God,78 living soberly and justly and piously,79 they can advance through Jesus Christ.”80 To remain in grace, one must necessarily fulfil God’s commandments. A just man can again become the devil’s slave not only by grave sins against faith, but by any other mortal sin as well. Christ’s teaching “excludes from the kingdom of God not only the unbelieving, but also the faithful who are fornicators, adulterers, effeminate, liars with mankind, thieves, covetous, drunkards, evil-tongued, greedy,81 and all others who commit deadly sins, from which, with the help of divine

74 2 Pet 1:10-11. 75 Cf Heb 5:8ff. 76 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1538). 77 Ibid, Dz 804 (1536); cf can. 18-22, Dz 828-832 (1568-1572); Innocent X, Const. Cum Occasione, 31 May 1653, prop. 1, Dz 1092 (2001); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 6, Dz 1356 (2406); Pius VI, Const. Auctorem Fidei, 28 August 1794, prop. 19, Dz 1519 (2619). 78 Rom 6:22. 79 Tit 2:12. 80 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1537). 81 Cf 1 Cor 6:9ff.

Justification and Christian Life 157

grace, they can refrain, and on account of which they are separated from the grace of Christ.”82 “Those who after receiving the grace of justification have lost it through sin, may again be justified when, awakened by God, they strive to regain, through the sacrament of penance and by Christ’s merits, the grace they have lost.”83

Need and importance of the ascetical struggle We Christians carry the great treasures of grace in vessels of clay (cf 2 Cor 4:7). God has entrusted his gifts to the weakness and fragility of human freedom. We can be certain of the help of God’s power, but our concupiscence, our love of comfort, and our pride sometimes cause us to reject grace and to fall into sin.84 We can forfeit the state of grace, primarily owing to the effects remaining even after original sin has been removed by baptism.85 “Through justifying grace, God certainly heals the wounds of the soul, but not those of the flesh. Concerning which the Apostle wrote: For I know that in me, that is, in my flesh, no good dwells.86 When the first man forfeited original justice, which had enabled him to bridle his passions, reason was no longer able to restrain them within the bounds of duty or repress inordinate desires repugnant to reason. Whence the Apostle tells us that sin, that is to say, the incentive to sin, dwells in the flesh, thus giving us to understand that it does not make a mere temporary stay within us as a passing guest, but that, as long as we live, it maintains its abode in our members as a permanent inhabitant of the body.”87

82 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 15, Dz 808 (1544). 83 Ibid, ch. 14, Dz 807 (1542). 84 Christ is passing by, 131. 85 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515). 86 Cf Rom 7:18. 87 Catechism of St Pius V, 4, 12, 10.

158 On Christian life

Therefore we should never cease fighting against the evil inclinations of our nature. Do you not know that in a race all the runners compete, but only one receives the prize? So run that you may obtain it. Every athlete exercises self-control in all things. They do it to receive a perishable wreath, but we an imperishable. Well, I do not run aimlessly, I do not box as one beating the air; but I pommel my body and subdue it, lest after preaching to others I myself should be disqualified.88 Because of this tendency to evil present in all men, no one can be absolutely certain of remaining justified at all times,89 or of being predestined for eternal life, unless he were to receive a very special revelation from God.90 We must struggle throughout our lives without ever giving in to a false sense of security about our holiness. “Let those who think themselves to stand, take heed lest they fall,91 and with fear and trembling work out their salvation,92 in labours, in vigils, in almsdeeds, in prayers and oblations, in fasting and chastity.93 For, knowing that they are born again unto a hope of glory,94 but not as yet unto glory itself, they ought to fear for the combat which yet remains with the flesh, the world and the devil. For in this battle they cannot be victorious unless they be, with God’s grace, obedient to the Apostle, who says: We are debtors, not to the flesh, to live according to the flesh; for if you live according to the flesh, you shall die. But if by the spirit you mortify the deeds of the flesh, you shall live95.”96 For a Christian who knows that he is a child of God, this uncertainty

88 1 Cor 9:24-27. 89 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 9, Dz 802 (1534). 90 Cf Ibid, ch. 12, Dz 805 (1540). 91 Cf 1 Cor 10:12. 92 Cf Phil 2:12. 93 Cf 2 Cor 6:3ff. 94 Cf 1 Pet 1:3. 95 Rom 8:12-13. 96 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 13, Dz 806 (1541).

Justification and Christian Life 159

should never lead to fear. Rather it should be an encouragement to a fuller and more responsible abandonment to God’s mercy. God does not forsake those who have once been justified by his grace, unless he is first forsaken by them.97 God is always faithful to his promises. If that hope were vain, why did Christ, in his own appointed time, undergo death for us sinners while we were still powerless to help ourselves? It is difficult enough to find anyone who will die on behalf of a just man, although perhaps there may be those who will face death for one so deserving. But here, as if God meant to prove how much he really loves us, it was while we were still sinners that Christ, in his own appointed time, died for us. All the more surely then, now that we have found justification through his blood, shall we be saved through Christ from God’s displeasure. Enemies of God, we were reconciled to God through his Son’s death; reconciled to him, we are surer than ever of finding salvation in his Son’s life.98 Each person, then, should distrust himself and “place his firm hope in God’s help. For God, unless men themselves fail to correspond to his graces, as he has begun the good work, so will he perfect it.”99 A deep and sturdy humility, based on knowledge of our own weakness, is essential. Together with humility, the realization of the greatness of man’s dignity – and of the overwhelming fact that, by grace, we are made children of God – forms in the Christian a single attitude. It is not our own efforts that save us and give us life; it is the grace of God. This is a truth which can never be forgotten. If it were, the divinization of our life would become perverted, turning into presumption and pride. And this would lead, sooner or later, to a breakdown of spiritual life, when the soul came face to face with its

97 Ibid, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1537). 98 Rom 5:6-10. 99 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 13, Dz 806 (1541); cf can. 22, Dz 832 (1572); Phil 2:13.

160 On Christian life

own weakness and wretchedness.100 But whoever places all his confidence in God while distrusting himself, and strives to respond to divine grace with deeds, will never encounter insurmountable difficulties. “God does not command the impossible,” the Church teaches. “But when he commands, he asks you to do what you can and to pray for what you cannot do and for help to do it.”101 With God’s help, Christians can not only keep intact, but also enrich the gift they have received. Nobody can be considered exempt from venial sin, “except through a special privilege from God, which the Church holds true regarding the Virgin Mary.”102 But men do not on that account “cease to be just, for the petition of the just, forgive us our trespasses,103 is both humble and true.”104 And if at any time they were to lose the life of grace through mortal sin, they can once again be justified, provided that with a humble and contrite heart105 they receive the sacrament of penance. Experience of sin, then, should not make us doubt our mission. True, our sins can make it difficult to recognize Christ. That is why we must face up to our personal miseries and seek to purify ourselves. But in doing this, we must realize that God has not promised us a complete victory over evil in this life. Instead he asks us to fight. Sufficit tibi gratia mea (2 Cor 12:9), my grace is sufficient for you, our Lord replied to Paul, when he wanted to be freed of the thorn in his flesh that humiliated him. The power of God is made manifest in our weakness, and it spurs

100 Christ is passing by, 133. 101 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11, Dz 804 (1536); cf St Augustine, De Natura et Gratia 43. 102 Ibid, can. 23, Dz 833 (1573). 103 Matt 6:12. 104 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 13, Dz 806 (1541); cf can. 22, Dz 832 (1572). 105 Ps 50:19.

Justification and Christian Life 161

us on to fight, to battle against our defects, even though we know that we will never achieve total victory during our pilgrimage on earth. Christian life is a continuous beginning again each day. It renews itself over and over.106 Through faith in Jesus Christ, accompanied and followed by our good works, we are forgiven our sins by God and granted a share in his life. But the inclination to evil remains in the justified, as in everyone else. Therefore, “man has to struggle in order to do what is right, and it is at great cost to himself, and aided by God’s grace, that he succeeds in achieving his own inner integrity,”107 overcoming temptations, rising again from his falls and growing in holiness. Thus, ascetical struggle is essential for a person wishing to lead a Christian life. As long as there is humble and constant determination in the practice of good works, God, who provides the sower with seed, will both give you bread to eat and will multiply your seed, and will increase the growth of the fruits of your justice.108

106 Christ is passing by, 114. 107 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 37. 108 2 Cor 9:10.

## THE LIFE OF GRACE {#cuaderno.2.13}

The new life ushered in by grace is not an existence common to all men, but a life given exclusively to believers in Christ, who are born not of blood nor of the will of the flesh nor of the will of man, but of God.1 If we are to fathom in some way the grandeur of this new existence, we should consider God’s eternal plan. He has not only given men their natural existence: he also offers them supernatural life, a sharing in his own intimate life, the very life of the three divine Persons. God the Father is the inexhaustible source of all life and perfection. He eternally begets the Son, the living image of his substance, the perfect splendour of his glory, the flawless mirror reflecting all God’s majesty in himself.2 In eternity the Father contemplates his Son and the Son contemplates the Father in an unending dialogue of love. You are my Father,3 says the Only-Begotten Son, in an act of total self-giving. And God the Father, in an overflow of love, also gives himself to the Son as he says: You are my Son, today I have begotten you.4 This mutual gift of Father and Son is the Holy Spirit, the third Person of the blessed Trinity, proceeding from the other two and unifying them, the bond of love between the Father and the Son,5 the seal of God’s

1 John 1:13. 2 Cf Heb 1:3. 3 Ps 88:27. 4 Ps 2:7. 5 Christ is passing by, 169.

The Life of Grace 163

intimate life. This eternal and unbounded dialogue of self-giving sums up the infinite happiness of God himself.

Divine filiation, the foundation of Christian life From all eternity men are gratuitously called by God to attain in heaven the contemplation and enjoyment of the Trinity in the One God. Eye has not seen, nor has ear heard, nor has it entered the heart of man, what God has prepared for those who love him (1 Cor 2:9)6 And since only the Son enjoys the Father’s good pleasure,7 God the Father destined us in love to be his sons through Jesus Christ.8 Unless we are united to Jesus, we cannot enter the intimacy of God’s life: no one comes to the Father, but by me.9 The fullness of God is revealed and given to us in Christ, in the love of Christ, in the Heart of Christ.10 Reborn spiritually through baptism, a Christian is grafted onto Christ and begins to live his very life. For as many of you as were baptized into Christ have put on Christ.11 We have entered into a communion of life with him that is far different from the union of men in society. A Christian’s union with Christ is so intimate that it radically transforms his existence, enabling God’s life to grow within his soul. Sacred Scripture likens it to the union between the body and head,12 or between the vine and branches,13 because the same life courses through both. There is only one race in the world, our Father writes, the race of the children of God. We should all speak the same language, taught us by

6 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 58. 7 Cf Matt 3:17; 17:5. 8 Eph 1:5. 9 John 14:6. 10 Christ is passing by, 163. 11 Gal 3:27; cf Second Council of Orange, year 529, can. 24, Dz 197 (394); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1530). 12 Cf 1 Cor 12:12-27. 13 Cf John 15:1-6.

164 On Christian life

our Father in heaven – the language Jesus spoke with his Father. It is the language of heart and mind, which you are using now, in your prayer – the language of contemplative souls, used by men who are spiritual, because they realize their divine filiation.14 This divine adoption melds a person into an image of the onlybegotten Son. Made a brother or sister of Christ, a living member of the Mystical Body, we are entitled to receive the permanent gifts of God’s grace. Divine filiation is a joyful truth, a consoling mystery. Divine filiation fills all our spiritual life, because it shows us how to speak to God, to know and to love our Father in heaven. And it makes our interior struggle overflow with hope and gives us the trusting simplicity of little children.15 Whenever a man adopts someone as his child, he gives him his own surname. Along with his love, he bestows on him the right to receive an inheritance; but he cannot communicate his own nature or life. Human adoption is merely external: it does not change the adopted person from within or give him any new intrinsic perfection. Adoption by God, on the contrary, transcends the natural order and the ordinary notion of adoption, for it involves a new birth and brings about a real change in the nature of the person adopted. Beloved, writes St John, we are God’s children now.16 We do not usurp a title or assume a mere fiction: the Spirit himself thus assures our spirit that we are children of God.17 Divine filiation is a joyful reality that leads the Apostle to exclaim: You are no longer strangers and sojourners, but you are fellow citizens with

14 Christ is passing by, 13. 15 Christ is passing by, 65. 16 1 John 3:2; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 5, Dz 792 (1515); Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 2, 4, Dz 794 and 796 (1522 and 1524); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 13 and 42, Dz 1013 and 1042 (1913 and 1942); Pius VI, Const. Auctorem Fidei, 28 August 1794, prop. 23, Dz 1523 (2623); First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 3, Dz 1793 (3012); Pius XI, Enc. Divini Redemptoris, 19 March 1937, (Dz 3771). 17 Rom 8:16.

The Life of Grace 165

the saints and members of the household of God.18 This God-given new way of being is sanctifying grace, which makes man over in Christ’s image. Through grace we become God’s children and are welcomed into the intimacy of God’s own life.

Sanctifying grace, a participation in divine nature The immediate source of this new life is Christ. He consummated the saving plan of God the Father, so that we might become true images of his Son.19 As the first-born among many brethren,20 Christ has already fulfilled in his human nature all the promises made to us. Through Jesus Christ, writes St Peter, God bestowed on us high and treasured graces which he had promised, so as to make us sharers in the divine nature by means of these graces.21 Sanctifying grace is a sharing in God’s nature. It is a new and supernatural way of being whereby God clothes the just and makes them capable of living his own life. Thus it is an interior reality, “a divine quality infused into the soul, a kind of splendour and light that cleans away every stain from our souls and makes them most beautiful and bright.”22 Sanctifying grace “joins our soul to God in a very close bond of love.”23 Sacred Scripture compares it to a pledge God places in the hearts of the faithful,24 to a spring of water welling up to eternal life,25 to a seed putting down roots in man’s heart.26 As a quality inhering in our nature, sanctifying grace is not a transitory

18 Eph 2:19; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 10, Dz 803 (1535). 19 Rom 8:29. 20 Ibid. 21 2 Pet 1:4. 22 Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 2, 50. 23 Ibid, 1, 9, 8. 24 Cf 2 Cor 5:5. 25 John 4:14. 26 Cf 1 John 3:9.

166 On Christian life

gift like actual grace. It does not consist, as such, in obedience to the commandments; rather, it is “a permanent principle of supernatural life,”27 a habit or stable disposition rooted in the soul’s essence, so that a person who receives it “can from then on live rightly and obey the commandments.”28 Although it can be lost through mortal sin, it is also called habitual grace because of its stability and permanence. This grace is something real, created by God. “The first creation,” St Thomas explains, “occurred when things were produced by God from nothing, each thing in accordance with its own nature; each creature was then new. Through sin, however, creation became old, and so it was necessary for a new creation to make things over in the order of grace. Now this arises out of nothing, since those who lack grace are nothing29 ... and so it is clear that the infusion of grace is a new creation.”30 Thus, sanctifying grace should not be confused with the divine nature itself or with the Holy Spirit. This is what is implied by the Council of Trent when it states that “the only formal cause (of justification) is God’s justice, not that by which he himself is just, but that by which he makes us just.”31 Because it is a created reality that is distinct from God, sanctifying grace is a gift proper to each believer. This gift can increase or decrease, depending on God’s will and on a person’s dispositions.32 “There is as great a difference,” says St Augustine, “between the light which illuminates and that which is illuminated by it, between the wisdom which creates and the wisdom which has been created, as there is between the

27 Pius XI, Enc. Casti Connubii, 31 December 1930, Dz 2237 (3714). 28 St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 42, Dz 1042 (1942). 29 Cf 1 Cor 13:2. 30 St Thomas Aquinas, In II Epist. S. Pauli ad Cor., 4, 192. 31 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 799 (1529). 32 Cf Ibid, can. 11 and 24, Dz 821 and 834 (1561 and 1574).

The Life of Grace 167

justice that justifies and the justice that results from justification.”33 Grace, then, is a limited participation in God’s nature, an imperfect imitation of God’s most perfect being. Because it is a principle of divine operations, the person in a state of grace is truly God-like, though he does not cease to be a creature. You are gods, sons of the Most High, all of you; nevertheless, you shall die like men, and fall like any prince.34 Grace does not violate nature; rather it builds on it and strengthens it. The supernatural order ... does not destroy or weaken the natural order ... on the contrary, it elevates and perfects it. Each order helps and complements the other, in accordance with its nature and dignity, because both have their origin in God, who cannot contradict himself: his work is perfect; for all his ways are justice35.”36 “Far from renouncing the activities of this life, far from suppressing his natural powers, the true Christian nurtures and perfects these, uniting them with his supernatural life in such a way that the life of nature is enhanced and provided with more effective aids, not only for the attainment of spiritual and eternal goods, but also for the needs of the natural order.”37 Strengthened and raised to the supernatural order by sanctifying grace, man can already know the intimate perfections of God here on earth. He can live with God’s own life, though obviously in a limited and imperfect way, by means of the theological virtues of faith, hope and charity. These virtues come with grace,38 and they raise the human mind and will to the supernatural order, enabling man to know and love God in his unity of nature and substance and in his trinity of Persons. This is the great boldness of the Christian faith: to proclaim the

33 St Augustine, Confessions, 12, 15, 20. 34 Ps 81:6-7. 35 Deut 32:4. 36 Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929, Dz 2206 (3689). 37 Ibid, Dz 2224; cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 38 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1530).

168 On Christian life

value and dignity of human nature and to affirm that we have been created to achieve the dignity of children of God, through the grace that raises us up to a supernatural level. An incredible boldness it would be, were it not founded on the promise of salvation given us by God the Father, were it not confirmed by the blood of Christ, and reaffirmed and made possible by the constant action of the Holy Spirit.39

The indwelling of the Blessed Trinity in the souls of the just The elevation to the supernatural order is a call to penetrate the intimacy of God’s own life, to know and to love God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit, and also to love – in the Trinity and the Unity of God – all the angels and all men.40 Grace divinizes human nature and makes it capable of acting in a supernatural way. The theological virtues and the gifts of the Holy Spirit strengthen and elevate man’s intellect and will to such an extent that “man no longer acts in a purely human fashion but as a god by participation.”41 The soul is deified: its new life contrasts with its former life and with that so often found in its surroundings! Faith tells us that a soul in the state of grace is truly a divinized soul.42 In fact, through the influence of the soul in grace, this divinization also affects the body, as a kind of anticipation of its glorious resurrection.43 This good divinization, as our Father liked to call it, is based on a very profound truth: “By means of grace God dwells in the just soul as in a temple, in a unique and intimate manner.”44 This is a supernatural presence of God within man, not just the natural presence by which God

39 Christ is passing by, 133. 40 Ibid. 41 St Thomas Aquinas, In III Sent., d. 34, q. 1, a. 3. 42 From our Father, Letter 24 March 1931, 3. 43 Ibid. 44 Leo XIII, Enc. Divinum illud, 9 May 1897 (Dz 3330).

The Life of Grace 169

keeps all things in existence. And it is infinitely more perfect than the presence (also in the natural order) of God in creatures endowed with understanding and will. “Even in an evil man there appear some vestiges of divine power and wisdom; but as regards charity, the distinguishing note of the presence of the Holy Spirit, no one shares in it except the just man.”45 Only those who are divinized by grace fulfil the words of Scripture: Do you not know that your body is a temple of the Holy Spirit within you, which you have from God?46 Contemplative prayer – comments our Father – will rise up within you whenever you meditate on this impressive reality: something as material as my body has been chosen by the Holy Spirit as his dwelling place ... I no longer belong to myself ... My body and my soul, my whole being, belong to God.47 Christ himself has revealed to us this sublime mystery, which is something man could never even suspect: the Spirit of truth whom the world cannot receive ... will dwell with you, and be in you.48 If a man loves me, our Lord taught the apostles, he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and make our home with him.49 The indwelling of God in the soul is “a hidden mystery which during our exile on earth can never be completely unveiled, never altogether understood or adequately expressed in human language.”50 Yet it can be understood to some extent by comparison with other supernatural mysteries and the ultimate end all men are to seek.51 “We say that the divine Persons ‘indwell’, because, since they are present in a mysterious way to living intelligent creatures, they are attained by

45 Ibid. 46 1 Cor 6:19; cf 1 Cor 3:16; 2 Cor 6:16; Rom 8:11. 47 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 121. 48 John 14:17. 49 John 14:23. 50 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943, Dz 2290 (3815). 51 Cf First Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Dei Filius, ch. 4, Dz 1796 (3016); cf Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943, Dz 2290 (3815).

170 On Christian life

them through knowledge and love,52 though in a manner which transcends all nature and is quite intimate and unique.”53 “This admirable union,” as the Church continues to teach, “differs only in regard to condition and state, from that in which God fills the blessed when he grants them eternal happiness.”54 Thus the human creature begins to enjoy a foretaste of the beatific vision of heaven, where “it will be possible in an absolutely indescribable manner to contemplate the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit with the eyes of the mind elevated by a supernatural light; to witness for all eternity, close at hand, the procession of the divine Persons; and to enjoy a beatitude very similar to that with which the most holy and undivided Trinity is blessed.”55 The precise way in which this indwelling occurs is something left to theologians to investigate. However, “all must, under pain of departing from pure doctrine and from the true teaching of the Church, hold as quite certain that any explanation of this mystical union is to be rejected if it makes the faithful in any way pass beyond the order of created things and so trespass upon the divine sphere; for no one single attribute of the eternal God can be predicated of them in the proper sense.”56 God’s indwelling in the soul does not eliminate the distance between the Creator and the creature, the infinite difference between God’s being and ours. “Moreover, this certain truth must be firmly borne in mind: that in these matters all things are to be held in common to the Trinity, so far as the same relates to God as the supreme efficient cause.”57 Although normally this supernatural indwelling of God is attributed to the Holy

52 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I, q. 43, a. 3. 53 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943, Dz 2290 (3815). 54 Leo XIII, Enc. Divinum illud, 9 May 1897 (Dz 3331). 55 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943, Dz 2290 (3815). 56 Ibid, Dz 2290 (3814). 57 Ibid.

The Life of Grace 171

Spirit,58 “it really occurs through the presence of the whole Trinity,”59 since all the divine operations ad extra – those which terminate outside God himself – should be referred to the one divine nature.60

Influence of the state of grace on moral life Sanctifying grace divinizes a Christian; it imprints Christ’s features on our soul and makes us an adopted child of God and a temple of the most holy Trinity. This likeness of being should necessarily be reflected in our thoughts, words and deeds, to such an extent that purely human life gives way to Christ’s life. He must increase, but I must decrease.61 As St Paul says: we should have in our hearts the same sentiments which were in Christ’s.62 “Therefore, let all those who glory in the Christian name not only look upon our divine Saviour as the most sublime and most perfect model of all virtues; but, by careful avoidance of sin and by the unremitting practice of holiness, let them also reproduce in their conduct his teaching and life, so that when the Lord appears they may be like him in glory, seeing him as he is.”63 We have been taught by our Father that Christ’s life repeats itself in the life of each one of us in some way, both in the interior development, in our sanctification, and in external events. This increasing resemblance to Christ to which the Holy Spirit leads us,64 aims at full identification and union with him, which will be complete only in heaven. Until then, God himself carries on his work within us through

58 Cf Symbolum Epiphanii, Dz 13 (44); Major Symbol of the Armenian Church (Dz 48); St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, prop. 13, 63, Dz 1013 and 1063 (1913 and 1963). 59 Leo XIII, Enc. Divinum illud, 9 May 1897 (Dz 3330). 60 Cf Council of Florence, Bull Cantate Domino, Dz 704 (1331). 61 John 3:30. 62 Phil 2:5. 63 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943; cf 1 John 3:2. 64 Cf 2 Cor 3:18.

172 On Christian life

the grace he abundantly pours out in the sacraments. “All the members must be formed in his likeness until Christ be formed in them.65 For this reason, we who have been made like to him, who have died with him and risen with him, are taken up into the mysteries of his life, until we reign together with him66.”67 The Spirit of the Lord, who dwells in the heart of the just man, gives supernatural value to all his actions. Likewise the Spirit helps us in our weakness; for we do not know how to pray as we ought, but the Spirit himself intercedes for us with sighs too deep for words.68 Because of the Holy Spirit’s action, the moral activity of a Christian in the state of grace can properly be called divine, because it proceeds from God’s action and man’s free response. But a humble and sincere response on man’s part is necessary: the Church’s teaching authority warns against “the dangerous error of those who attempt to deduce, from our union with Christ, a kind of rash quietism which attributes the whole spiritual life of the Christian and his progress in virtue solely to the action of the divine Spirit, excluding and showing contempt for the cooperation and help that we must lend. Nobody, it is true, can deny that the Holy Spirit of Jesus Christ is the one source of all supernatural virtue for both the Church and her members ... Nevertheless, that men should be persevering and constant in their holy deeds, that they should advance with fervour in grace and in virtue, that they should not only strive toward the summit of Christian perfection but also stimulate others as far as possible to achieve it: all this is something that the heavenly Spirit does not wish to bring about without men also playing their part with active daily diligence.”69

65 Cf Gal 4:19. 66 Cf Phil 3:21; 2 Tim 2:11; Eph 2:6; Col 2:12. 67 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 7. 68 Rom 8:26. 69 Pius XII, Enc. Mystici Corporis, 29 June 1943.

The Life of Grace 173

A Christian’s moral activity should be aimed at cooperating with God in the task of his sanctification by removing obstacles to the action of the Holy Spirit and carrying out actions pleasing to God. My food is to do the will of him who sent me, to accomplish his work.70 In practice, this necessary human cooperation can be summarized, following Christ’s example, under three principal headings: docility to divine inspiration, life of prayer and spirit of penance. First of all, docility, our Father writes, because it is the Holy Spirit who, with his inspirations, gives a supernatural tone to our thoughts, desires and actions. It is he who leads us to adhere to Christ’s teaching and to assimilate it in a profound way. It is he who gives us the light by which we perceive our personal calling and the strength to carry out all that God expects of us. If we are docile to the Holy Spirit, the image of Christ will be formed more and more fully in us, and we will come closer every day to God the Father. For whoever are led by the Spirit of God, they are the children of God (Rom 8:14) ... Secondly, a life of prayer, because the giving of one’s self, the obedience and meekness of a Christian, are born of love and lead to love. And love leads to a personal relationship, to conversation and friendship. Christian life requires a constant dialogue with God, One and Three, and it is to this intimacy that the Holy Spirit leads us. For who among men knows the things of a man save the spirit of the man which is in him? Even so, the things of God no one knows but the Spirit of God (1 Cor 2:11). If we have a constant relationship with the Holy Spirit, we will become spiritual ourselves, we will realize that we are Christ’s brothers and sisters and children of God, and we will not hesitate to call upon Him as our Father at any time (cf Gal 4:6; Rom 8:15) ... And finally, union with the Cross, because in the life of Christ the

70 John 4:34.

174 On Christian life

Resurrection and Pentecost were preceded by Calvary. This is the order of events that must be followed in the life of every Christian. We are, as St Paul tells us, joint heirs with Christ, provided, however, we suffer with him, that we may also be glorified with him (Rom 8:17). The Holy Spirit is the fruit of the Cross, of total dedication to God, of seeking only his glory and of renouncing ourselves completely.71 “The followers of Christ, called by God not in virtue of their works but by his design and grace, and justified in the Lord Jesus, have been made sons of God in the baptism of faith and partakers of the divine nature, and so are truly sanctified. They must therefore hold on to and perfect in their lives that sanctification which they have received from God.”72 To attain the sublime end to which God calls us, “the true Christian has to live a supernatural life in Christ who is our life,73 and display that life in everything that he does, so that the life of Jesus may be manifested in our mortal flesh74.”75 The divine seed that has taken root in our soul, a life based on knowing and loving the most blessed Trinity, will reach its full development in heaven, if here on earth we strive, with God’s help, to act in accord with our new existence. As St Paul says: Strip off the old man with his deeds and put on the new, one that is being renewed unto perfect knowledge according to the image of his Creator.76 We all, with faces unveiled, reflecting as in a mirror the glory of the Lord, are being transformed into his very image from glory to glory, as through the Spirit of the Lord.77

71 Christ is passing by, 135-137. 72 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 73 Cf Col 3:4. 74 2 Cor 4:11. 75 Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929. 76 Col 3:9-10. 77 2 Cor 3:18.

## MORALITY AND THE VIRTUES (I) {#cuaderno.2.14}

Christian life stems from God’s special choice. He selects souls and enriches them as chosen and destined by God the Father and sanctified by the Spirit for obedience to Jesus Christ and for sprinkling with his blood.1 God’s call is all grace; it is an unmerited gift filling us with good things, above all, divine filiation.2 It also awakens us to the requirements of our new status: As obedient children, do not be conformed to the passions of your former ignorance, but as he who called you is holy, be holy yourselves in all your conduct; since it is written, “You shall be holy, for I am holy.”3 The call to holiness does not imply a departure from our earthly path: The ordinary life of a man among his fellow citizens is not something dull and uninteresting. It is in these circumstances that God wants the vast majority of his children to achieve sanctity ... God calls us through what happens during our day: through the suffering and joy of those we live with, through the human interests of our colleagues and the trifles of family life. He also calls us through the great problems, conflicts and challenges of each period of history, which attract the effort and idealism of a large part of mankind.4

1 1 Pet 1:2. 2 Cf Rom 7:16-17. 3 1 Pet 1:14-16. 4 Christ is passing by, 110.

176 On Christian life

Holiness, the fullness of Christian life Holiness entails much more than just being naturally good. We are called to be supernaturally good, sharing in the divine goodness which Christ taught us was the only goodness worthy of the name: Why do you call me good? No one is good but God alone.5 Holiness requires a prompt, constant and persevering acceptance of God’s will, and a no less constant effort to develop the gifts accompanying God’s call. It implies a struggle to begin and begin again, whenever necessary.6 Our effort to improve reflects uprightness and steadfastness in doing good, which is characteristic of a fully Christian life. Sanctity: how often we utter this word as if it were an empty sound. For many people it is an unattainable ideal, something to do with ascetical theology, but not a real goal for them, a living reality. The first Christians didn’t think that way. They often used the word “saints” to describe each other in a very natural manner: greetings to all the saints (Rom 16:15); greet every saint in Christ Jesus (Phil 4:21).7 There is an all too common tendency to emphasize sanctity’s more spectacular aspects, such as great trials, extraordinary circumstances, even martyrdom. This seems to imply that Christian life lived to the full is an enterprise for very select souls, exceptional people, always few in number. Yet, God’s quite explicit intention is that everybody should seek his intimacy: This is the will of God, your sanctification.8 This call promises Christians a hard life, a life of risk and of constant tension. How Christianity has been disfigured when someone has tried to turn it into an easy and comfortable life! But it is also disfiguring the truth to maintain that this deep, serious way of life,

5 Luke 18:19. 6 Cf The Way, 292. 7 Christ is passing by, 96. 8 1 Thess 4:3.

Morality and the Virtues (I) 177

which is totally bound up with all the difficulties of human existence, is something full of anguish, oppression or fear. The Christian is a realist. His supernatural and human realism helps him appreciate all the aspects of his life: sorrow and joy, his own and other people’s suffering, certainty and doubt, generosity and the inclination to selfishness. The Christian experiences all this, and he confronts it all, with human integrity and with the strength he receives from God.9 By pouring out his gifts through Jesus our Saviour,10 God has made it possible for all souls to aim at a full and perfect Christian life.
