He strengthens us with his grace; and with grace comes the perfect law of charity,11 revealing to us his salvific will, his thoughts of love and peace for men. In the words of the prophet Jeremiah: I know the plans I have for you, says the Lord, plans for welfare and not for evil, to give you a future and a hope.12

The nature of moral virtues In transforming us, God counts on our freedom. Divine gifts may far surpass the bounds of human nature, but they never act at variance with it. The supernatural order does not destroy or pass over the natural order. “On the contrary, it elevates and perfects the natural order. Each order helps and complements the other, in accordance with its nature and dignity, since both have their origin in God, who cannot contradict himself.”13 God’s help is necessary for morally upright behaviour; without it participation in divine life would be impossible. But this supernatural help is effective because man is capable of receiving and adapting to it.

9 Christ is passing by, 60. 10 Cf Tit 3:6. 11 Cf Jas 1:25. 12 Jer 29:11. 13 Pius XI, Enc. Divini illius Magistri, 31 December 1929.

178 On Christian life

He can act in harmony with God’s salvific initiative: in a word, he can obey grace.14 Within human nature there are different capacities for seconding God’s will. With a view to the development of a Christian life that is sincerely and stably oriented to goodness, the Creator has so constituted the human person that good actions leave an imprint on the soul in the form of a habit. Good habits perfect and improve nature, thus making perseverance on the path to goodness easier: that is what a virtue is. In the words of St Thomas: “Virtue is what makes the person possessing it good, and his works good likewise.”15 Virtue is not simply a natural disposition to behave in a certain fashion: to be patient in adversity or loyal to one’s commitments, and so on. These inclinations can serve as a good foundation for growth in Christian life. But true virtue is not simply the result of know-how, experience, or an impulsive decision – or even a pondered decision to seek a particular good objective, such as having a good name, pleasure or riches; or avoiding some evil, such as being laughed at, or suffering pain and harm.16 Virtue takes root in the soul, perfecting it, when man acts morally, with his mind fixed on the unlimited good that is God. Thus St Augustine defines virtue as “a good quality of the soul enabling man to live well, which no one can use for evil,”17 since virtue directs man’s moral behaviour toward his last end. “The true dignity and excellence of man consists, then, in virtue. Virtue is the common patrimony of all mortal men, and can be equally reached by high and low, by rich and poor. The reward of eternal happiness is only given to those replete with virtue and merit.”18

14 Cf 1 Cor 15:10; 2 Cor 6:1; Gal 3:1, 5:7; 1 Tim 4:14; 2 Tim 1:6. 15 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 55, a. 3. 16 Ibid, II-II, q. 123, a. 1 ad 2. 17 St Augustine, Retractationes, I, 9. 18 Leo XIII, Enc. Rerum Novarum, 15 May 1891.

Morality and the Virtues (I) 179

Importance of the virtues in moral life Christian life immediately aims at developing virtues. That is why we must learn to do good.19 We must be resolutely determined to fulfil God’s will. As virtue takes root in our soul, we are led gently but continually toward perfection. In the beginning virtue points us toward small, feasible goals; but as we progress, it makes us more fully appreciate the marvels of true goodness and urges us to practice it with greater promptness and joy. Indeed “human perfection consists in nothing other than the knowledge and practice of virtue.”20 In this fight we can always count on God’s help. Grace not only heals and strengthens our nature, making the growth of natural virtues easier, but with it come supernatural virtues, other pure gifts of God.21 Whence they are called infused virtues. His divine power has granted to us all things that pertain to life and godliness, through the knowledge of him who called us to his own glory and excellence, by which he has granted to us his precious and very great promises, that through these you may escape from the corruption that is in the world because of passion, and become partakers of the divine nature. For this very reason make every effort to supplement your faith with virtue, and virtue with knowledge, and knowledge with self-control, and self-control with steadfastness, and steadfastness with godliness, and godliness with brotherly affection, and brotherly affection with love. For if these things are yours and abound, they keep you from being ineffective or unfruitful in the knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ. For whoever lacks these things is blind and short-sighted and has forgotten that he was cleansed from his old sins. Therefore,

19 Is 1:17. 20 Leo XIII, Enc. Sapientiae Christianae, 10 January 1890. 21 Cf Council of Vienne, Const. De Summa Trinit. et Fide Cath., Dz 483 (904); Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, can. 11, Dz 800, 821 (1530, 1531, 1561); Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 2, 51.

180 On Christian life

brethren, be the more zealous to confirm your call and election.22 Virtues are necessary to our vocation. We need them to carry out the tasks God entrusts to each of us as a share in the redemptive work of his Son. But virtue is also a realization of that call. Christian virtues, both natural and infused, perfect in us the image of the one and only perfect model, our Lord Jesus Christ. They identify us with him, awakening in us his sentiments,23 and enable us to follow his example.24 The natural and infused virtues do this in different ways.

The role of human virtues Natural virtues are the ground for supernatural virtues. To deny the goodness of these operative habits, as did the Jansenists, would echo the Protestant teaching on the radical perversion of human nature.25 To despise their need, as did the spiritualism of the Quietists, who were condemned for this error,26 bespeaks ignorance of what it means to be human and to relate to the world around us. Whatever is truly human is elevated and perfected by grace without its essence being changed in any way. To reach their goal, Christians, like everyone else, have to struggle to overcome difficulties and dangers, even at the cost of fortune, of reputation and of life.27 In the order of grace as in the order of nature, goodness never imposes itself on the will, nor can we ever achieve

22 2 Pet 1:3-10. 23 Cf Phil 2:5. 24 Cf John 13:15; 1 Cor 4:16, 11:1; 2 Pet 2:21. 25 Cf St Pius V, Bull Ex Omnibus Afflictionibus, 1 October 1567, 16, 25, 36, 37, 38, 62; Dz 1016, 1025, 1036, 1037, 1038, 1062 (1916, 1925, 1936, 1937, 1938, 1962): Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, 7-9, Dz 1297-1299 (2307-2309); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 44-67, Dz 1394-1417 (2444-2467). 26 Cf Innocent XI, Decree of the Holy Office, 28 August 1687, and Const. Coelestis Pastor, 20 November 1687, Dz 1221ff (2201ff); Innocent XII, Brief Cum Alias ad Apostolatus, 12 March 1699, prop. 18, Dz 1344 (2368). 27 The Way, 519.

Morality and the Virtues (I) 181

good effortlessly: violenti rapiunt,28 the violent take it by storm. A Christian must also moderate his passions, lest he stray from his Godgiven end and seek a permanent dwelling in the earthly city, instead of looking towards the city which is to come.29 And since no Christian travels this road alone, but only in the company of his peers and forming part of the society of God’s children, he must practice justice by giving to each his due and honouring the commitments he has undertaken before God and men. Furthermore, since a Christian lives in a world of palpable realities where he strives to discover objectively what God expects of him at each moment, he must be prudent: he must use the right means at the right time.30 Human virtues are especially important for a Christian, called to share in Christ’s life and redemptive mission by sanctifying temporal concerns and the duties proper to his state. These virtues contribute to perfecting the human realities that make up and define his Christian vocation. They help you to do your work perfectly, to love God and your fellow-men by putting love into the little things of everyday life, and discovering that divine something which is hidden in small details (...) I assure you, my children, that when a Christian carries out with love the most insignificant everyday action, that action overflows with the transcendence of God.31 That is why a principal part of Opus Dei’s spirit is to foster human virtues in the lives, the characters of my daughters and sons. Our Mother the Work wants us to be friends of freedom and of personal responsibility, to be sincere, loyal, generous, self-sacrificing, optimistic, tenacious, determined, with an upright intention and a capacity for work.32

28 Matt 11:12. 29 Heb 13:14. 30 Cf Luke 12:42. 31 Conversations with Monsignor Escrivá, 116. 32 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 52.

182 On Christian life

The cardinal virtues Traditional Christian teaching sums up natural moral activity in the practice of four fundamental virtues: prudence, justice, fortitude and temperance. These are called cardinal virtues because on them hinges our natural moral life. In the life of a Christian, however, it is impossible to discern where natural moral activity ends and supernatural activity begins, since grace elevates, supernaturalizes and divinizes everything. Prudence enables a person to find the most apt means to achieve the good.33 A prudent attitude is not one of suspicion, or mediocrity, or shrewdness. Rather prudence involves reasoning correctly – and supernaturally if one is in the state of grace – in order to find in each case the best way of serving God and souls.34 To be prudent, more than faith and moral knowledge are required: we must also know the pertinent facts, the people involved and the circumstances, if we are to draw fruitful lessons. Thus we must reflect on our own experience and ask advice from persons who can give it. On the basis of this knowledge, we then have to judge – in the light of faith and moral principles, and with the help of grace – what is really important in a particular situation. But it is only at a third stage that prudence most fully comes into play: when, after selecting the most suitable means to reach an end, all the advice and judgments are put into practice.35 So crucial is prudence to morality that it has been called the guide of all virtue, for it directs and regulates the other virtues. That is why we need this habit if we wish to build a strong Christian life. Our Lord wants us to be prudent, thoughtful and measured in all we do; docile in learning and in carrying out what is asked of us; prompt to skirt

33 Cf Prov 3:14-15. 34 Cf Rom 8:6. 35 Cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., II-II, q. 47, a. 3.

Morality and the Virtues (I) 183

danger, with a balanced spirit of initiative; ready to judge – if ours is the duty – when we possess all the necessary information; and he wants us to flee habitually from excessive concern for temporal things.36 Justice leads us to give everyone his due, thus respecting the rights of others. The virtue of justice will prompt us, with priestly soul and lay outlook, to consider our dependence on God in all things, giving to God what is God’s and to Caesar what is Caesar’s.37 We owe God a boundless love above all things, adoration, worship, unconditional obedience, and gratitude for all his gifts, both in the order of nature and grace. Others have a right to their good name, to their property, to their lives, which are God-given gifts nobody can harm with impunity. The virtue of justice will lead us to cherish freedom – in the first place, the lawful freedom of others, because only then will we have the right to defend our own freedom – the freedom that Christ has won for us (cf Gal 4:31). We will defend truth, which will always make us free: veritas liberabit vos (John 8:32). We will be grateful, affable, generous, hardworking; we will be sincere, loyal and faithful friends: vos autem dixi amicos (John 15:14) ... And we will know how to make amends for our mistakes, remembering what I have often taught you: one of my greatest joys is to rectify, when justice requires rectification.38 You must also practice the human virtue of fortitude, which gives strength to the soul in its pursuit of good, even if difficult, without being held back by fear. It will also make you patient and persevering.39 Courage helps us to overcome the defects of our temperament and gladly put up with adversity which seasons our life, without shrinking before difficulties. By growing in fortitude we will persevere as upright men and women, who fulfil the resolutions they have made no

36 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 54. 37 Ibid, 57. 38 Ibid, 58. 39 Ibid, 62.

184 On Christian life

matter what the cost, particularly in the little things of each day. Fortitude will also give you greatness of soul, nobility of character and magnanimity, which is a resolute willingness to undertake great things for God and neighbour. Your vocation to serve our Lord and his Church, our Father writes, requires of you this spiritual disposition.40 The fourth cardinal virtue is temperance, which moderates the use of earthly goods and helps us fix our hearts on heaven where no thief approaches and no moth destroys.41 Temperance asks us to be modest, sober, clement, humble; it makes us love decency, decorum, moderation, gentleness and mortification.42 If all the cardinal virtues are important to a Christian, temperance often has a special practical relevance, faced as we are with so many incitements to pleasure and consequently with continual occasions to practice this virtue. People expect from us, who are children of God in his Work, the bonus odor Christi which – supported by our temperance – enkindles them and draws them forward. Let us try to do everything with moderation, because that’s what temperance is all about. It is a cardinal virtue (from ‘cardo’ meaning hinge or pivot), a firm point of support. And in the firmness of that cardinal virtue of yours, your friends will find support, almost without realizing it; and you, in practice, will give spiritual guidance to many people who do not even know what spiritual direction is, and who perhaps would never want it.43 A Christian must not confine himself to practicing these virtues in a human manner, because there is no human perfection without supernatural perfection. Moral virtues of the natural order, it is true, are perfect

40 Ibid, 62-63. 41 Luke 12:33. 42 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 64. 43 Ibid, 65.

Morality and the Virtues (I) 185

in their kind:44 they render a person capable of “carrying out good works directed to an end in keeping with man’s natural capacity.”45 But when it is a question of attaining a supernatural end, human efforts are not enough: a further intervention of God is required. This transformation of natural virtues into supernatural ones, which perfects man with regard to his last end, is carried out through charity.46 “With regard to the virtues that lead to a holy life,” St Augustine wrote, “I affirm that they are nothing but the sign and summary of love for God. As I understand it, the four forms of virtue proceed from the four forms that love assumes. I would not hesitate to describe the four virtues in the following way: temperance is the love that keeps itself intact for its object; fortitude is the love that easily suffers everything for the sake of what it loves; justice is the love that keeps the right order in things because it seeks only to serve the loved one; prudence is the love that sees clearly everything that is favourable or harmful. However I am not speaking of any kind of love, but of love for God, supreme good, supreme wisdom and unity. Thus we can formulate these definitions with greater precision by saying: temperance is the love that keeps itself whole and intact for God; fortitude is the love that, for God, bears everything lightly; justice is the love that serves only God, and therefore properly orders everything within man’s dominion; prudence is the love that knows how to distinguish rightly between what is advantageous on the path towards God and what is not.”47 Natural moral virtues, which are operative habits acquired by the continual practice of good actions, perfect human nature in its own order. They also help human nature to more easily carry out acts of the supernatural virtues infused by God in the soul along with grace. If we

44 Cf Benedict XIV, Const. De Serv. Dei Beat., 3, 21, 6. 45 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 65, a. 2. 46 Ibid. 47 St Augustine, De Mor. Eccl. Cath., 1, 15, 21.

186 On Christian life

make an effort to practice these human virtues, and also strive to raise them to the supernatural order, we will always be full of joy. We will have the joy that is so proper to our spirit and so pleasing to God, because it helps make the way more pleasant for others, while making it easy for us continually to renew our dedication: in simplicitate cordis mei laetus obtuli universa, because with simplicity of heart we will have been able to give everything to God (cf 1 Chron 29:17).48 When these virtues are supernaturalized, they make us fertile ground for receiving the infused virtues more effectively. At the same time, they are ordinarily a consequence of the theological virtues, because the Holy Spirit dwells in the soul of the just man – in the person who believes in God, who hopes in Him, who loves Him – and acts through the motions of His grace, granting a filial trust in the Father and giving us greater ease in doing good, even humanly speaking. Operatur in vobis et velle et perficere, he makes us love what is good and perfects our actions (Phil 2:13), helping us in a special way: postulat pro nobis gemitibus inenarrabilibus, he intercedes for us in a way impossible to put into words (Rom 8:26), so as to improve our human behaviour.49

48 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 69. 49 Ibid, 53.

## MORALITY AND THE VIRTUES (II) {#cuaderno.2.15}

If then you have been raised with Christ, seek the things that are above, where Christ is, seated at the right hand of God. Set your minds on things that are above, not on things that are on earth ... Put on then, as God’s chosen ones, holy and beloved, compassion, kindness, lowliness, meekness, and patience, forbearing one another and, if one has a complaint against another, forgiving each other ... Above all these put on love, which binds everything together in perfect harmony.1 Moral virtues are the crown of natural morality, the summit of human perfection. However, we should never forget that God has destined all men to a supernatural goal infinitely beyond the capacity of our created nature. If human virtues, because of original sin, already encounter serious difficulties in leading man to natural goodness, such virtues can never guide man to holiness. No matter how much progress we make on our own, we can never surpass the limits of our nature. We do this only through grace, which God infuses into our soul along with charity and “the noble company of all the virtues”.2

The theological virtues, foundation of Christian life The theological virtues occupy pride of place among the gifts that God in his goodness bestows on us along with grace. Because they have God

1 Col 3:1-14. 2 Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 2, 51.

188 On Christian life

himself as their object, they are the foundation and essence of Christian life. The Council of Trent states that in baptism, “in the very act of being justified, along with the remission of his sins, a man receives through Jesus Christ, to whom he is joined, the infused gifts of faith, hope and charity.”3 The theological virtues are God’s gift raising the soul’s powers, thereby enabling us to enter the intimate sphere of God’s own life. Since they are a supernatural and free gift, we must ask God to increase our faith. We must foster our hope of possessing the goods awaiting us, goods that are infinitely greater than all the things we may leave behind here on earth. And we must think about God’s Love with all its splendour, its beauty, its fullness of life, its knowledge ... Thanks to the theological virtues, a Christian is able to know with God’s certainty, to hope with God’s security, to love with God’s own love. This holds true not only in certain moments or situations, but as long as these virtues remain in the soul. Our Father liked to consider how a Christian, in his ordinary life, in the simplest details, in the normal circumstances of a normal day can put faith, hope, and charity into practice. There lies the essence of the conduct of a soul which relies on divine help.4

The life of faith of a Christian A Christian is someone who lives by faith, hope, and charity – three gifts poured into his heart by the Father, in Christ, with the infusion of the Holy Spirit.5 These virtues bring the seed of supernatural life received in baptism to blossom. Thus a Christian truly grows to maturity in Christ our head,6 so that according to the riches of his glory he may grant you to be

3 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 7, Dz 800 (1530). 4 Christ is passing by, 169. 5 Cf Rom 5:5. 6 Cf Eph 4:15.

Morality and the Virtues (II) 189

strengthened with might through his Spirit in the inner man,7 created after the likeness of God in true righteousness and holiness.8 “Faith is the beginning of man’s salvation,”9 “the foundation and root of all justification.”10 In this respect, faith is the primary Christian virtue, for without it neither hope nor charity are possible. By faith we adhere to the one and only God and to Jesus Christ whom he has sent.11 “How closely this faith unites us to God, Jesus’ beloved disciple tells us: Whoever confesses that Jesus is the Son of God, God abides in him, and he in God.12 Christian faith binds us no less closely to one another and to our divine head. For all who believe, having the same spirit of faith,13 are illumined by the same light of Christ, nourished by the same food of Christ, and live under the teaching authority of Christ. If we are all enlivened by the same spirit of faith, we are all living the same life by faith in the Son of God, who loved me and gave himself for me.14 And Christ our head, welcomed by our living faith and dwelling in our hearts,15 will be the perfecter of our faith as he is its author16.”17 In Christian life, faith above all provides knowledge of (God’s) will in all spiritual wisdom and understanding, to lead a life worthy of the Lord, fully pleasing to him, bearing fruit in every good work and increasing in the knowledge of God.18 By faith a Christian can “discern in the events, needs, and longings which he shares with other men of

7 Eph 3:16. 8 Eph 4:24. 9 St Fulgentius, De Fide ad Petrum, 1. 10 Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 8, Dz 801 (1532). 11 Cf John 17:3. 12 1 John 4:15. 13 Cf 2 Cor 4:13. 14 Gal 2:20. 15 Cf Eph 3:17. 16 Cf Heb 12:2. 17 Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 November 1947. 18 Col 1:9-10.

190 On Christian life

our time, the genuine signs of the presence or of the purpose of God. For faith throws a new light on all things and makes known the full ideal which God has set for man, thus guiding the mind toward solutions that are fully human.”19 Faith is a light God gives to those who seek him with a sincere heart, who humbly and constantly search for the signs of the Creator in his creatures, and who open their souls to the intimate action of the Holy Spirit. Faith reminds us that God is always acting. He has created us and keeps us in existence, and he leads all creation by his grace toward the glorious freedom of the children of God (cf Rom 8:21).20 This divine light that helps us fix our gaze on heaven does not of itself remove us from the life we Christians share with other men and women, nor does it spare us their same concerns and duties. Our Father writes: Following Christ does not mean taking refuge in the temple, shrugging one’s shoulders at social development, ignoring the achievements and aberrations alike of men or nations. On the contrary, Christian faith helps us see the world as God’s creation and appreciate all its nobility and beauty, recognizing the dignity of each person, made in God’s image. It helps us admire the very special gift of freedom which makes us masters of our actions and enables us, with God’s grace, to build our eternal destiny.21 “Only the light of faith and meditation on God’s Word can enable us to find everywhere and always the God “in whom we live and exist.”22 Only thus can we seek his will in everything, see Christ in all men, acquaintance or stranger, make sound judgments on the true meaning and value of temporal realities both in themselves and in relation to man’s end.”23

19 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Gaudium et Spes, 11. 20 Christ is passing by, 130. 21 Ibid, 99. 22 Acts 17:28. 23 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 4.

Morality and the Virtues (II) 191

The life of hope of a Christian The theological virtue of faith illuminates the end toward which moral life is directed. It is, however, the theological virtue of hope that confirms us in the certainty of winning the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus.24 “As it is by faith that on this earth we adhere to God as the source of truth, so it is by the virtue of Christian hope that we seek him as source of beatitude, looking for the blessed hope and coming of the glory of the great God.25 This desire for the kingdom of heaven, which makes us want here no lasting city but seek one that is to come and yearn for supernatural glory,26 enabled the Apostle of the Gentiles to affirm: there is one body and one Spirit, just as you were called to the one hope that belongs to your call.27 Christ abides in us as the hope of glory28.29 Certainly faith and knowledge of the truth give hope of eternal life which God, who never lies, promised ages ago.30 Nevertheless, only hope can bring to a Christian’s struggle the tenacity and firmness that faith alone cannot give. Beloved, we are God’s children now; it does not yet appear what we shall be, but we know that when he appears we shall be like him, for we shall see him as he is. And every one who thus hopes in him purifies himself as he is pure.31 Christians “in life’s trials draw courage from hope, convinced that present sufferings are no measure of the future glory to be revealed in us.”32 But “far from diminishing our concern to develop this earth, the

24 Phil 3:14. 25 Tit 2:13. 26 Cf Heb 13:14. 27 Eph 4:4. 28 Cf Col 1:27. 29 Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 November 1947. 30 Tit 1:1-2; cf Gal 5:5. 31 1 John 3:2-3. 32 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Apostolicam Actuositatem, 4; cf Rom 8:18.

192 On Christian life

expectancy of a new earth should spur us on.”33 All this is part of our God-entrusted task. Through hope Christians live sober, upright, and godly lives in this world.34 While pilgrims here on earth, it is by hope that we are saved.35 We do not trust in our own strength, but in God’s infinite mercy and promises.36 Let us foster in our souls the virtue of hope, imagining and looking eagerly to our eternal reward, whenever God wishes to give it to us. Perhaps someone will say to me: Isn’t it a little selfish to think of heaven? No. Hope is a great virtue that requires a strong faith. And faith and hope together demand a great love for the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit. See how in this way we live the three theological virtues. If you are faithful, this is the way you will travel spiritually each day almost without realizing it, just as you hardly realize you are breathing.37

The life of love of a Christian “So faith, hope, love abide, these three; but the greatest of these is love.38 If even in the natural order the love of friendship is something supremely noble, what then shall we say of that supernatural love which God pours into our hearts? God is love, and he who abides in love abides in God, and God abides in him.39 The effect of this charity

– such would seem to be God’s law – is to compel him to enter into our

33 Second Vatican Council, Past. Const. Gaudium et Spes, 39. 34 Tit 2:12. 35 Cf Rom 8:24. 36 Cf 2 Tim 4:7-8; Jas 1:12. 37 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 72; cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 11 and can. 26, 31, Dz 804, 836, 841 (1539, 1576, 1581); Innocent XI, Const. Coelestis Pastor, 20 November 1687, prop. 7 and 12, Dz 1227, 1232 (2207, 2212); Clement XI, Const. Unigenitus Dei Filius, 8 September 1713, prop. 57, Dz 1407 (2457). 38 1 Cor 13:13. 39 1 John 4:16.

Morality and the Virtues (II) 193

loving hearts to return love for love, as he said: If a man loves me, he will keep my word, and my Father will love him, and we will come to him and make our home with him.40 Charity, then, more than any other virtue, unites us closely with Christ. On fire with this flame from heaven, how many children of the Church have rejoiced to suffer insults for his sake, joyfully meeting and overcoming the hardest trials, even at the cost of their lives and the shedding of their blood. Therefore our divine Saviour earnestly exhorts us: Abide in my love. And as charity, if it does not issue effectively in good works, is something altogether empty and unprofitable, he immediately adds: If you keep my commandments, you will abide in my love, just as I have kept my Father’s commandments and abide in his love41.”42 Charity is the driving force of Christian life; the virtue of charity “governs, gives meaning to, and perfects all the means of sanctification.”43 Charity is the fullness of the morality established by Christ;44 it binds together everything in perfect harmony,45 and is the sign that a Christian has passed from death to life.46 Without charity, faith is dead;47 hope is diminished, though not totally lost: if any one does sin, we have an advocate with the Father, Jesus Christ the righteous; and he is the expiation for our sins, and not for ours only but also for the sins of the whole world.48 Charity is the virtue that imbues Christian life with its most characteristic features of joy, generosity and simplicity. It also confers

40 John 14:23. 41 John 15:9-10. 42 Pius XII, Enc. Mediator Dei, 20 November 1947. 43 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 42. 44 Cf Rom 13:10. 45 Col 3:14. 46 Cf 1 John 3:14. 47 Cf Jas 2:17; Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 28, Dz 838 (1578). 48 1 John 2:1-2.

194 On Christian life

greatness of soul, since by charity a person begins to seek and love God

– even before possessing him completely – with all his heart, with all his soul, with all his mind and strength.49 Following Christ’s example,50 a Christian loves all men and women as his brothers and sisters, destined as they are for the same supernatural end. Love for God, and love for one’s neighbour, trying to be generous, without limit or restrictions, with an open and understanding mind. First of all, love for God, which is the vinculum perfectionis (Col 3:14). The measure I give to you for loving God is to love him without measure. Out of love for God, love for one’s neighbour: live in peace with others, excuse and forgive. Charity is the basis of the Christian ideal and our religion’s most lovable virtue.51

Growing in faith, hope and charity Christian existence means to live by faith, hope and charity: allowing God to take possession of our lives and to change our hearts radically, to make us resemble him more and more. A mature, profound and strong Christian life is something which cannot be improvised, because it is the fruit of the growth of God’s grace in us.52 While the natural virtues result from man’s free moral action, the theological virtues are exclusively God’s gift. They are infused virtues we have through God’s pure generosity: we have not merited them, nor can we acquire them by our intelligence. To grow in these virtues a Christian must have recourse to the sources of grace – the sacraments of the Church – and cooperate with God in bringing forth good deeds.53

49 Cf Matt 12:30. 50 Cf John 13:34; 15:12. 51 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 73. 52 Christ is passing by, 134. 53 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 10, Dz 803 (1535).

Morality and the Virtues (II) 195

The very exercise of the infused virtues contributes to their growth, as the merit won brings about an increase of grace and virtue.54 The soul likewise becomes better disposed to receive the sanctifying action of the Holy Spirit. St Thomas says: “There are two types of virtue, acquired and infused. Moral action contributes to both, but each in a different way: it generates acquired virtue and disposes one to receive infused virtue. Once this latter is obtained, moral action preserves and strengthens it.”55 Once the human virtues are supernaturalized, they lead us to practise the theological virtues and to receive more docilely the gifts of the Holy Spirit.56 Like all graces that God lavishes on us with baptism, the theological virtues are the common patrimony of Christians. Thus if “all Christians in any state or walk of life are called to the fullness of the Christian life and to the perfection of love,”57 then the means must be available to all. “Accordingly all Christians, in the conditions, duties and circumstances of their life and through all these, will sanctify themselves more and more if they receive all things with faith from the hand of the heavenly Father and cooperate with the divine will, showing forth in temporal service the love with which God has loved the world.”58 The one thing necessary is to persevere, with God’s help, in this desire and effort. Sanctification is the work of a lifetime. The divine seed of charity which God has sown in our souls yearns to grow, to express itself in deeds, to yield fruits that continually concur with what God wants.59

The Gifts of the Holy Spirit Along with human virtues, my daughters and sons, we must foster the

54 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, can. 32, Dz 842 (1582). 55 St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 92, a. 1, ad 1. 56 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 70 57 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 58 Ibid, 41. 59 Christ is passing by, 58.

196 On Christian life

theological virtues – your life of faith, of hope and of charity – given to us in baptism through the ineffable action of the Holy Spirit. The Great Stranger – as I like to call him – lives, as I was saying, in the souls of those who are in God’s grace, transforming them into a dwelling of the Blessed Trinity: templum enim Dei sanctum est: quod estis vos, God’s temple is holy, and you are that temple (1 Cor 3:17).60 Christian life attains its fullness under the influence of the gifts of the Holy Spirit: wisdom, understanding, counsel, fortitude, knowledge, piety and fear of the Lord.61 “The just man, living the life of grace and acting with the appropriate virtues, much as the soul acts through its powers, needs the seven gifts called gifts of the Holy Spirit. Thanks to these, the soul is enabled to follow divine inspirations more promptly and easily. These gifts are so effective that they lead the soul to the heights of sanctity; and they are so excellent that they remain intact – though perfected – in the kingdom of heaven. Through these gifts the Holy Spirit moves and encourages us to make the Gospel beatitudes fully our own.”62 Infused virtues raise our human ways of acting to a supernatural plane, thus enabling us to act in a Christian way. But the gifts of the Holy Spirit add certain operations that are entirely new and divine. They raise Christian life to unsuspected heights and perfection. Thanks to these gifts, the Spirit himself interiorly moves us, melding us to his own way of acting: For all who are led by the Spirit of God are sons of God.63 The gifts of the Holy Spirit are the most exalted manifestation of the action of the three persons of the Blessed Trinity in the souls of the just, dwelling there through grace as in a temple. The possession of the gifts is not confined to any particular state in life or to extraordinary ways of Christian perfection: their purpose is to perfect the supernatural life

60 From our Father, Instruction, May 1935, 14 September 1950, 71. 61 Cf Is 11:2; Synod of Rome, year 382, Decretum Damasi, Dz 83 (178). 62 Leo XIII, Enc. Divinum illud, 9 May 1897. 63 Rom 8:14; cf St Thomas Aquinas, S. Th., I-II, q. 68, a. 1.

Morality and the Virtues (II) 197

initiated by the theological virtues. Since they are inseparable from charity, these gifts are found in all souls in the state of grace. They accompany all Christians on the ordinary paths to holiness. Nor do they depend on one’s cultural development or the level of one’s scientific, professional or religious education, though these forms of knowledge are helpful. It often happens that the Holy Spirit is more active in simple people with scant human knowledge who are truly pious. All that God asks in order to work in us is humble docility. There is a wisdom attained only by sanctity, our Father writes. And there are souls who are hidden, unknown, deeply humble, with a spirit of sacrifice, who are holy and have a wonderful supernatural outlook: I praise thee, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, that thou didst hide these things from the wise and prudent, and didst reveal them to little ones (Matt 11:25). A supernatural outlook which is often lacking in the pompous pronouncements of supposed scholars: evanuerunt in cogitationibus suis, et obscuratum est insipiens cor eorum, dicentes enim se esse sapientes stulti facti sunt (Rom 1:21- 22); they became futile in their thinking and their senseless minds were darkened. Claiming to be wise, they became fools.64 Throughout life we should recognize the Holy Spirit’s inspirations and try to follow them if we are effectively to attain the supernatural end to which we are destined. The ordinary way God makes his will known to us is through spiritual direction, but we also receive the silent, gentle and strong action of his Spirit, who enlightens souls as an interior Teacher65 by means of his seven gifts. Among them there is one which we Christians all need in a special way: the gift of wisdom. It makes us know God and rejoice in his presence, thereby placing us in a position from which we can judge accurately the situations and events of this

64 From our Father, Letter 8 August 1956, 42. 65 From our Father, Letter 6 May 1945, 4.

198 On Christian life

life,66 this common existence which we have to sanctify. The Christian vocation is a life of holiness. We are not destined to pursue just any happiness, because we have been called to penetrate the intimacy of God’s own life, to know and to love God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit, and also to love – in the Trinity and the Unity of God – all the angels and all men. This is the great boldness of the Christian faith: to proclaim the value and dignity of human nature and to affirm that we have been created to achieve the dignity of children of God, through the grace that raises us up to a supernatural level. An incredible boldness it would be, were it not founded on the promise of salvation given us by God the Father, were it not confirmed by the blood of Christ, and reaffirmed and made possible by the constant action of the Holy Spirit.67 The attainment of our final destiny is the aim of moral behaviour. We reach this end, not by isolated acts, but by persevering in the upright use of all our powers. This is the specific purpose of the natural and infused virtues and of the gifts of the Holy Spirit, which raise and perfect human nature. Thus the Christian is led to mature manhood, to the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ,68 attaining complete identification with our Lord Jesus Christ, Teacher and Model of all perfection.69 “The forms and tasks of life are many, but holiness is one: that sanctity which is cultivated by all who act under God’s Spirit and, obeying the Father’s voice and adoring God the Father in spirit and in truth, follow Christ, poor, humble, and cross-bearing, that they may deserve to be partakers of his glory.”70

66 Christ is passing by, 133. 67 Ibid. 68 Eph 4:13. 69 Cf Gal 2:20. 70 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 41.

## BAPTISM, GATEWAY TO CHRISTIAN LIFE (I) {#cuaderno.2.16}

When God created man in his own image and likeness,1 he endowed him with reason with which to know his maker. He also left inscribed on man’s heart a law whereby to judge the goodness and evil of his own actions, a law that reflects the immutable and eternal law whereby God governs all creation. And “by freely and gratuitously elevating man to share with him in life and glory,”2 God made man a participant in the divine nature,3 giving him a supernatural end absolutely surpassing his natural powers and possibilities. To enable man to reach this end, God endowed him with the gifts of grace, which, though lost by Adam’s sin, were more than restored by Christ. Grace renews a man from within and converts him – a sinner and rebel – into a good and faithful servant (cf Matt 25:21). The source of all grace is God’s love for us, and which he has revealed not just in words but also in deeds ... Love is revealed to us in the Incarnation, in Jesus Christ’s redemptive journey on our earth, culminating in the supreme sacrifice of the Cross. And on the Cross he showed his love through a new sign: One of the soldiers pierced his side with a spear, and at once there came

1 Cf Gen 1:26-27. 2 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Ad Gentes, 2. 3 Cf 2 Pet 1:4.

200 On Christian life

out blood and water (John 19:34).4 Divine grace, which gives man the capacity to develop a moral supernatural life, reaches souls principally through the sacraments. Among them, “holy baptism, which is the gateway to the spiritual life, occupies the first place, since through it we become members of Christ and of his body the Church.”5 Baptism enables us to receive fruitfully the other means of grace and to live as God’s children.

Divine origin of Baptism God’s plan has always been that men would be called, and truly be, his children. By baptism, a spiritual regeneration through water and the Holy Spirit, he restores to us the divine life lost through our first parents’ sin. Now cleansed of sin, man can be intimately united with Christ and live as a child of the Father. This filiation is the root and source of strength for all Christian life. In many passages of the Old Testament God makes his saving will known by means of images and prophecies: his will to unite man with himself, to fill him with his grace. A day will come, the prophet Zechariah teaches, when there shall be a fountain opened for the house of David and the inhabitants of Jerusalem to cleanse them from sin and uncleanness.6 And Ezekiel, referring to a new alliance that will replace the old, writes: I will sprinkle clean water upon you, and you shall be clean from all your uncleannesses, and from all your idols I will cleanse you. A new heart I will give you, and a new spirit I will put within you; and I will take out of your flesh the heart of stone and give you a heart of flesh. And I will put my spirit within you, and cause you to walk in my statutes.7

4 Christ is passing by, 162. 5 Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 696 (1314); cf Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 14; Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 3. 6 Zech 13:1. 7 Ezek 36:25-27.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 201

These inspired words show the fervour of God’s love. Like the father of the prodigal son,8 God also comes out to meet man the sinner, offering him purification, a way of reparation and return. Baptismal water cleanses man and transforms him into a new creature through the infusion of the divine life of grace. This mystery is prefigured in the Old Testament. Together with the prophecies and the promises, there are events that symbolize baptism as a shadow of what is to come.9 Circumcision, the passage through the Red Sea, the water Moses brought forth from the rock in the desert – all are figures expressing in particular ways some facets of Christian baptism: the baptized becoming part of God’s people, freedom from slavery to sin and attainment of the life of grace, the supernatural waters that can quench man’s thirst. With Christ, in the fullness of time,10 these divine promises reached their fulfilment. During the years of his public life, Jesus proclaimed this saving mystery by progressively revealing to the Apostles different aspects of the sacrament of baptism. The words of John the Baptist were used for his first proclamation: I baptize you with water for repentance, but he who is coming after me ... will baptize you with the Holy Spirit.11 Although the precursor’s baptism was but a penitential rite and not yet Christian baptism, it did point to the dispositions required to receive baptism: faith in Christ, the Messiah, cause of all grace, and voluntary renunciation of sin. Another stage of this revelation was the baptism of Christ himself in the Jordan. This visibly manifested that the Blessed Trinity is the source of baptismal regeneration.12 It also showed that the proper effect of baptism is divine filiation and the opening up of heaven.

8 Cf Luke 15:11ff. 9 Col 2:17. 10 Cf Gal 4:4. 11 Matt 3:11. 12 Cf Matt 3:16-17.

202 On Christian life

Afterwards, in his conversation with Nicodemus, Jesus revealed clearly his teaching about baptism: it would be a new birth brought about by water and the Holy Spirit, and an absolute requirement for salvation.13 The Fathers of the Church unanimously see this sacrament symbolized in the water that flowed from Christ’s side on the cross.14 However its formal and solemn institution, and its declaration as universally obligatory, followed the resurrection when Jesus took leave of his disciples: Go therefore and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.15 Jesus’ command was soon put into practice by the Apostles and their successors. The Acts tell us that on the very day of Pentecost more than three thousand were baptized.16 By means of this salvific rite they were incorporated into the nascent Church. The divine origin of baptism and the other sacraments has been declared a truth of faith by the Church’s solemn teaching authority.17

Nature of Baptism Like the other sacraments, baptism consists of three essential elements: “things as matter, words as form, and the person of the minister who confers it with the intention of doing what the Church does. If any one of these elements is lacking, the sacrament is not conferred.”18

13 Cf John 3:5. 14 Cf John 19:34. 15 Matt 28:19, cf Mark 16:16. 16 Cf Acts 2:41. 17 Cf Council of Verona, year 1184, Dz 402 (761); Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 465 (860); Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 695 (1310); Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis, can. 1, Dz 844 (1601); Pius IV, Bull Iniunctum Nobis, 13 November 1564, Dz 996 (1864); Benedict XIV, Const. Nuper ad Nos, 16 March 1743, Dz 1470 (2536). 18 Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 695 (1312).

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 203

The matter of baptism is water, an element which signifies perfectly the purifying effect proper to this sacrament.19 In using something so common and simple to signify and confer grace, God gives us a new reason to be grateful.20 “It is all the same,” writes Tertullian, “whether one is baptized in the sea or a pond, in a river or a fountain, in a lake or a well ... All waters, privileged from the beginning to be vehicles of the Spirit, have been changed into a means of sanctification, if God’s name is invoked upon them.”21 The action by which water is poured on the person being baptized constitutes, according to traditional terminology, the ‘proximate matter’ of baptism. The Church permits three forms of administering it: by immersion, aspersion or infusion. For many centuries it has been customary to use the last method.22 The words pronounced by the minister at the moment of administering baptism make up the ‘form’ of the sacrament. This is an essential element, since it specifically determines the significance of the washing with water, which ceases to be a common, ordinary action on becoming a deed of Christ. Faithful to the command of our Lord, the Church-established form for this sacrament gives testimony to the trinitarian origin of baptismal grace:

19 Cf Fourth Lateran Council, Const. De Fide Cath., Dz 430 (802); Council of Vienne, Const. Fidei Cath., Dz 482 (903); Clement VI, Letter Super Quibusdam, 29 September 1351, Dz 574 (1082); Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 696 (1314); Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis, can. 2 De Sacr. Bapt., Dz 858 (1615). 20 No other liquid (beer, milk, etc.) is valid matter for baptism: cf Innocent III, Letter Non Ut Apponeres, 1 March 1206, Dz 412 (787); Gregory IX, Letter Cum Sicut Ex, 8 July 1241, Dz 447 (829). However a small amount of antiseptic may be added to prevent infection when an infant is baptized in the womb: cf Reply of the Holy Office to the Archbishop of Utrecht, 21 August 1901, Dz 1977 (3356). Water used in solemn baptism should be blessed as baptismal water, otherwise the baptism is not licit: cf Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 21. 21 Tertullian, De Bapt., 4. 22 Cf St Zachary, Letter Sacris Liminibus, 1 May 748, Dz 297 (589); Alexander III, Epist. ad Pontium, Dz 398-399 (757-758); Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 22.

204 On Christian life

“I baptize you in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.”23 Thus “if anyone were to have been submerged in the fountain of baptism without invoking the Trinity, he would not be a perfect Christian24.”25 God thus brings about a radical transformation in our soul: he divinizes it, making it a temple of the Holy Spirit and a living member of the Body of Christ. Having been incorporated into the Church, our existence acquires a new supernatural dimension. We begin to live as a Christian, tending each day to be more like Christ, identifying all our thoughts, actions and desires with our Lord’s. These essential elements of the baptismal rite are accompanied by other ceremonies: the blessing of the water or commemoration of a previous blessing, renunciation of the devil, profession of faith, anointings, lighted candles, and so on. Their purpose is to show in ways our senses can perceive what happens in the soul of the baptized: the expulsion of the devil, to whose power man was subject through original sin, and the illumination of grace that enables him to delight in the things of God.

23 Cf St Stephen I, Letter to the Bishops of Asia Minor, year 256, Dz 47 (111); First Council of Arles, year 314, Dz 53 (123); Council of Rome, year 382, prop. 24, Dz 82 (176-177); Innocent I, Letter Magna Me Gratulatio, 13 December 414, Dz 97 (214); Pelagius I, Letter Admonemus ut, 2 February 559, Dz 229 (445); St Gregory II, Letter Desiderabilem Mihi, 22 November 726, Dz 296a (580); St Gregory III, Letter Magna Nos Habuit, year 732, Dz 582; Council of Rome, year 862, Dz 329 (637); Nicholas I, Answer to the Bulgarians, 13 November 866, Dz 334- 335 (644-646); Fourth Lateran Council, Const. De Fide Cath., Dz 430 (802); Council of Vienne, Const. Fidei Cath., Dz 482 (903); Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 23. 24 St Zachary, Letter Sacris Liminibus, 1 May 748, Dz 297a (589). 25 The words “I baptize you,” or other similar words, are always necessary for validity because they determine the meaning of the action that is being carried out: cf Alexander III, Epist. ad Pontium, Dz 398 (757); Alexander VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 7 December 1690, prop. 27, Dz 1317 (2327). That is why the deprecatory formula used in oriental rites is valid: cf Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 696 (1311). Defective pronunciation of the words through ignorance of the language or through an involuntary error, does not invalidate the sacrament: cf St Zachary, Letter Virgilius et Sedonius, 1 July 746, Dz 297 (588); Stephen II, Answer to a Monastery of Quiercy, year 754 (592).

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 205

Minister of Baptism The minister of baptism “is the priest to whom pertains the office to baptize.”26 “However, in case of necessity, not only can a priest or deacon baptize, but also a lay man or woman, even a pagan or heretic, as long as the form established by the Church is used and there is the intention of doing what the Church does.”27 In any case the minister cannot be the baptismal candidate, for no one can baptize himself.28 Through the minister, the Church baptizes by virtue of the power received from her Founder. For this reason the Magisterium has always taught that the efficacy of baptism and the other sacraments does not depend on the minister’s faith or state of grace, although these conditions are most advisable. “All those who receive baptism, even if it were from the hands of a drunkard, a murderer or an adulterer,” St Augustine strongly affirms, repeating the Church’s official teaching, “are baptized by Christ.”29 The sacraments are actions of Christ himself, and they produce grace by virtue of their own power, ex opere operato, and not as a result of the merits of those who administer them, even when the recipient’s personal dispositions could limit or even impede the sacramental effects.30 In the soul of a person properly disposed, baptism works with all its vivifying and sanctifying force: another reason for giving thanks.

26 “The ordinary ministers of baptism are bishops, priests and deacons,” one reads in the Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 11. The duty of administering baptism falls principally on whoever has care of souls, though other “priests and deacons ... prepare people for baptism, and if the bishop or the parish priest asks them, or consents to their doing so, can also administer it.” (Ibid, 14). 27 Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 696 (1315); cf Benedict XIV, Const. Nuper ad Nos, 16 March 1743, Dz 1470 (2536); C.I.C. can. 861; Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 17. 28 Cf Innocent III, Letter Debitum Officii Pontificalis, 28 August 1206, Dz 413 (788). 29 St Augustine, In Ioann. Ev. Tract., 5, 18. 30 Cf Nicholas I, Reply to the Bulgarians, 13 November 866, Dz 334a (644); cf St Gregory II, Letter Desiderabilem Mihi, 22 November 726, Dz 296a (580).

206 On Christian life

In putting this teaching into practice, the Church has always refused to rebaptize those who have been baptized by a non-Catholic or heretic, if in its administration all the conditions necessary for its validity were respected.31 The procedure followed by the Church in these cases is as follows: “In the conversion of heretics, from whatever place or sect they originate, it is necessary to inquire about the validity of the baptism received in heresy. After examining each case, if it is ascertained that baptism has not been conferred or that it was conferred invalidly, they have to be baptized in an absolute manner. But if after making the investigation in accordance with the circumstances of time and place, nothing is discovered in favour of, or against, the validity of the baptism, then conditional baptism is administered privately. Finally, if it is confirmed that the baptism was valid, only the profession of faith is made.”32

Who can be baptized My children, there are many men in the world, and there is not one who is not called by the Master. He calls all men of all races, languages and latitudes with a general calling from the very moment they are born. He calls them with the affirmation that he has come on earth to save all: Omnes homines vult salvos fieri (1 Tim 2:4). And

De Sacramentis, can. 4 De Sacr. Bapt., Dz 860 (1617); Benedict XIV, Brief Singulari Nobis, 9 February 1749 (Dz 2566ff); Pius IX, Instruction of the Holy Office, 24 January 1887 (Dz 3126). 32 Leo XIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 20 November 1878, Dz 1848 (3128); cf St Leo the Great, Letter Frequenter Quidem, 24 October 458 (Dz 319); St Gregory III, Letter Magna Nos Habuit, year 732 (Dz 582); C.I.C., can. 869.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 207

he calls them with another marvellous affirmation addressed to all men without any exception: You, therefore, must be perfect, as your heavenly Father is perfect (Matt 5:48). He calls them to Christian life, to a life of sanctity, to a life of the elect. This life in Christ that is born with the first baptismal graces is meant for all souls. Our Lord urged his disciples to go and teach all nations, baptizing them ... For all men of all times he became man, lived, suffered and died on the cross, rose from the dead and ascended into heaven. Down through the centuries the Catholic Church has evangelized peoples, applying to them the fruits of redemption by means of baptism administered in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. Every living person, adult or child, who has not previously been validly baptized, is a subject fit to receive baptism and, with it, grace.33 Man should freely respond to God’s saving will. Thus the Church wishes adults to freely and consciously accept the union with God granted through baptism. The Church teaches that in the case of adults “three dispositions are required to receive baptism appropriately: faith, penance, and the intention to receive the sacrament. Faith is necessary because adults ought to be instructed, according to their intellectual capacity, in the mysteries of the Christian religion, and they should firmly believe in them. Penance is also necessary, since they have to be sorry for their sins and make an act of contrition or attrition. Thirdly, they must have the intention or desire to receive this sacrament because, if this intention were missing, the baptismal character would not be imprinted on the soul of the adult.”34 The desire to be baptized is an essential prerequisite for the validity of adult baptism. Without this desire, adults would be “neither licitly

33 Cf C.I.C., can. 864. 34 Pius IX, Instruction of the Holy Office, 1 August 1860 (Dz 2836-2837); cf Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 3.

208 On Christian life

nor validly baptized,”35 because God wants men freely to cooperate in his plan of salvation.36 The Church also requires a profession of the Catholic faith’s Creed from those receiving this sacrament. The sacraments are actions requiring faith: only the person who believes can fruitfully receive them. Faith is essential to baptism, not because belief causes it to be effective, but so that the subject may be capable of receiving grace, and so the sacrament may not remain unfruitful. In case of urgent need, it suffices to confess the fundamental truths of Christianity, without which there is no salvation: the existence of one God who rewards us,37 and faith in the mysteries of the Blessed Trinity and Incarnation.38 That is what Philip the deacon asked of Queen Candace’s servant: “See, here is water! What is to prevent my being baptized?” And Philip said, “if you believe with all your heart, you may.” And he replied, “I believe that Jesus Christ is the Son of God.”39 Another precondition for fruitful adult baptism is an express rejection of all attachment to sin, with the resolution of not sinning again. This disposition is necessary, since with baptism one receives the Holy Spirit and the ability to live as a Christian. It also responds to the need of man who, moved by faith, wants to shun in future anything that would obstruct God’s action in his soul. Repent, St Peter taught the first converts from Judaism, and be baptized every one of you in the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of your sins; and

35 Ibid. 36 Therefore baptism administered during sleep, without the consent of the subject, would not be valid; nor would baptism administered to a demented person who did not want to receive baptism before losing the use of reason. Nevertheless, a person who agrees to be baptized through fear would receive the sacrament validly, since his intention would be right, though as long as he lacked the necessary faith and sorrow for sins, he would not receive the fruits of the sacrament. In the case of children before the use of reason, the Catholic teaching is that they are baptized in the faith of the Church.. 37 Cf Heb 11:6. 38 Cf Clement XI, Reply of the Holy Office, 25 January 1703, Dz 1349a (2380). 39 Acts 8:36-37.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 209

you shall receive the gift of the Holy Spirit.40 One has to question adults about their dispositions for receiving baptism even if they are in danger of death, briefly explaining these fundamental truths and moving them to contrition if necessary. If this is impossible, and if it is not known whether the person is “sufficiently instructed in the mysteries of faith and believes in them as he ought, or if there is doubt about whether the person is sincerely repentant for his past life, when there is imminent danger of death the sacrament ought to be administered in an absolute manner, unconditionally. On the other hand, when the doubt refers to the person’s real intention to receive baptism, and if after carrying out a diligent examination of his intention the doubt continues, baptism ought to be conferred conditionally.”41 Outside the danger of death, if an adult receives baptism without sufficient faith, or without wanting to distance himself from sin, the sacrament, though validly received, would remain “dead”; that is, the baptismal character would be imprinted on the soul, but grace would not be conferred and, as a consequence, sins would not be forgiven. To “revive” the sacrament, the obstacle impeding the action of grace – whether it be attachment to sin or a lack of faith – would have to be overcome. Then original sin and all personal sins committed prior to the reception of baptism would be pardoned. But sins committed while the sacrament was ‘dead’ must be confessed in the sacrament of penance.

Necessity of Baptism for salvation Unless one is born of water and the Spirit, he cannot enter the kingdom of God,42 our Lord taught Nicodemus. And he said to the

40 Acts 2:38. 41 Pius IX, Instruction of the Holy Office, 1 August 1860 (Dz 2838); cf Leo XIII, Reply of the Holy Office, 30 March 1898, Dz 1966a (3335). 42 John 3:5.

210 On Christian life

apostles when sending them out to preach and baptize: He who believes and is baptized will be saved.43 Baptism is absolutely necessary for salvation, because it is the only way God has given us to wipe out original sin and form part of Christ’s Church. This teaching, so clearly affirmed by our Lord, has been rejected by some heretics down through history: by Pelagius and his followers, because they did not admit the existence of original sin or its transmission to all men; by the Cathari and the Albigensians, because (as a consequence of their dualist doctrine) they attributed the institution of baptism to an evil God; by some Protestants, because they affirmed that faith was the only principle of justification; and, more recently, by the modernists, because they reduced Christ’s doctrine and all his institutions and norms to something exclusively human. Confronted with these diverse errors, the Church has always taught the absolute necessity of baptism and the appropriateness of receiving it as soon as possible: “if anyone were to say that baptism is optional, that is to say, not necessary for salvation, let him be anathema.”44 Nevertheless, this necessity does not limit God’s universal salvific will;45 he gives all men, through Christ’s merits, sufficient grace for their justification.46 The need for this sacrament ought not to be understood in the sense that only those who receive baptism of water are saved.

Nuper ad Nos, 16 March 1743, Dz 1470 (2536); Pius XII, Letter from the Holy Office to the Archbishop of Boston, 8 August 1949 (Dz 3869); Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (I) 211

The Church has always firmly believed that this can be made up for by martyrdom – baptism of blood – when a person suffers death for Christ before having received the sacrament, or by the desire to receive it – baptism of desire – united with an act of perfect contrition. “God in his infinite mercy,” the Church authoritatively tells us, “has established that the effects of these aids for salvation, which are ordained to man’s ultimate end by divine institution only, and not by intrinsic necessity, should in certain circumstances be obtained with the sole desire of receiving them. We see this enunciated clearly in the words of the Council of Trent in regard to the sacraments of regeneration and penance ... It is not necessary that this desire be explicit, as in the case of catechumens. God also accepts the implicit desire of those invincibly ignorant. This desire is called implicit because it is found present in the good dispositions of soul by which man wishes to conform his will to God’s will47.”48 The sacrament of baptism, absolutely necessary for salvation, initiates man into Christian life. It is a true birth to supernatural life which opens the gate to the other means ordained by divine Providence, so that all souls may grow in love for God, and better imitate him who is the Way, Truth and Life of our life.49 For in Christ Jesus you are all sons of God, through faith. For as many of you as were baptized into Christ have put on Christ. There is neither slave nor free, there is neither male nor female; for you are all one in Christ Jesus.50

47 Pius XII, Letter from the Holy Office to the Archbishop of Boston, 8 August 1949 (Dz 3869); Innocent II, Letter Apostolicam Sedem, year 1140, Dz 388 (741); Council of Trent, Decr. De Iustificatione, ch. 4, Dz 796 (1524); C.I.C., can. 865. 48 Theologians understand “implicit desire” to be the desire someone would have if he were to hear of Christ’s revelation and the need for baptism to be saved. This presupposes the person wants to live in accord with God’s will and follows in his own life the dictates of natural law (cf Rom 2:12-16).. 49 Cf John 14:6. 50 Gal 3:26-28.

## BAPTISM, GATEWAY TO CHRISTIAN LIFE (II) {#cuaderno.2.17}

Baptism was instituted by Christ so that all men could receive the merits of his passion and death and be reconciled with God. It is the sacrament that initiates Christian life in the soul, giving grace, supernatural virtues and gifts. Thus man is enabled to know, love and fulfil God’s will. “When we are baptized, we are enlightened; being enlightened, we become adopted children; becoming adopted children, we are made perfect; and becoming perfect, we are made divine. This ceremony is often called ‘free gift’, ‘enlightenment’, ‘perfection’, and ‘cleansing’ – ‘cleansing’, because through it we are completely purified of our sins; ‘free gift’, because by it the punishments due to our sins are remitted; ‘enlightenment’, since by it we behold the wonderful holy light of salvation, that is, it enables us to see God clearly; finally we call it ‘perfection’ as needing nothing further, for what more does he need who possesses the knowledge of God?”1

Effects of Baptism: Character In baptism, our Father God has taken possession of our lives; he

1 Clement of Alexandria, Paedag., 1, 6.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 213

has incorporated us into the life of Christ and has sent to us the Holy Spirit.2 Baptism is a personal encounter with Jesus Christ: he pardons us original sin and any personal sins, brings us through grace into divine intimacy and makes us members of his Mystical Body, the Church. It is an unmerited gift that imprints on the baptized person, through the initial grace, Christ’s image. This first sacrament confers a character, that is, a spiritual mark or seal indelibly impressed on the soul.3 Though the baptized person may later be separated from God through sin, the mark remains in the soul, even without sanctifying grace. He is still marked as having been redeemed by Christ, made a Christian, a member of the Church, and thus can have recourse to the Church to be reconciled with God. Since it confers this character, baptism cannot be repeated.4 A person once rescued from the “condemned mass” of those still stained by original sin cannot be rescued again; a person incorporated into the Church cannot enter the Church anew. Through baptism and the Christian character it confers on the soul, a person becomes a member of the Christian faithful and is called to holiness. A baptized person also receives the spiritual means that are needed to develop Christ’s life in his or her personal circumstances. This indelible spiritual mark is the ultimate foundation for the equality

2 Christ is passing by, 128. 3 Cf Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 411 (781); Urban VIII, Decree of the Holy Office, 23 July 1639 (Dz 1998): Benedict XIV, Brief Singular Nobis, 9 February 1749 (Dz 2566); C.I.C., can. 845. 4 Cf Pope Siricius, Letter Directa ad Decessorem, 10 February 385, Dz 88 (183); St Leo I, Letter Regressus ad Nos, 21 March 458 (Dz 316); Letter Frequenter Quidem, 24 October 458 (Dz 319ff); St Gregory II, Letter Desiderabilem Mihi, 22 November 726, Dz 296 (580), Nicholas I, Reply to the Bulgarians, 13 November 866, Dz 334 (644); Fourth Lateran Council, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (855); Clement VI, Letter Super Quibusdam, 29 September 1351, Dz 574 (1081); Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis, can. 11, Dz 867 (1624); Decr. De Poenitentia, ch. 1, Dz 894 (1670); Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 4.

214 On Christian life

of all Christians in the Mystical Body of Christ: There are no secondclass Christians, expected only to practise a watered-down version of the Gospel, our Father writes. We have all received the same baptism; and although there is a great variety of charisms and human situations, there is only one Spirit who distributes God’s gifts, only one faith, only one hope, only one love (cf 1 Cor 12:4-6 and 13:1- 13).5 All the faithful incorporated into Christ by baptism are called to seek the fullness of Christian life.6 Once baptized, no one can disregard the call to sanctity. “The followers of Christ, called by God not in virtue of their works but by his design and grace, and justified in the Lord Jesus, have been made sons of God in the baptism of faith and made partakers of the divine nature, and so are truly sanctified. They must therefore hold on to and perfect in their lives that sanctification which they have received from God.”7 To attain the goal to which God calls us, the baptismal character gives us a share in the priestly, prophetic and royal office of Christ. This confers the right and the duty to offer spiritual sacrifices to God and to take part in the eucharistic sacrifice. It also confers the right and the duty to show others, through our apostolic activity, the path to salvation and to contribute to the sanctification of temporal activities. An apostle is a Christian who feels himself grafted onto Christ, identified with Christ through Baptism; enabled to fight for Christ through Confirmation; called to serve God with his activity in the world, through his participation in the royal, prophetic and priestly functions of Christ which enable him to guide men to God, to teach them the truth of the Gospel, and to co-redeem them with his prayer and his expiation.8

5 Christ is passing by, 134. 6 From our Father, Letter 11 March 1940, 25. 7 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 40. 8 From our Father, Letter 9 January 1932, 86.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 215

Effects of Baptism: Grace The grace of baptism is called ‘first’ grace, since it clothes the soul for the first time. Before baptism man is separated from God’s life owing to original sin. For that reason baptism is said to be a ‘sacrament of the dead’ – as is the sacrament of penance. The other sacraments, in contrast, are called ‘sacraments of the living’, because in order to receive them supernatural life must be present in the soul. Along with habitual or sanctifying grace, baptism, like the other sacraments, confers a specific sacramental grace, needed to live worthily in our new life. All the sacraments make us similar to Christ, each in its specific way. Through sacramental grace we receive the supernatural help needed to live as God’s children in every situation, to answer all the requirements of Christian life. By baptism we are identified with Christ, who died and rose from the dead: Do you not know that all of us who have been baptized into Christ Jesus were baptized into his death? We were buried therefore with him by baptism into death, so that as Christ was raised from the dead by the glory of the Father, we too might walk in newness of life.9 And the Second Vatican Council states: “By the sacrament of baptism, whenever it is properly conferred in the way the Lord determined and received with the proper dispositions of soul, man becomes truly incorporated into the crucified and glorified Christ and is reborn to a sharing of the divine life, as the Apostle says: For you were buried together with him in baptism, and in him also rose again through faith in the working of God who raised him from the dead10.”11 The practical consequences of the spiritual identification with Christ

9 Rom 6:3-4. 10 Col 2:12. 11 Second Vatican Council, Decr. Unitatis Redintegratio, 22; cf Ordo Baptismi Parvulorum, De Init. Christ., 6.

216 On Christian life

wrought by baptism are of utmost importance. We Christians – you and I, my daughters and sons – must imitate Christ, we must be alter Christus.12 God asks all of us to have in our hearts the same sentiments Christ had in his (cf Phil 2:5) ... All of us, priests and lay people, are given both the chance and the duty to be contemplative souls in all circumstances – also in the midst of the world – and to identify ourselves with Christ (cf Gal 4:19). Thus we will follow our Lord, we will put on the person of Christ (cf Gal 3:27) and we will live his life, being able to say with the Apostle: vivo autem, iam non ego: vivit vero in me Christus (Gal 2:20). And then each one of us, as a good Christian, is no longer just alter Christus, but ipse Christus.13 This double incorporation into Christ and his Church is the ultimate source of the great transformation that should take place in the life of every Christian. “Incorporated into the Church by baptism, the faithful are appointed by their baptismal character to Christian religious worship; re-born as sons of God, they must profess before men the faith they have received from God through the Church.”14

New Life in Christ The ceremony of baptism itself points out that the configuration with Christ this sacrament brings about is a spiritual regeneration, as our Lord taught Nicodemus:15 a true supernatural birth, making of the baptized person a new creature.16 This regeneration in the Spirit is the foundation of divine filiation: “By baptism men are grafted into the paschal

12 From our Father, Letter 16 July 1933, 16. 13 From our Father, Letter 2 February 1945, 6. 14 Second Vatican Council, Dogm. Const. Lumen Gentium, 11. 15 Cf John 3:5. 16 Cf Innocent I, Letter Inter Ceteras, 27 January 417 (Dz 219); Indiculus, year 431, Dz 130 (239); Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 695 (1311); Council of Trent, Decr. De Poenitentia, ch. 2, Dz 895 (1672).

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 217

mystery of Christ; they die with him, are buried with him and rise with him. They receive the spirit of adoption as sons in which we cry, Abba, Father,17 and thus become true adorers such as the Father seeks.”18 God loves us to the point of welcoming us into his intimate life, making us brothers and sisters of Christ, members of his Mystical Body and adopted children who are destined to enjoy fully in heaven the union that divinizes us and fills us with happiness. Divine adoption, which establishes a new relationship between engraced man and God, brings about the sinner’s justification. Baptism erases all sin from the soul and infuses grace, so that man “is changed from unjust to just, from enemy to friend, becoming heir to the hope of eternal life.”19 The theological reason for these wonderful effects is clear: “On being incorporated to the Passion and Death of Christ by baptism ... all the redemptive merits of Christ’s Passion are applied to the baptized person as if that person had suffered and died.”20 And since Christ’s death has a universal effect, reaching every sin and any punishment, both original sin and all personal sins are forgiven in baptism.21 All temporal and eternal punishment due to sins is also remitted. “For this reason,” the Church points out, “no penance for past sins should be imposed on those

218 On Christian life

who are being baptized; if they die before committing any sin, they immediately go to heaven and enjoy the vision of God.”22 Configuration with the risen Christ, signified by rising out of the baptismal waters, means that a baptized person has received divine grace, the infused virtues and gifts of the Holy Spirit, which transform and sanctify him.23 The soul is deified: its new life contrasts with its former life and with that so often found in its surroundings! Faith tells us that a soul in the state of grace is truly a divinized soul: God has granted to us his precious and very great promises, that through these you may ... become partakers of the divine nature (2 Pet 1:4). This theological notion of man is almost as far removed from a purely human and natural concept of man, as God himself is removed from humanity.24 Through baptism the soul becomes a temple of the Blessed Trinity.25 “By baptism,” we read in an ancient Christian document, “we have been renewed, created again from the beginning. God truly lives in us, in the dwelling place of our heart. In what way? With his word, the object of our faith; with the call of his promises; with the wisdom of his counsels; with the commandments of his doctrine. He himself prophesies in us, opening our lips to pray; he dwells in person within us, and with the grace of repentance he leads us, once enslaved by death, into an incorruptible temple.”26 Despite the great gifts that clothe the baptized person, the consequences of original sin still remain in our human nature. Although they come from original sin, these inclinations are not in themselves sin.

22 Council of Florence, Bull Exultate Deo, Dz 696 (1316); cf Benedict XII, Const. Benedictus Deus, 29 January 1336, Dz 530 (1000), Council of Trent, Decr.
