Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 219

Christians are still subject to error, concupiscence and death. “Although concupiscence is left to be wrestled with, it cannot harm those who do not consent, but manfully resist by the grace of Jesus Christ.”27 Since God in his mercy has so desired it, baptism moreover gives us a right to the actual graces needed to overcome temptations and persevere in doing good, although our free human cooperation is also needed.28 Baptism sows in man the seed of a glorious renewal, whence he will reach the perfect subjection of body to soul and of the whole man to God, an order that prevailed prior to original sin. Though nobody can escape from the law of death, through the influence of the soul in grace, this divinization also affects the body, as a kind of anticipation of its glorious resurrection.29 At the end of the world the effects of baptism will reach their completion, with the body’s redemption and resurrection to glory: “for this perishable nature must put on the imperishable, and this mortal nature must put on immortality.30 Christian faith teaches that bodily death, from which man would have been immune had he not sinned,31 will be overcome when that wholeness which he lost through his own fault will be given once again to him by the almighty and merciful Saviour.”32 Final perseverance, however, is not a gift that is conferred with baptismal grace: further help from God, humbly and trustingly sought, is needed.33 We must also cooperate by good works and obedience to the commandments of God and the Church.34 Until that moment arrives, each “Christian is certainly bound both by need and by duty to

struggle with evil through many afflictions and to suffer death; but as one who has been made a partner in the paschal mystery, and as one who has been configured to the death of Christ, he will go forward, strengthened by hope, to the resurrection35.”36

Baptism of Infants It is only through baptism that man can be united to Christ, to his death and glorious resurrection, and receive Christian life in the soul. Since it is a sacrament that is absolutely necessary for salvation, the Church from the very beginning baptized newly-born infants.37 “The Church has learned from the Apostles the practice of baptizing children also. The Apostles, discerning as they did the divine mysteries, knew that all men are born stained with original sin, which can only be cleansed by means of water and the Holy Spirit.”38 Nobody comes into the world free of original sin: only the blessed Virgin, through a special privilege from almighty God, was preserved free from all sin from the moment of her conception.39 The rest of mankind are born “children of wrath:40 behold, I was brought forth in iniquity, and in sin did my mother conceive me.41 It is part of the rule of faith that even little children, incapable yet

Comm., 5, 9. 39 Cf Pius IX, Bull Ineffabilis Deus, 8 December 1854, Dz 1641 (2803). 40 Cf Eph 2:3. 41 Ps 50:7.

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 221

of committing any sin themselves, are truly baptized in the remission of sins, so that by regeneration they may be cleansed of what they received through generation.”42 A person without the use of reason cannot distinguish between good and evil; such a person cannot know God’s will, and therefore cannot obey the divine law; neither can he be sorry for sin or desire grace. “Nevertheless, the state of grace at the moment of death is absolutely necessary for salvation.”43 Only baptism can open the gates of heaven to those who have not yet reached the use of reason. That is why the Church, with maternal solicitude, exhorts us “not to delay administering baptism to children for forty or eighty days, or any other length of time, as some people do. It should be conferred as soon as it conveniently can be, since infants cannot be helped by any remedy other than baptism, by which they are freed from the devil and adopted as children of God.”44 The Church also earnestly recommends “parish priests and preachers to instruct the faithful frequently with regard to this grave obligation. Christian parents are specially obliged to have their children baptized as soon as possible;”45 indifference or excessive delay in the administration of baptism to an infant could be a grave sin.46 When children are baptized, the faith needed to receive the sacrament fruitfully is supplied by the Church. As a mother, she wishes to see all her children saved, and professes in their name the fundamental truths of the faith, when they are not able to do so for themselves.47

42 Sixteenth Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 2, Dz 102 (223); cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Peccato Originali, can. 4, Dz 791 (1514). 43 Pius XII, Discourse to Midwives, 29 October 1951. 44 Council of Florence, Bull Cantate Domino, Dz 712 (1349). 45 C.I.C., can. 867. 46 Cf Catechism of St Pius V, 2, 2, 34; Catechism of St Pius X, 4, 2, 564. 47 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis, can. 13, Dz 869 (1626). This is the reason for the custom of having godparents, who give their consent and witness to the faith in the child’s name, and who are responsible, along with the parents, for the child being brought up a Catholic.

222 On Christian life

The practice of baptizing newborn children underlines a fundamental right God gives to all men: that of receiving from the Church the means to salvation. Since a child cannot of itself claim that right, it must be exercised in the child’s name by its parents or by those taking their place. This does not imply any imposition on the child’s freedom. If the child were denied baptism, a will foreign to its own would also be followed; and there certainly was no imposition on the child’s freedom when it was given life without its consent, nor when educated, fed or healed without taking its will into account. The validity and effects of the baptism of children do not depend on the subsequent use of freedom, when a child reaches the age of reason;48 nor do they necessarily depend on the actual use of reason if the baptized person is permanently impaired. The grace received in baptism becomes operative, if the person in question does not act to impede it, when the use of freedom becomes possible to him.

The state of unbaptized children who die The Church has not defined exactly what the eternal state of unbaptized children is who die before attaining the use of reason. However Catholic teaching is that such children, since they are incapable of making the perfect act of contrition that would open the gates of heaven to them, do not enjoy the beatific vision: “The souls of those who die in mortal sin or with only the stain of original sin, descend immediately to hell to be punished with different types of punishment.”49 It is certain,

48 Cf Council of Trent, Decr. De Sacramentis, can. 13-14, Dz 869-870 (1626-1627). 49 Second Council of Lyon, Profession of Faith of Michael Paleologus, Dz 464 (858); Cf Pope Siricius, Letter Directa ad Decessorem, 10 February 385 (Dz 184); Council of Carthage, year 418, can. 2, Dz 102 (223); Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780); Council of Florence, Bull Laetentur Coeli, Dz 693 (1306).

Baptism, Gateway to Christian Life (II) 223

therefore, that they do not enjoy the vision of God.50 However, since they have committed no personal sins, their punishment is not the same as that of the damned, who voluntarily turned away from God. “Sin is of two types: original and actual. Original sin is that which is contracted without consent; actual is that which is committed with consent ... The punishment of original sin is the privation of the vision of God; that of actual sin is the eternal torment of hell.”51 According to the opinion commonly held by theologians and firmly based on the teaching of the Church’s magisterium, the souls of children who die without baptism go to a special place called ‘limbo’. This is not the destination of the damned, but a place where they suffer no sense pain, though they will not enjoy the vision of God. Theirs is a natural happiness in keeping with God’s justice and mercy.52 These souls which only have original sin and are not forgiven by baptism will suffer only what is called the pain of loss, the privation of the supernatural vision of God. Tell me, my daughters and sons, our Father writes, if this is not sufficient reason to insist, as the Church 53 does, on the baptism of newborn children? So firmly does the Church believe in the need for baptism to attain the beatific vision that, while always defending the parent’s right under natural law to bring up their children and forbidding the baptism of children whose parents are opposed, she nevertheless praises those who baptize such children in danger of death or in other specific

50 Various theories trying to justify the salvation of children who die without baptism have been qualified by the Holy See as “lacking solid foundation” (cf Monitum of the Holy Office, 18 February 1950). 51 Innocent III, Letter Maiores Ecclesiae Causas, year 1201, Dz 410 (780). 52 Cf Pius VI, Bull Auctorem Fidei, 28 August 1794, prop. 26, Dz 1526 (2626). Translator’s Note: The Catechism of the Catholic Church makes no statement on this point (cf no. 1261). Consequently this does not form part of official doctrine; it is only a theological opinion, which, in times past, was quite widely held. 53 From our Father, Letter 19 March 1967, 98.

224 On Christian life

circumstances, because this opens for them the gates to eternal life.54 Christian life, which God gratuitously wants to grant us in its fullness, begins at the moment of baptism. The invocation of God, One and Three, Father, Son and Holy Spirit, gives the water a supernatural power, enabling it to remove all sins and to sow in the soul the seeds of a new existence – that of God’s children. By baptism God has put his seal upon us and given us his Spirit in our hearts as a guarantee55 of the goods promised us: the beatific vision, the eternal happiness of heaven. Counting on the strength given us in baptism, we can aspire to our supernatural end. Each Christian, aided by grace, must finish what God has begun. Make every effort to supplement your faith with virtue, and virtue with knowledge, and knowledge with self-control, and selfcontrol with steadfastness, and steadfastness with godliness, and godliness with brotherly affection, and brotherly affection with love. For if these things are yours and abound, they keep you from being ineffective or unfruitful in the knowledge of our Lord Jesus Christ.56
